SYNOPTICS
SVKMs NMIMS
MUKESH PATEL SCH.OF TECH.MGMT ENGG-NM-M
Programme: B. Tech (AI)/B. Tech (AI & DS)/B. Tech (AI & ML)/B. Tech (CSE - CS)/B. Tech

Year: I / II / III/Semester II/Semester IV/Semester VI(Exam Year: 2025-2026)
Subject: Principles of Economics and Management
Date: 06 May 2026   Time: 10:00 am to 01:00 pm (03:00 Hrs.)
Max. Marks: 100
FINAL EXAMINATION (Acad. Year:2025-2026)
Instructions:
1. This question paper contains 7 pages.
2. Question No. 1 is compulsory.
3. Out of the remaining questions, attempt any 4 questions.
4. In all 5 questions to be attempted.
5. All questions carry equal marks.
6. Answer to each new question to be started on a fresh page.
7. Figures in brackets on the right-hand side indicate full marks.
8. Assume suitable data if necessary.
Answers for:
Q1. Brief Notes
(a) PPC (Production Possibility Curve)
• Represents maximum combinations of two goods using given resources and technology.
• Downward sloping due to scarcity of resources.
• Concave to origin due to increasing opportunity cost.
•   Points on curve show efficient use, inside show inefficiency, outside are unattainable.
(b) Average Cost and Marginal Cost
• AC = TC / Q, MC = ΔTC / ΔQ
• MC intersects AC at its minimum point.
• When MC < AC, AC falls; when MC > AC, AC rises.
• Both curves are typically U-shaped due to law of returns.
********** 1 **********
--- page 2 ---
(c) LPG (Liberalization, Privatization, Globalization)
• Introduced in 1991 to reform Indian economy.
•   Liberalization: removal of government restrictions.
•   Privatization: increased role of private sector.
•   Globalization: integration with global markets.
•   Objective: improve efficiency, growth, and competitiveness.
(d) Levels of Management
• Top Level: Policy making and strategic decisions.
• Middle Level: Implementation of plans and coordination.
• Lower Level: Supervision of day-to-day operations.
All the below answers headings should be explained in detail by the student considering its 10 mark
Q2
(a) Isoquant and Producer Equilibrium
• Isoquant shows combinations of inputs yielding same output.
• Downward sloping and convex due to diminishing MRTS.
• Producer equilibrium occurs where isoquant is tangent to isocost line.
•   Condition: MRTS = ratio of input prices.
• At this M point in diagram, producer minimizes cost for given output.
(b) Cost Types & MC-AC Relationship
Fixed & Variable Cost
Explicit & Implicit Cost
Economic & Accounting Cost
MC, AC, TC
All the above should be explained in detail by the student.
• Fixed cost remains constant irrespective of output.
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
• Variable cost changes with output.
•   Total cost = Fixed cost + Variable cost.
• MC and AC are U-shaped.
• MC intersects AC at its minimum point.
•   Relationship indicates cost efficiency and production behavior.
Q3
(a) Perfect vs Imperfect Markets
• Perfect Market:
Large number of firms, homogeneous product, price taker, free entry and exit.
• Imperfect Market:
Fewer firms, product differentiation, price maker, barriers to entry.
Types of Imperfect Markets:
• Monopoly (single seller)
• Monopolistic competition (many sellers with differentiated products)
• Oligopoly (few dominant firms)
• Duopoly (two sellers)
All markets should be explained with example
(b) Need for LPG Reforms in India
• Economic crisis in 1991: low foreign exchange reserves, high fiscal deficit.
•   Inefficiency of public sector and license raj.
• Need to increase productivity and global competitiveness.
Reforms aimed at:
• Encouraging private sector
•   Attracting foreign investment
• Boosting exports
• Improving efficiency and growth
Q4
(a) Reasons for Failure of Planning
1. Inaccurate forecasting
2. Lack of flexibility
3. Poor communication
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
4. External environmental uncertainties
5. Lack of coordination and commitment
Example: Sudden economic policy changes disrupting business plans.
(b) Taylor vs Fayol vs Mayo
Basis   F.W. Taylor   Henri Fayol   Elton Mayo
Approach   Scientific Management   Administrative Theory   Human Relations Approach
Focus   Shop-floor efficiency   Overall management process Human behavior at workplace

motivation
Q5
(a) Line vs Staff & Conflict Resolution
• Line authority: Direct responsibility for achieving objectives.
•   Staff authority: Advisory role providing expertise.
Conflict arises due to:
•   Differences in authority and responsibility
• Lack of understanding of each other's roles
Solutions:
•   Clearly define roles
• Improve communication
• Encourage collaboration
• Conduct joint decision-making
(b) Improving Team Effectiveness
•   Establish clear goals and responsibilities
• Improve communication channels
• Conduct regular meetings
• Promote team spirit over departmental interests
• Ensure leadership coordination
•   Introduce accountability mechanisms
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
Q6
(a)
Consumer equilibrium is the state where a rational consumer maximizes total utility (satisfaction) from
purchasing goods, given their limited income and prevailing market prices. At this point, the last unit of
money spent provides equal marginal utility across all products, or the marginal utility of a good equals
its price, resulting in no incentive to change consumption
A movement along the demand curve is caused solely by a change in a good's price, resulting in a
contraction or expansion of quantity demanded. Conversely, a shift in the entire demand curve,
caused by non-price factors like income or preferences, represents a change in demand at every
price level.
Movement Along the Demand Curve (Change in Quantity Demanded) – Diagram a
• Cause: A change in the price of the product itself.
•   Effect: A move from one point to another on the same curve.
• Upward Movement (Contraction): Price increases, resulting in a decrease in quantity
demanded.
• Downward Movement (Expansion): Price decreases, resulting in an increase in quantity
demanded. [1,
Shift in the Demand Curve (Change in Demand) - Diagram b
• Cause: Changes in factors other than the product's price, such as income, tastes, population, or
prices of related goods.
•   Effect: The entire curve moves to the left or right.
• Rightward Shift (Increase in Demand): Caused by factors like increased income, higher
popularity, or higher prices of substitutes.
• Leftward Shift (Decrease in Demand): Caused by factors like reduced income, lower
popularity, or lower prices of substitutes.
Key Determinants Causing Shifts
• Income: Higher income increases demand for normal goods, shifting the curve right.
• Prices of Related Goods: If the price of a substitute (e.g., tea) increases, demand for the primary
good (e.g., coffee) increases. If the price of a complement (e.g., petrol) increases, demand for the
primary good (e.g., cars) decreases.
• Tastes/Preferences: Popularity increases boost demand.
• Expectations: Expecting higher future prices can increase current demand.
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
(b) Types of Elasticity of Demand
1. Price Elasticity: responsiveness to price change.
= % change in quantity demanded / % change in price
2. Income Elasticity: responsiveness to income change.
= % change in quantity demanded / % change in income
3. Cross Elasticity: responsiveness to price of related goods.
= % change in quantity demanded of commodity A / % change in price of commodity B
Q7
(a) Herzberg’s theory
Herzberg classified factors into:
• Hygiene factors: salary, working conditions, policies, supervision, and job security.
• Motivators: achievement, recognition, responsibility, advancement, meaningful work.
Case analysis
The Company has provided hygiene factors such as good salary and working conditions. These may
reduce dissatisfaction, but they do not create strong motivation.
Employees are leaving because motivator factors are weak:
• Work is Monotonous,
• Recognition is Low,
• Growth Opportunities are Limited,
•   Responsibility may be Low.
Practical steps
1. Enrich jobs by making work more meaningful.
2. Recognise good performance.
3. Give employees more responsibility.
4. Create career growth and promotion paths.
5. Encourage achievement-oriented work design.
6. Involve employees in problem-solving.
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
Important distinction
Hygiene factors prevent dissatisfaction; motivators create satisfaction and commitment.
Conclusion
The organisation must go beyond salary and facilities. To improve motivation, it should focus on
recognition, responsibility, achievement, and career development.
(b) Inventory & Coordination Improvement
Problems in the case
• Excess inventory increases carrying cost.
• Poor coordination leads to shortages and emergency buying.
• Working capital gets blocked.
•   Functional departments are operating in isolation.
Inventory control meaning
Inventory control means maintaining optimum stock levels so that materials are available when needed
without excessive investment.
Inventory control techniques
1. Set minimum, maximum, and reorder levels.
2. Use EOQ-type thinking for economic ordering.
3. Classify inventory by importance.
4. Monitor fast-moving and slow-moving items.
5. Maintain proper stock records.
6. Avoid overstocking and understocking.
Need for functional coordination
• Marketing should share demand forecasts and promotion schedules.
• Finance should monitor cost, budget, and working capital.
• Operations/HR should ensure manpower and process readiness for expected demand.
Benefits of coordination
•   Better Planning,
• Lower Carrying Cost,
• Fewer Stockouts,
• Smoother Production,
• Improved Profitability,
• Stronger Customer Service.
Conclusion
Inventory control alone is not enough. Effective coordination among marketing, finance, and
operations is essential for cost control and operational efficiency.
********** 7 **********
````

• Income: Higher income increases demand for normal goods, shifting the curve right.
• Prices of Related Goods: If the price of a substitute (e.g., tea) increases, demand for the primary
good (e.g., coffee) increases. If the price of a complement (e.g., petrol) increases, demand for the
primary good (e.g., cars) decreases.
• Tastes/Preferences: Popularity increases boost demand.
• Expectations: Expecting higher future prices can increase current demand.
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
(b) Types of Elasticity of Demand
1. Price Elasticity: responsiveness to price change.
= % change in quantity demanded / % change in price
2. Income Elasticity: responsiveness to income change.
= % change in quantity demanded / % change in income
3. Cross Elasticity: responsiveness to price of related goods.
= % change in quantity demanded of commodity A / % change in price of commodity B
Q7
(a) Herzberg’s theory
Herzberg classified factors into:
• Hygiene factors: salary, working conditions, policies, supervision, and job security.
• Motivators: achievement, recognition, responsibility, advancement, meaningful work.
Case analysis
The Company has provided hygiene factors such as good salary and working conditions. These may
reduce dissatisfaction, but they do not create strong motivation.
Employees are leaving because motivator factors are weak:
• Work is Monotonous,
• Recognition is Low,
• Growth Opportunities are Limited,
•   Responsibility may be Low.
Practical steps
1. Enrich jobs by making work more meaningful.
2. Recognise good performance.
3. Give employees more responsibility.
4. Create career growth and promotion paths.
5. Encourage achievement-oriented work design.
6. Involve employees in problem-solving.
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
Important distinction
Hygiene factors prevent dissatisfaction; motivators create satisfaction and commitment.
Conclusion
The organisation must go beyond salary and facilities. To improve motivation, it should focus on
recognition, responsibility, achievement, and career development.
(b) Inventory & Coordination Improvement
Problems in the case
• Excess inventory increases carrying cost.
• Poor coordination leads to shortages and emergency buying.
• Working capital gets blocked.
•   Functional departments are operating in isolation.
Inventory control meaning
Inventory control means maintaining optimum stock levels so that materials are available when needed
without excessive investment.
Inventory control techniques
1. Set minimum, maximum, and reorder levels.
2. Use EOQ-type thinking for economic ordering.
3. Classify inventory by importance.
4. Monitor fast-moving and slow-moving items.
5. Maintain proper stock records.
6. Avoid overstocking and understocking.
Need for functional coordination
• Marketing should share demand forecasts and promotion schedules.
• Finance should monitor cost, budget, and working capital.
• Operations/HR should ensure manpower and process readiness for expected demand.
Benefits of coordination
•   Better Planning,
• Lower Carrying Cost,
• Fewer Stockouts,
• Smoother Production,
• Improved Profitability,
• Stronger Customer Service.
Conclusion
Inventory control alone is not enough. Effective coordination among marketing, finance, and
operations is essential for cost control and operational efficiency.
********** 7 **********
````

Answers for:
Q1. Brief Notes
(a) PPC (Production Possibility Curve)
• Represents maximum combinations of two goods using given resources and technology.
• Downward sloping due to scarcity of resources.
• Concave to origin due to increasing opportunity cost.
•   Points on curve show efficient use, inside show inefficiency, outside are unattainable.
(b) Average Cost and Marginal Cost
• AC = TC / Q, MC = ΔTC / ΔQ
• MC intersects AC at its minimum point.
• When MC < AC, AC falls; when MC > AC, AC rises.
• Both curves are typically U-shaped due to law of returns.
********** 1 **********
--- page 2 ---
(c) LPG (Liberalization, Privatization, Globalization)
• Introduced in 1991 to reform Indian economy.
•   Liberalization: removal of government restrictions.
•   Privatization: increased role of private sector.
•   Globalization: integration with global markets.
•   Objective: improve efficiency, growth, and competitiveness.
(d) Levels of Management
• Top Level: Policy making and strategic decisions.
• Middle Level: Implementation of plans and coordination.
• Lower Level: Supervision of day-to-day operations.
All the below answers headings should be explained in detail by the student considering its 10 mark
Q2
(a) Isoquant and Producer Equilibrium
• Isoquant shows combinations of inputs yielding same output.
• Downward sloping and convex due to diminishing MRTS.
• Producer equilibrium occurs where isoquant is tangent to isocost line.
•   Condition: MRTS = ratio of input prices.
• At this M point in diagram, producer minimizes cost for given output.
(b) Cost Types & MC-AC Relationship
Fixed & Variable Cost
Explicit & Implicit Cost
Economic & Accounting Cost
MC, AC, TC
All the above should be explained in detail by the student.
• Fixed cost remains constant irrespective of output.
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
• Variable cost changes with output.
•   Total cost = Fixed cost + Variable cost.
• MC and AC are U-shaped.
• MC intersects AC at its minimum point.
•   Relationship indicates cost efficiency and production behavior.
Q3
(a) Perfect vs Imperfect Markets
• Perfect Market:
Large number of firms, homogeneous product, price taker, free entry and exit.
• Imperfect Market:
Fewer firms, product differentiation, price maker, barriers to entry.
Types of Imperfect Markets:
• Monopoly (single seller)
• Monopolistic competition (many sellers with differentiated products)
• Oligopoly (few dominant firms)
• Duopoly (two sellers)
All markets should be explained with example
(b) Need for LPG Reforms in India
• Economic crisis in 1991: low foreign exchange reserves, high fiscal deficit.
•   Inefficiency of public sector and license raj.
• Need to increase productivity and global competitiveness.
Reforms aimed at:
• Encouraging private sector
•   Attracting foreign investment
• Boosting exports
• Improving efficiency and growth

1. Inaccurate forecasting
2. Lack of flexibility
3. Poor communication

Q5
(a) Line vs Staff & Conflict Resolution
• Line authority: Direct responsibility for achieving objectives.
•   Staff authority: Advisory role providing expertise.
Conflict arises due to:
•   Differences in authority and responsibility
• Lack of understanding of each other's roles
Solutions:

• Improve communication
• Encourage collaboration
• Conduct joint decision-making
(b) Improving Team Effectiveness
•   Establish clear goals and responsibilities
• Improve communication channels
• Conduct regular meetings
• Promote team spirit over departmental interests
• Ensure leadership coordination
•   Introduce accountability mechanisms
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
Q6
(a)
Consumer equilibrium is the state where a rational consumer maximizes total utility (satisfaction) from
purchasing goods, given their limited income and prevailing market prices. At this point, the last unit of
money spent provides equal marginal utility across all products, or the marginal utility of a good equals
its price, resulting in no incentive to change consumption
A movement along the demand curve is caused solely by a change in a good's price, resulting in a
contraction or expansion of quantity demanded. Conversely, a shift in the entire demand curve,
caused by non-price factors like income or preferences, represents a change in demand at every
price level.
Movement Along the Demand Curve (Change in Quantity Demanded) – Diagram a
• Cause: A change in the price of the product itself.
•   Effect: A move from one point to another on the same curve.
• Upward Movement (Contraction): Price increases, resulting in a decrease in quantity
demanded.
• Downward Movement (Expansion): Price decreases, resulting in an increase in quantity
demanded. [1,
Shift in the Demand Curve (Change in Demand) - Diagram b
• Cause: Changes in factors other than the product's price, such as income, tastes, population, or
prices of related goods.
•   Effect: The entire curve moves to the left or right.
• Rightward Shift (Increase in Demand): Caused by factors like increased income, higher
popularity, or higher prices of substitutes.
• Leftward Shift (Decrease in Demand): Caused by factors like reduced income, lower
popularity, or lower prices of substitutes.
Key Determinants Causing Shifts
• Income: Higher income increases demand for normal goods, shifting the curve right.
• Prices of Related Goods: If the price of a substitute (e.g., tea) increases, demand for the primary
good (e.g., coffee) increases. If the price of a complement (e.g., petrol) increases, demand for the
primary good (e.g., cars) decreases.
• Tastes/Preferences: Popularity increases boost demand.
• Expectations: Expecting higher future prices can increase current demand.
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
(b) Types of Elasticity of Demand
1. Price Elasticity: responsiveness to price change.
= % change in quantity demanded / % change in price
2. Income Elasticity: responsiveness to income change.
= % change in quantity demanded / % change in income
3. Cross Elasticity: responsiveness to price of related goods.
= % change in quantity demanded of commodity A / % change in price of commodity B

3. Give employees more responsibility.

5. Encourage achievement-oriented work design.
6. Involve employees in problem-solving.

Basis   F.W. Taylor   Henri Fayol   Elton Mayo
Approach   Scientific Management   Administrative Theory   Human Relations Approach
Focus   Shop-floor efficiency   Overall management process Human behavior at workplace
Level of
Lower level (workers)   Top and middle level   All levels
Management
Improve productivity   Functions of management   Importance of social and
Key Idea
through scientific methods (planning, organizing, etc.)   psychological factors
Economic man (motivated   Social man (motivated by
View of Worker   Organizational resource
by money)   relationships)
Time study, motion study,
Techniques   14 principles of management Hawthorne experiments
standardization
Motivation   Monetary incentives   Discipline and order   Recognition, belongingness
Improved understanding of
Increased efficiency and   Structured management
Contribution   employee morale and
productivity   theory
motivation
Q5
(a) Line vs Staff & Conflict Resolution
• Line authority: Direct responsibility for achieving objectives.
•   Staff authority: Advisory role providing expertise.
Conflict arises due to:
•   Differences in authority and responsibility
• Lack of understanding of each other's roles
Solutions:
•   Clearly define roles
• Improve communication
• Encourage collaboration
• Conduct joint decision-making
(b) Improving Team Effectiveness
•   Establish clear goals and responsibilities
• Improve communication channels
• Conduct regular meetings
• Promote team spirit over departmental interests
• Ensure leadership coordination
•   Introduce accountability mechanisms
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
Q6
(a)
Consumer equilibrium is the state where a rational consumer maximizes total utility (satisfaction) from
purchasing goods, given their limited income and prevailing market prices. At this point, the last unit of
money spent provides equal marginal utility across all products, or the marginal utility of a good equals
its price, resulting in no incentive to change consumption
A movement along the demand curve is caused solely by a change in a good's price, resulting in a
contraction or expansion of quantity demanded. Conversely, a shift in the entire demand curve,
caused by non-price factors like income or preferences, represents a change in demand at every
price level.
Movement Along the Demand Curve (Change in Quantity Demanded) – Diagram a
• Cause: A change in the price of the product itself.
•   Effect: A move from one point to another on the same curve.
• Upward Movement (Contraction): Price increases, resulting in a decrease in quantity
demanded.
• Downward Movement (Expansion): Price decreases, resulting in an increase in quantity
demanded. [1,
Shift in the Demand Curve (Change in Demand) - Diagram b
• Cause: Changes in factors other than the product's price, such as income, tastes, population, or
prices of related goods.
•   Effect: The entire curve moves to the left or right.
• Rightward Shift (Increase in Demand): Caused by factors like increased income, higher
popularity, or higher prices of substitutes.
• Leftward Shift (Decrease in Demand): Caused by factors like reduced income, lower
popularity, or lower prices of substitutes.
Key Determinants Causing Shifts
• Income: Higher income increases demand for normal goods, shifting the curve right.
• Prices of Related Goods: If the price of a substitute (e.g., tea) increases, demand for the primary
good (e.g., coffee) increases. If the price of a complement (e.g., petrol) increases, demand for the
primary good (e.g., cars) decreases.
• Tastes/Preferences: Popularity increases boost demand.
• Expectations: Expecting higher future prices can increase current demand.
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
(b) Types of Elasticity of Demand
1. Price Elasticity: responsiveness to price change.
= % change in quantity demanded / % change in price
2. Income Elasticity: responsiveness to income change.
= % change in quantity demanded / % change in income
3. Cross Elasticity: responsiveness to price of related goods.
= % change in quantity demanded of commodity A / % change in price of commodity B
Q7
(a) Herzberg’s theory
Herzberg classified factors into:
• Hygiene factors: salary, working conditions, policies, supervision, and job security.
• Motivators: achievement, recognition, responsibility, advancement, meaningful work.
Case analysis
The Company has provided hygiene factors such as good salary and working conditions. These may
reduce dissatisfaction, but they do not create strong motivation.
Employees are leaving because motivator factors are weak:
• Work is Monotonous,
• Recognition is Low,
• Growth Opportunities are Limited,
•   Responsibility may be Low.
Practical steps
1. Enrich jobs by making work more meaningful.
2. Recognise good performance.
3. Give employees more responsibility.
4. Create career growth and promotion paths.
5. Encourage achievement-oriented work design.
6. Involve employees in problem-solving.
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
Important distinction
Hygiene factors prevent dissatisfaction; motivators create satisfaction and commitment.
Conclusion
The organisation must go beyond salary and facilities. To improve motivation, it should focus on
recognition, responsibility, achievement, and career development.
(b) Inventory & Coordination Improvement
Problems in the case
• Excess inventory increases carrying cost.
• Poor coordination leads to shortages and emergency buying.
• Working capital gets blocked.
•   Functional departments are operating in isolation.
Inventory control meaning
Inventory control means maintaining optimum stock levels so that materials are available when needed
without excessive investment.
Inventory control techniques
1. Set minimum, maximum, and reorder levels.
2. Use EOQ-type thinking for economic ordering.
3. Classify inventory by importance.
4. Monitor fast-moving and slow-moving items.
5. Maintain proper stock records.
6. Avoid overstocking and understocking.
Need for functional coordination
• Marketing should share demand forecasts and promotion schedules.
• Finance should monitor cost, budget, and working capital.
• Operations/HR should ensure manpower and process readiness for expected demand.
Benefits of coordination
•   Better Planning,
• Lower Carrying Cost,
• Fewer Stockouts,
• Smoother Production,
• Improved Profitability,
• Stronger Customer Service.
Conclusion
Inventory control alone is not enough. Effective coordination among marketing, finance, and
operations is essential for cost control and operational efficiency.
********** 7 **********
````

Answers for:
Q1. Brief Notes
(a) PPC (Production Possibility Curve)
• Represents maximum combinations of two goods using given resources and technology.
• Downward sloping due to scarcity of resources.
• Concave to origin due to increasing opportunity cost.
•   Points on curve show efficient use, inside show inefficiency, outside are unattainable.
(b) Average Cost and Marginal Cost
• AC = TC / Q, MC = ΔTC / ΔQ
• MC intersects AC at its minimum point.
• When MC < AC, AC falls; when MC > AC, AC rises.
• Both curves are typically U-shaped due to law of returns.

•   Privatization: increased role of private sector.
•   Globalization: integration with global markets.
•   Objective: improve efficiency, growth, and competitiveness.
(d) Levels of Management
• Top Level: Policy making and strategic decisions.
• Middle Level: Implementation of plans and coordination.
• Lower Level: Supervision of day-to-day operations.

Q2
(a) Isoquant and Producer Equilibrium
• Isoquant shows combinations of inputs yielding same output.
• Downward sloping and convex due to diminishing MRTS.
• Producer equilibrium occurs where isoquant is tangent to isocost line.
•   Condition: MRTS = ratio of input prices.
• At this M point in diagram, producer minimizes cost for given output.
(b) Cost Types & MC-AC Relationship
Fixed & Variable Cost
Explicit & Implicit Cost
Economic & Accounting Cost
MC, AC, TC
All the above should be explained in detail by the student.
• Fixed cost remains constant irrespective of output.
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
• Variable cost changes with output.
•   Total cost = Fixed cost + Variable cost.
• MC and AC are U-shaped.
• MC intersects AC at its minimum point.
•   Relationship indicates cost efficiency and production behavior.
Q3
(a) Perfect vs Imperfect Markets
• Perfect Market:
Large number of firms, homogeneous product, price taker, free entry and exit.
• Imperfect Market:
Fewer firms, product differentiation, price maker, barriers to entry.
Types of Imperfect Markets:
• Monopoly (single seller)
• Monopolistic competition (many sellers with differentiated products)
• Oligopoly (few dominant firms)
• Duopoly (two sellers)
All markets should be explained with example

Q1

Q2   (Answers should be related to as follows)   [20]
a.   Critically evaluate the various economic systems and discuss how the
problem of scarcity of resources is addressed in each system?
Ans: 1. Capitalist (Market) Economy
•   Resource Allocation: Uses the price mechanism—resources
are owned privately, and markets decide what, how and for whom to
produce based on supply and demand.
•   Scarcity Approach: Scarcity is addressed by rationing through
prices; only those able and willing to pay obtain resources.
Opportunity cost guides choices; the most valued uses attract
investment. Efficiency is high, but inequality and under-provision of
public goods may persist.
•   Critique: While innovation is encouraged, unequal resource
distribution can leave some needs unmet. Market failures like
monopolies or externalities diminish allocative efficiency.
2. Socialist (Planned/Command) Economy
•   Resource Allocation: The government owns most resources
and uses centralized plans to allocate them. Production and
distribution are determined by planners with social welfare as the
main goal.
•   Scarcity Approach: Scarcity is handled through rationing,
quota systems, and state prioritization; essential goods and services
are often guaranteed. Equal access is emphasized but with less
CO-1   flexibility for individual preferences.
BL- Understand and   •   Critique: Risk   of   inefficiency   and   misallocation— [10]
Apply   overproduction (gluts) or shortages occur if planners misjudge needs,
as real-time consumer choices are missing. Innovation can be stifled,
and black markets may emerge for unmet needs.
3. Mixed Economy
•   Resource Allocation: Combines private enterprise with
government regulation. The market handles most economic activity,
but the government intervenes for social welfare, public goods, and
correcting market failures.
•   Scarcity Approach: Scarcity is addressed through a blend:
prices guide many resource allocations, but laws, subsidies, and
public programs redistribute resources to address social welfare and
ensure minimum standards.
•   Critique: Tries to balance efficiency with equity. However,
government intervention can introduce distortions and red tape, while
unregulated sectors may retain some inefficiencies and inequality.
4. Traditional Economy
•   Resource Allocation: Relies on customs and traditions—
resources are allocated by established patterns, like farming or barter.
•   Scarcity Approach: Scarcity is addressed by subsistence
production and sharing; excess is rare and roles are fixed.
•   Critique: Stable but inflexible; little innovation or adaptation
to changing wants, and growth is limited.

b.   Assess the concept of price elasticity of demand in detail. Discuss the
factors   influencing   elasticity.
Ans. Price elasticity of demand (PED) measures how sensitive the
quantity demanded of a good   is to changes in   its price.
Mathematically, it is defined as:
% change in quantity demanded
PED =
% change in price
A result greater than 1 (in absolute value) indicates elastic demand —
quantity demanded changes more than proportionally to price
changes. If the value is less than 1, demand is inelastic — quantity
demanded changes less than the price. If PED is exactly 1, demand is
unit elastic. Most goods have a negative PED because of the inverse
relationship between price and quantity demanded, as per the law of
demand.[2][4][1]
Factors Influencing Price Elasticity
1. Availability of substitutes: If close substitutes exist, demand is
usually more elastic. Consumers can easily switch to alternatives
CO- 1; BL- Apply   [10]
if price rises.
2. Necessity vs. Luxury: Necessities tend to have inelastic
demand (consumers buy regardless of price), whereas luxuries
are more elastic.
3. Proportion of income: Goods taking up a larger share of the
consumer's income have more elastic demand, since price
changes have a bigger impact on budget.
4. Time horizon: Demand elasticity increases over time, as
consumers find alternatives or adjust behavior.
5. Addictiveness and brand loyalty: Addictive or strongly
branded goods usually have inelastic demand because buyers
continue purchasing even if prices rise.
6. Definition of the market: Broader categories (e.g., food) are
less elastic than narrow ones (e.g., specific ice cream brands).
In summary, price elasticity of demand reflects how consumers
respond to price changes and is shaped by substitutes, necessity,
income share, time, and consumer habits. It is central to business
pricing,   tax   policy,   and   economic   welfare   analysis.
Q3   [20]
a.
Compare and contrast cardinal utility and ordinal utility approaches.
Critically discuss their relevance in analyzing consumer behavior.
CO-2; BL- Understand
[10]
& Analyze   Ans. Cardinal Utility vs Ordinal Utility: Comparison and
Relevance
Cardinal utility is the approach in economics where satisfaction from
consuming goods is assigned numerical values ("utils"). It assumes
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
utility can be precisely measured—such as saying eating pizza gives
10 units of satisfaction, while ice cream gives 6 units—which allows
for direct comparison and calculation of marginal utility, total utility,
and how much more a consumer prefers one bundle over another. This
quantitative method is used in early microeconomic models, like
Marshall’s marginal utility analysis.
Ordinal utility, preferred in modern economics, rejects measurable
values and instead ranks preferences qualitatively. Here, consumers
only indicate order—"tea is preferred to coffee"—without quantifying
by how much. Ordinal utility forms the basis for indifference curve
analysis, which describes consumer equilibrium and responses to
price/income changes using preference rankings rather than
numbers.[2][3][4]
Critical discussion: While cardinal utility made early economic
modeling possible, its reliance on measuring subjective satisfaction
proved unrealistic—consumers rarely assign exact numbers to
happiness from goods. Ordinal utility, being based on rankings,
reflects actual decision-making and is more robust for analyzing
diverse consumer behavior. Most modern theories use ordinal utility
because it does not assume interpersonal or precise measurement of
satisfaction, just observable choices. Nevertheless, both approaches
enrich our understanding of consumption: cardinal utility highlights
concepts like diminishing marginal utility, and ordinal utility provides
tools for analyzing choices, market demand, and equilibrium using
indifference curves and budget constraints.
CO-2;   b.   Discuss the Law of Diminishing Returns. Explain the various stages
BL-   understand and   of   this law   with   the   help   of   a   table and   diagram.
Analyze
Ans. Law of Diminishing Returns: Explanation and Stages
The Law of Diminishing Returns states that when increasing units of
a variable input (e.g., labor) are added to fixed inputs (e.g., land or
capital), the total output initially increases at an increasing rate, then
at a decreasing rate, and eventually declines. This law operates only
in the short run when at least one input remains fixed.
Stages:
[10]
1. Increasing Returns: Total product (TP) rises at an increasing
rate as each added unit of variable input contributes more than
the previous one. Marginal product (MP) also increases. This
happens because fixed inputs are underutilized initially, leading
to better utilization and specialization.
2. Diminishing Returns: TP continues to rise but at a decreasing
rate; MP declines but remains positive. Due to fixed input
constraints, added variable units become less effective. This
stage is economically important as rational producers prefer to
operate here.
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
3. Negative Returns: TP decreases; MP becomes negative as
overcrowding and inefficiency set in from excessive variable
input.
Example Table:
Labor   Total Product   Marginal
Units   (TP)   Product (MP)
1   10   10
2   25   15
3   36   11
4   44   8
5   48   4
6   48   0
7   45   -3
Diagram: The total product curve rises steeply in Stage 1, flattens in
Stage 2, and declines in Stage 3. Marginal product curve peaks early,
then declines, crossing zero at the maximum TP point.
This law guides producers to optimize input usage and avoid
inefficiencies caused by overuse   of   variable   inputs.[1][2][3]
Q4   [20]
a.   Using diagrams, explain how price and output are determined in a
perfectly competitive market. Discuss all the features of the Perfect
Competition   Structure   and   its   example.
Ans: In a perfectly competitive market, price and output are
determined where the industry supply and demand curves intersect,
establishing the equilibrium market price (P1). Individual firms, being
price takers, face a horizontal demand curve at this price. Each firm
CO- 3; BL-   maximizes profit by producing where marginal cost (MC) equals
marginal revenue (MR), which, in perfect competition, equals the [10]
Understand & Apply
market price. This point determines the firm's optimal output (Q1).
The diagram shows the industry supply and demand curves setting the
equilibrium price, with firms’ MC curves intersecting MR at Q1. The
features of perfect competition include many firms selling
homogeneous products, free entry and exit, perfect knowledge, and
no market power for any single firm. Examples include markets for
agricultural products like wheat or vegetables, where numerous
sellers produce identical goods and prices are dictated by overall
supply and demand.
b.   Discuss the main components of the New Economic Policy (1991) in
CO-3; BL- Remember   India. How did those components together transform the Indian
economy?   [10]
and Apply
Ans: The New Economic Policy (NEP) of 1991 in India was a
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
landmark shift that aimed to transform a struggling, inward-looking
economy into a vibrant, market-oriented, and globally integrated one.
The   policy’s   three core components were   Liberalization,
Privatization, and Globalization (LPG), each bringing substantial
reform and long-term economic change.[1][2]
Main Components
• Liberalization: The government reduced its direct control over
industries by abolishing licensing requirements for most sectors,
removing trade barriers, reducing regulations, and opening up to
foreign goods and capital. Financial sector reforms allowed the
entry of private and foreign banks, deregulated interest rates,
and improved monetary policy transparency.[3][1]
• Privatization: The public sector’s dominant role was reduced
by disinvesting state enterprises, allowing private sector entry
into key industries, and making public sector firms more
competitive. Monopoly laws were relaxed to encourage growth
and efficiency.[4][1][3]
• Globalization: India integrated with the global economy by
promoting exports, encouraging foreign direct investment (FDI),
easing import restrictions, and linking domestic policies to
international standards. The rupee was devalued to boost
exports, and investment approvals for foreign companies were
streamlined.[2][5][1]
Transformative Impacts on the Indian Economy
These reforms produced deep and wide-ranging effects. Economic
growth accelerated, with GDP growth rates rising to 6–7% per annum
in the decades that followed, sometimes reaching double digits.
Foreign investment surged—from just $97 million in 1991 to over $81
billion by 2024—bringing in modern technology, managerial know-
how, and global integration.[6][1]
The private sector became a major driver in industries like IT,
banking, telecom, and manufacturing. Foreign exchange reserves
grew dramatically, providing stability and confidence. Poverty levels
declined as rapid growth created jobs and raised living standards,
although the gap between rich and poor also widened. India became a
key global economic player, joining organizations like the WTO and
G20, increasing exports, and negotiating free trade agreements.[7][1]
In sum, the 1991 NEP broke old barriers, energized productivity, and
converted India into a dynamic, globally competitive economy by
combining the strengths of liberalized markets, private enterprise, and
global   engagement.[1][2][6]
Q5   [20]
CO- 4; BL- Understand   a.   Discuss the contributions of F.W. Taylor and Henry Fayol to the
[10]
& Apply   development of management thought. Compare their approaches with
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
suitable   examples.
Ans: F.W. Taylor and Henry Fayol are foundational figures in
management thought, each shaping the discipline with distinct yet
complementary approaches.
Taylor's   Contributions   (Scientific   Management):
F.W. Taylor, known as the 'Father of Scientific Management,' focused
on improving efficiency at the shop-floor level. His main principles
included: using scientific methods to analyze work rather than relying
on rule-of-thumb, selecting and training workers scientifically,
fostering cooperation between management and workers, and
implementing differential piece-rate wage systems. Taylor introduced
"time and motion studies" to optimize task performance and believed
in splitting responsibilities—managers planned while workers
executed. For example, at Bethlehem Steel, his studies improved
shovel design, reducing waste and boosting output.[1][2][3]
Fayol's   Contributions   (Administrative   Theory):
Henry Fayol, regarded as the 'Father of Modern Management,' shifted
the focus to the organization as a whole. He set out 14 management
principles such as division of work, authority with responsibility,
unity of command, unity of direction, and centralization. Fayol
defined   five   managerial   functions:   planning,   organizing,
commanding, coordinating, and controlling, offering a top-down view
of management. His methods are universal, guiding managers in all
industries. For example, his "unity of command" principle ensures
each employee receives orders from only one superior, preventing
confusion.[4][5][6][7]
Comparison Table:
Aspect   F.W. Taylor   Henry Fayol
Focus   Shop-floor/Production   Top   Management/Whole
Level   Organization
Approac   Bottom-up,   Scientific,   Top-down,   Administrative,
h   Task-based   Process-based
Principle   Four main   (Scientific   Fourteen   (Administrative
s   management)   theory)
Methods   Observation,   Experience,   general
experimentation   guidelines
Wage   Differential   piece-rate   Profit sharing emphasized
System   system
Example   Optimizing   worker   Structuring departments, roles
tasks/machines
In essence, Taylor emphasized "how things are done efficiently,"
helping operational managers boost productivity, while Fayol stressed
"how things are managed and led," advising top managers on
administration. Both laid the groundwork for modern management—
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
a blend of scientific analysis and administrative best practices—
which   remains   relevant   across   industries   today.[8][9][10]
b.   Examine the key steps involved in the planning process. Why do many
organizational   plans   fail   despite   careful   preparation?
Ans: The planning process typically involves the following steps:
1. Setting Objectives: Clearly define what the organization aims
to achieve. Objectives should be specific, measurable,
achievable, relevant, and time-bound (SMART).[1][2][3]
2. Developing Planning Premises: Assess internal and external
factors likely to affect plans. This includes market trends,
resource availability, legal considerations, and competition.[4][1]
3. Identifying Alternative Courses of Action: Generate different
ways to reach objectives, considering both conventional and
innovative options.[5][1]
4. Evaluating Alternatives: Analyze the strengths, weaknesses,
advantages, and risks of each option to make an informed
decision.[1][4]
5. Selecting the Best Alternative: Choose the most appropriate
plan based on evaluation, balancing efficiency, feasibility, and
alignment with organizational goals.[6][1]
CO-4;   6. Formulating Derivative Plans: Develop detailed sub-plans for
BL- Apply   departments or teams to support the main plan. This ensures [10]
coordination across the organization.[4]
7. Implementation: Communicate the plan, allocate resources,
assign responsibilities, and initiate action.[7][1]
8. Monitoring and Follow-Up: Continually review progress,
compare results with objectives, and make adjustments as
necessary to stay on track.[5][4]
Why Organizational Plans Fail
Despite careful preparation, many organizational plans do not achieve
their intended results due to several reasons:
• Lack of Clarity: Ambiguous goals or poor communication lead
to confusion and misaligned efforts.[8]
• Poor Execution: Even well-made plans fail if implementation
is weak, resources are inadequate, or   staff are not
committed.[9][8]
• Resource Constraints: Insufficient finances, manpower,
technology, or time result in bottlenecks.[9]
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
• Resistance to Change: Employees or management may be
reluctant to accept new plans due to entrenched habits or fear of
loss.[10]
• Inadequate Monitoring: Failure to regularly review progress
and respond to setbacks causes strategies to drift off track.[8][9]
• External Environmental Changes:   Market   volatility,
regulatory shifts, or technological disruption can render plans
obsolete.[4]
• Unrealistic Objectives: Overly ambitious or poorly researched
goals set organizations up for disappointment.[9]
• Lack of Leadership Commitment: Without strong support
from top management, plans may lack authority and
momentum.[8]
In summary, successful organizational planning demands not only
careful preparation but also clear communication, resource allocation,
adaptability, consistent follow-through, and buy-in from all involved
stakeholders.The planning process in management involves several
systematic steps. First, organizations set clear objectives, defining
what they want to achieve in specific, measurable terms. Next, they
develop planning premises by assessing external and internal factors
likely to impact their goals, such as market trends and resource
availability. Third, they identify alternative courses of action,
generating different strategies to accomplish objectives. These
alternatives are then evaluated based on feasibility, costs, risks, and
expected outcomes. After this evaluation, decision-makers select the
most suitable plan and proceed to formulate derivative or supporting
plans for each department. The plan is implemented by assigning
resources, communicating roles, and executing tasks. Finally,
organizations monitor progress, comparing results against objectives
and making necessary adjustments to ensure success.[6][1][5][4][8][9]
Despite careful planning, many organizational plans fail. Key reasons
include a lack of clarity in goals or communication, which leads to
misalignment and confusion among employees. Poor execution can
undermine even the best plans, especially when resources are
insufficient or staff are not fully committed. Resistance to change is
common, as employees may fear disruption or feel attached to existing
routines. Plans can become outdated due to unexpected changes in the
external environment, such as new technologies or regulatory shifts.
Insufficient monitoring and follow-up also allow problems to go
unnoticed until it is too late to adjust course. Unrealistic expectations
and lack of top management support further contribute to
organizational plans falling short of their intended outcomes.[10][4][8][9]

a.   A new enterprise plans to enter the renewable energy sector by
establishing a solar panel manufacturing facility. The founders are
concerned about designing the right Organisational structure so that
responsibilities are clear, resources are efficiently used, and the
business can expand in the future. Discuss how the principles of
organising can guide this enterprise in building an effective
Organisational   framework.   Discuss   any   five   of   them.
Ans: To design an efficient and scalable organisational structure for a
new solar panel manufacturing enterprise, applying proven principles
of organising is critical. These principles clarify roles, streamline
resource use, and support expansion as the business grows. Five key
principles relevant for this context are:
1. Division of Work
Tasks should be divided according to specialization, allowing
employees to focus on what they do best. For example, separate
engineering, procurement, production, and sales departments enhance
productivity and expertise.
2. Authority and Responsibility
Managers must have clear authority to make decisions and assign
work, balanced by corresponding responsibility for outcomes. This
ensures accountability, reduces conflict, and enables prompt
CO-4 & 5;   resolution of challenges on the shop floor or in logistics.
BL- Understand and   [10]
Apply   3. Scalar Chain
A well-defined line of command—running from top management to
lower-level staff—facilitates clear communication and reporting. In a
solar panel plant, this could mean a structured hierarchy from the
general manager to line supervisors and workers.
4. Unity of Command
Each employee should report to only one superior, preventing
confusion and overlapping instructions. This   is essential in
manufacturing environments to maintain operational discipline and
clarity.
5. Coordination
Synergy among departments (production, quality control, logistics) is
key to efficient functioning. Principles of coordination help
synchronize efforts, foster teamwork, and ensure that the various units
work towards common goals—such as meeting production targets or
delivering finished panels on schedule.
By rigorously observing these principles, the solar panel enterprise
can avoid miscommunication, wastage, overlaps, and bottlenecks—
laying the foundation for sustainable growth and competitive
performance in the renewable energy sector.To build an effective
organisational structure for a solar panel manufacturing enterprise,
applying the principles of organising ensures clear responsibility,

• Division of Work: Specialisation enhances efficiency. Assign

productivity and facilitate expert performance.[1]
• Authority and Responsibility: Managers need both the power
to make decisions and accountability for the results. Explicit

• Unity of Command: Each employee should receive orders from
only one superior, cutting out conflicting instructions and
ensuring operational clarity in high-tech environments like solar

• Scalar Chain: A clear hierarchy from top management to the
shop floor facilitates smooth communication and problem-

• Coordination: All departments—procurement, production,
sales—must synchronize their efforts towards common goals.
This principle avoids duplication, streamlines timelines, and
adapts to market change, which is crucial for renewable energy

Applying these organising principles helps the enterprise allocate

a robust framework that supports growth and innovation in the

help improve efficiency and maintain market leadership are:

measurable standards for operational costs, on-time departures,
and service quality. These benchmarks provide targets for
employees and systems to achieve and maintain excellence.
2. Accurate and Timely Measurement: Collecting real-time data
on costs, flight schedules, and customer feedback allows IndiGo
to identify issues promptly. This precision enables quick
interventions before minor problems escalate.
3. Comparison of Actual vs. Standards: Analyzing deviations
between   actual performance and   standards   highlights
inefficiencies—such as frequent delays or increased costs—
enabling targeted corrective action.
4. Taking Corrective Actions: When variances occur, IndiGo
must promptly adjust processes, resources, or employee efforts.
For example, improving ground handling or investing in better
maintenance may reduce delays and costs.
5. Continuous Monitoring and Feedback: Ongoing review
ensures controls adapt to evolving operational challenges and
customer expectations, driving continuous improvement and
sustaining competitive advantage.

Q2   (Answers should be related to as follows)   [20]
a.   Critically evaluate the various economic systems and discuss how the
problem of scarcity of resources is addressed in each system?
Ans: 1. Capitalist (Market) Economy
•   Resource Allocation: Uses the price mechanism—resources
are owned privately, and markets decide what, how and for whom to
produce based on supply and demand.
•   Scarcity Approach: Scarcity is addressed by rationing through
prices; only those able and willing to pay obtain resources.
Opportunity cost guides choices; the most valued uses attract
investment. Efficiency is high, but inequality and under-provision of
public goods may persist.
•   Critique: While innovation is encouraged, unequal resource
distribution can leave some needs unmet. Market failures like
monopolies or externalities diminish allocative efficiency.
2. Socialist (Planned/Command) Economy
•   Resource Allocation: The government owns most resources
and uses centralized plans to allocate them. Production and
distribution are determined by planners with social welfare as the
main goal.
•   Scarcity Approach: Scarcity is handled through rationing,
quota systems, and state prioritization; essential goods and services
are often guaranteed. Equal access is emphasized but with less
CO-1   flexibility for individual preferences.
BL- Understand and   •   Critique: Risk   of   inefficiency   and   misallocation— [10]
Apply   overproduction (gluts) or shortages occur if planners misjudge needs,
as real-time consumer choices are missing. Innovation can be stifled,
and black markets may emerge for unmet needs.
3. Mixed Economy
•   Resource Allocation: Combines private enterprise with
government regulation. The market handles most economic activity,
but the government intervenes for social welfare, public goods, and
correcting market failures.
•   Scarcity Approach: Scarcity is addressed through a blend:
prices guide many resource allocations, but laws, subsidies, and
public programs redistribute resources to address social welfare and
ensure minimum standards.
•   Critique: Tries to balance efficiency with equity. However,
government intervention can introduce distortions and red tape, while
unregulated sectors may retain some inefficiencies and inequality.
4. Traditional Economy
•   Resource Allocation: Relies on customs and traditions—
resources are allocated by established patterns, like farming or barter.
•   Scarcity Approach: Scarcity is addressed by subsistence
production and sharing; excess is rare and roles are fixed.
•   Critique: Stable but inflexible; little innovation or adaptation
to changing wants, and growth is limited.

b.   Assess the concept of price elasticity of demand in detail. Discuss the
factors   influencing   elasticity.
Ans. Price elasticity of demand (PED) measures how sensitive the
quantity demanded of a good   is to changes in   its price.
Mathematically, it is defined as:
% change in quantity demanded
PED =
% change in price
A result greater than 1 (in absolute value) indicates elastic demand —
quantity demanded changes more than proportionally to price
changes. If the value is less than 1, demand is inelastic — quantity
demanded changes less than the price. If PED is exactly 1, demand is
unit elastic. Most goods have a negative PED because of the inverse
relationship between price and quantity demanded, as per the law of
demand.[2][4][1]
Factors Influencing Price Elasticity
1. Availability of substitutes: If close substitutes exist, demand is
usually more elastic. Consumers can easily switch to alternatives
CO- 1; BL- Apply   [10]
if price rises.
2. Necessity vs. Luxury: Necessities tend to have inelastic
demand (consumers buy regardless of price), whereas luxuries
are more elastic.
3. Proportion of income: Goods taking up a larger share of the
consumer's income have more elastic demand, since price
changes have a bigger impact on budget.
4. Time horizon: Demand elasticity increases over time, as
consumers find alternatives or adjust behavior.
5. Addictiveness and brand loyalty: Addictive or strongly
branded goods usually have inelastic demand because buyers
continue purchasing even if prices rise.
6. Definition of the market: Broader categories (e.g., food) are
less elastic than narrow ones (e.g., specific ice cream brands).
In summary, price elasticity of demand reflects how consumers
respond to price changes and is shaped by substitutes, necessity,
income share, time, and consumer habits. It is central to business
pricing,   tax   policy,   and   economic   welfare   analysis.
Q3   [20]
a.
Compare and contrast cardinal utility and ordinal utility approaches.
Critically discuss their relevance in analyzing consumer behavior.
CO-2; BL- Understand
[10]
& Analyze   Ans. Cardinal Utility vs Ordinal Utility: Comparison and
Relevance
Cardinal utility is the approach in economics where satisfaction from
consuming goods is assigned numerical values ("utils"). It assumes
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
utility can be precisely measured—such as saying eating pizza gives
10 units of satisfaction, while ice cream gives 6 units—which allows
for direct comparison and calculation of marginal utility, total utility,
and how much more a consumer prefers one bundle over another. This
quantitative method is used in early microeconomic models, like
Marshall’s marginal utility analysis.
Ordinal utility, preferred in modern economics, rejects measurable
values and instead ranks preferences qualitatively. Here, consumers
only indicate order—"tea is preferred to coffee"—without quantifying
by how much. Ordinal utility forms the basis for indifference curve
analysis, which describes consumer equilibrium and responses to
price/income changes using preference rankings rather than
numbers.[2][3][4]
Critical discussion: While cardinal utility made early economic
modeling possible, its reliance on measuring subjective satisfaction
proved unrealistic—consumers rarely assign exact numbers to
happiness from goods. Ordinal utility, being based on rankings,
reflects actual decision-making and is more robust for analyzing
diverse consumer behavior. Most modern theories use ordinal utility
because it does not assume interpersonal or precise measurement of
satisfaction, just observable choices. Nevertheless, both approaches
enrich our understanding of consumption: cardinal utility highlights
concepts like diminishing marginal utility, and ordinal utility provides
tools for analyzing choices, market demand, and equilibrium using
indifference curves and budget constraints.
CO-2;   b.   Discuss the Law of Diminishing Returns. Explain the various stages
BL-   understand and   of   this law   with   the   help   of   a   table and   diagram.
Analyze
Ans. Law of Diminishing Returns: Explanation and Stages
The Law of Diminishing Returns states that when increasing units of
a variable input (e.g., labor) are added to fixed inputs (e.g., land or
capital), the total output initially increases at an increasing rate, then
at a decreasing rate, and eventually declines. This law operates only
in the short run when at least one input remains fixed.
Stages:
[10]
1. Increasing Returns: Total product (TP) rises at an increasing
rate as each added unit of variable input contributes more than
the previous one. Marginal product (MP) also increases. This
happens because fixed inputs are underutilized initially, leading
to better utilization and specialization.
2. Diminishing Returns: TP continues to rise but at a decreasing
rate; MP declines but remains positive. Due to fixed input
constraints, added variable units become less effective. This
stage is economically important as rational producers prefer to
operate here.
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
3. Negative Returns: TP decreases; MP becomes negative as
overcrowding and inefficiency set in from excessive variable
input.
Example Table:
Labor   Total Product   Marginal
Units   (TP)   Product (MP)
1   10   10
2   25   15
3   36   11
4   44   8
5   48   4
6   48   0
7   45   -3
Diagram: The total product curve rises steeply in Stage 1, flattens in
Stage 2, and declines in Stage 3. Marginal product curve peaks early,
then declines, crossing zero at the maximum TP point.
This law guides producers to optimize input usage and avoid
inefficiencies caused by overuse   of   variable   inputs.[1][2][3]
Q4   [20]
a.   Using diagrams, explain how price and output are determined in a
perfectly competitive market. Discuss all the features of the Perfect
Competition   Structure   and   its   example.
Ans: In a perfectly competitive market, price and output are
determined where the industry supply and demand curves intersect,
establishing the equilibrium market price (P1). Individual firms, being
price takers, face a horizontal demand curve at this price. Each firm
CO- 3; BL-   maximizes profit by producing where marginal cost (MC) equals
marginal revenue (MR), which, in perfect competition, equals the [10]
Understand & Apply
market price. This point determines the firm's optimal output (Q1).
The diagram shows the industry supply and demand curves setting the
equilibrium price, with firms’ MC curves intersecting MR at Q1. The
features of perfect competition include many firms selling
homogeneous products, free entry and exit, perfect knowledge, and
no market power for any single firm. Examples include markets for
agricultural products like wheat or vegetables, where numerous
sellers produce identical goods and prices are dictated by overall
supply and demand.
b.   Discuss the main components of the New Economic Policy (1991) in
CO-3; BL- Remember   India. How did those components together transform the Indian
economy?   [10]
and Apply
Ans: The New Economic Policy (NEP) of 1991 in India was a
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
landmark shift that aimed to transform a struggling, inward-looking
economy into a vibrant, market-oriented, and globally integrated one.
The   policy’s   three core components were   Liberalization,
Privatization, and Globalization (LPG), each bringing substantial
reform and long-term economic change.[1][2]
Main Components
• Liberalization: The government reduced its direct control over
industries by abolishing licensing requirements for most sectors,
removing trade barriers, reducing regulations, and opening up to
foreign goods and capital. Financial sector reforms allowed the
entry of private and foreign banks, deregulated interest rates,
and improved monetary policy transparency.[3][1]
• Privatization: The public sector’s dominant role was reduced
by disinvesting state enterprises, allowing private sector entry
into key industries, and making public sector firms more
competitive. Monopoly laws were relaxed to encourage growth
and efficiency.[4][1][3]
• Globalization: India integrated with the global economy by
promoting exports, encouraging foreign direct investment (FDI),
easing import restrictions, and linking domestic policies to
international standards. The rupee was devalued to boost
exports, and investment approvals for foreign companies were
streamlined.[2][5][1]
Transformative Impacts on the Indian Economy
These reforms produced deep and wide-ranging effects. Economic
growth accelerated, with GDP growth rates rising to 6–7% per annum
in the decades that followed, sometimes reaching double digits.
Foreign investment surged—from just $97 million in 1991 to over $81
billion by 2024—bringing in modern technology, managerial know-
how, and global integration.[6][1]
The private sector became a major driver in industries like IT,
banking, telecom, and manufacturing. Foreign exchange reserves
grew dramatically, providing stability and confidence. Poverty levels
declined as rapid growth created jobs and raised living standards,
although the gap between rich and poor also widened. India became a
key global economic player, joining organizations like the WTO and
G20, increasing exports, and negotiating free trade agreements.[7][1]
In sum, the 1991 NEP broke old barriers, energized productivity, and
converted India into a dynamic, globally competitive economy by
combining the strengths of liberalized markets, private enterprise, and
global   engagement.[1][2][6]
Q5   [20]
CO- 4; BL- Understand   a.   Discuss the contributions of F.W. Taylor and Henry Fayol to the
[10]
& Apply   development of management thought. Compare their approaches with
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
suitable   examples.
Ans: F.W. Taylor and Henry Fayol are foundational figures in
management thought, each shaping the discipline with distinct yet
complementary approaches.
Taylor's   Contributions   (Scientific   Management):
F.W. Taylor, known as the 'Father of Scientific Management,' focused
on improving efficiency at the shop-floor level. His main principles
included: using scientific methods to analyze work rather than relying
on rule-of-thumb, selecting and training workers scientifically,
fostering cooperation between management and workers, and
implementing differential piece-rate wage systems. Taylor introduced
"time and motion studies" to optimize task performance and believed
in splitting responsibilities—managers planned while workers
executed. For example, at Bethlehem Steel, his studies improved
shovel design, reducing waste and boosting output.[1][2][3]
Fayol's   Contributions   (Administrative   Theory):
Henry Fayol, regarded as the 'Father of Modern Management,' shifted
the focus to the organization as a whole. He set out 14 management
principles such as division of work, authority with responsibility,
unity of command, unity of direction, and centralization. Fayol
defined   five   managerial   functions:   planning,   organizing,
commanding, coordinating, and controlling, offering a top-down view
of management. His methods are universal, guiding managers in all
industries. For example, his "unity of command" principle ensures
each employee receives orders from only one superior, preventing
confusion.[4][5][6][7]
Comparison Table:
Aspect   F.W. Taylor   Henry Fayol
Focus   Shop-floor/Production   Top   Management/Whole
Level   Organization
Approac   Bottom-up,   Scientific,   Top-down,   Administrative,
h   Task-based   Process-based
Principle   Four main   (Scientific   Fourteen   (Administrative
s   management)   theory)
Methods   Observation,   Experience,   general
experimentation   guidelines
Wage   Differential   piece-rate   Profit sharing emphasized
System   system
Example   Optimizing   worker   Structuring departments, roles
tasks/machines
In essence, Taylor emphasized "how things are done efficiently,"
helping operational managers boost productivity, while Fayol stressed
"how things are managed and led," advising top managers on
administration. Both laid the groundwork for modern management—
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
a blend of scientific analysis and administrative best practices—
which   remains   relevant   across   industries   today.[8][9][10]
b.   Examine the key steps involved in the planning process. Why do many
organizational   plans   fail   despite   careful   preparation?
Ans: The planning process typically involves the following steps:
1. Setting Objectives: Clearly define what the organization aims
to achieve. Objectives should be specific, measurable,
achievable, relevant, and time-bound (SMART).[1][2][3]
2. Developing Planning Premises: Assess internal and external
factors likely to affect plans. This includes market trends,
resource availability, legal considerations, and competition.[4][1]
3. Identifying Alternative Courses of Action: Generate different
ways to reach objectives, considering both conventional and
innovative options.[5][1]
4. Evaluating Alternatives: Analyze the strengths, weaknesses,
advantages, and risks of each option to make an informed
decision.[1][4]
5. Selecting the Best Alternative: Choose the most appropriate
plan based on evaluation, balancing efficiency, feasibility, and
alignment with organizational goals.[6][1]
CO-4;   6. Formulating Derivative Plans: Develop detailed sub-plans for
BL- Apply   departments or teams to support the main plan. This ensures [10]
coordination across the organization.[4]
7. Implementation: Communicate the plan, allocate resources,
assign responsibilities, and initiate action.[7][1]
8. Monitoring and Follow-Up: Continually review progress,
compare results with objectives, and make adjustments as
necessary to stay on track.[5][4]
Why Organizational Plans Fail
Despite careful preparation, many organizational plans do not achieve
their intended results due to several reasons:
• Lack of Clarity: Ambiguous goals or poor communication lead
to confusion and misaligned efforts.[8]
• Poor Execution: Even well-made plans fail if implementation
is weak, resources are inadequate, or   staff are not
committed.[9][8]
• Resource Constraints: Insufficient finances, manpower,
technology, or time result in bottlenecks.[9]
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
• Resistance to Change: Employees or management may be
reluctant to accept new plans due to entrenched habits or fear of
loss.[10]
• Inadequate Monitoring: Failure to regularly review progress
and respond to setbacks causes strategies to drift off track.[8][9]
• External Environmental Changes:   Market   volatility,
regulatory shifts, or technological disruption can render plans
obsolete.[4]
• Unrealistic Objectives: Overly ambitious or poorly researched
goals set organizations up for disappointment.[9]
• Lack of Leadership Commitment: Without strong support
from top management, plans may lack authority and
momentum.[8]
In summary, successful organizational planning demands not only
careful preparation but also clear communication, resource allocation,
adaptability, consistent follow-through, and buy-in from all involved
stakeholders.The planning process in management involves several
systematic steps. First, organizations set clear objectives, defining
what they want to achieve in specific, measurable terms. Next, they
develop planning premises by assessing external and internal factors
likely to impact their goals, such as market trends and resource
availability. Third, they identify alternative courses of action,
generating different strategies to accomplish objectives. These
alternatives are then evaluated based on feasibility, costs, risks, and
expected outcomes. After this evaluation, decision-makers select the
most suitable plan and proceed to formulate derivative or supporting
plans for each department. The plan is implemented by assigning
resources, communicating roles, and executing tasks. Finally,
organizations monitor progress, comparing results against objectives
and making necessary adjustments to ensure success.[6][1][5][4][8][9]
Despite careful planning, many organizational plans fail. Key reasons
include a lack of clarity in goals or communication, which leads to
misalignment and confusion among employees. Poor execution can
undermine even the best plans, especially when resources are
insufficient or staff are not fully committed. Resistance to change is
common, as employees may fear disruption or feel attached to existing
routines. Plans can become outdated due to unexpected changes in the
external environment, such as new technologies or regulatory shifts.
Insufficient monitoring and follow-up also allow problems to go
unnoticed until it is too late to adjust course. Unrealistic expectations
and lack of top management support further contribute to
organizational plans falling short of their intended outcomes.[10][4][8][9]

a.   A new enterprise plans to enter the renewable energy sector by
establishing a solar panel manufacturing facility. The founders are
concerned about designing the right Organisational structure so that
responsibilities are clear, resources are efficiently used, and the
business can expand in the future. Discuss how the principles of
organising can guide this enterprise in building an effective
Organisational   framework.   Discuss   any   five   of   them.
Ans: To design an efficient and scalable organisational structure for a
new solar panel manufacturing enterprise, applying proven principles
of organising is critical. These principles clarify roles, streamline
resource use, and support expansion as the business grows. Five key
principles relevant for this context are:
1. Division of Work
Tasks should be divided according to specialization, allowing
employees to focus on what they do best. For example, separate
engineering, procurement, production, and sales departments enhance
productivity and expertise.
2. Authority and Responsibility
Managers must have clear authority to make decisions and assign
work, balanced by corresponding responsibility for outcomes. This
ensures accountability, reduces conflict, and enables prompt
CO-4 & 5;   resolution of challenges on the shop floor or in logistics.
BL- Understand and   [10]
Apply   3. Scalar Chain
A well-defined line of command—running from top management to
lower-level staff—facilitates clear communication and reporting. In a
solar panel plant, this could mean a structured hierarchy from the
general manager to line supervisors and workers.
4. Unity of Command
Each employee should report to only one superior, preventing
confusion and overlapping instructions. This   is essential in
manufacturing environments to maintain operational discipline and
clarity.
5. Coordination
Synergy among departments (production, quality control, logistics) is
key to efficient functioning. Principles of coordination help
synchronize efforts, foster teamwork, and ensure that the various units
work towards common goals—such as meeting production targets or
delivering finished panels on schedule.
By rigorously observing these principles, the solar panel enterprise
can avoid miscommunication, wastage, overlaps, and bottlenecks—
laying the foundation for sustainable growth and competitive
performance in the renewable energy sector.To build an effective
organisational structure for a solar panel manufacturing enterprise,
applying the principles of organising ensures clear responsibility,
********** 10 **********

resource optimisation, and future-ready expansion. Here are five

• Division of Work: Specialisation enhances efficiency. Assign

productivity and facilitate expert performance.[1]
• Authority and Responsibility: Managers need both the power
to make decisions and accountability for the results. Explicit
reporting lines and balanced responsibility prevent confusion
and drive disciplined execution.[2]
• Unity of Command: Each employee should receive orders from
only one superior, cutting out conflicting instructions and
ensuring operational clarity in high-tech environments like solar

• Scalar Chain: A clear hierarchy from top management to the
shop floor facilitates smooth communication and problem-
solving. This alignment helps scale processes efficiently as the

• Coordination: All departments—procurement, production,
sales—must synchronize their efforts towards common goals.
This principle avoids duplication, streamlines timelines, and
adapts to market change, which is crucial for renewable energy
ventures.[1]
Applying these organising principles helps the enterprise allocate
resources efficiently, clarify roles and decision-making, and establish
a robust framework that supports growth and innovation in the
dynamic   renewable   energy   sector.[2][1]

help improve efficiency and maintain market leadership are:

measurable standards for operational costs, on-time departures,
and service quality. These benchmarks provide targets for
employees and systems to achieve and maintain excellence.
2. Accurate and Timely Measurement: Collecting real-time data
on costs, flight schedules, and customer feedback allows IndiGo
to identify issues promptly. This precision enables quick
interventions before minor problems escalate.
3. Comparison of Actual vs. Standards: Analyzing deviations
between   actual performance and   standards   highlights
inefficiencies—such as frequent delays or increased costs—
enabling targeted corrective action.
4. Taking Corrective Actions: When variances occur, IndiGo
must promptly adjust processes, resources, or employee efforts.
For example, improving ground handling or investing in better
maintenance may reduce delays and costs.
5. Continuous Monitoring and Feedback: Ongoing review
ensures controls adapt to evolving operational challenges and
customer expectations, driving continuous improvement and
sustaining competitive advantage.

Q2   (Answers should be related to as follows)   [20]
a.   Critically evaluate the various economic systems and discuss how the
problem of scarcity of resources is addressed in each system?
Ans: 1. Capitalist (Market) Economy
•   Resource Allocation: Uses the price mechanism—resources
are owned privately, and markets decide what, how and for whom to
produce based on supply and demand.
•   Scarcity Approach: Scarcity is addressed by rationing through
prices; only those able and willing to pay obtain resources.
Opportunity cost guides choices; the most valued uses attract
investment. Efficiency is high, but inequality and under-provision of
public goods may persist.
•   Critique: While innovation is encouraged, unequal resource
distribution can leave some needs unmet. Market failures like
monopolies or externalities diminish allocative efficiency.
2. Socialist (Planned/Command) Economy
•   Resource Allocation: The government owns most resources
and uses centralized plans to allocate them. Production and
distribution are determined by planners with social welfare as the
main goal.
•   Scarcity Approach: Scarcity is handled through rationing,
quota systems, and state prioritization; essential goods and services
are often guaranteed. Equal access is emphasized but with less
CO-1   flexibility for individual preferences.
BL- Understand and   •   Critique: Risk   of   inefficiency   and   misallocation— [10]
Apply   overproduction (gluts) or shortages occur if planners misjudge needs,
as real-time consumer choices are missing. Innovation can be stifled,
and black markets may emerge for unmet needs.
3. Mixed Economy
•   Resource Allocation: Combines private enterprise with
government regulation. The market handles most economic activity,
but the government intervenes for social welfare, public goods, and
correcting market failures.
•   Scarcity Approach: Scarcity is addressed through a blend:
prices guide many resource allocations, but laws, subsidies, and
public programs redistribute resources to address social welfare and
ensure minimum standards.
•   Critique: Tries to balance efficiency with equity. However,
government intervention can introduce distortions and red tape, while
unregulated sectors may retain some inefficiencies and inequality.
4. Traditional Economy
•   Resource Allocation: Relies on customs and traditions—
resources are allocated by established patterns, like farming or barter.
•   Scarcity Approach: Scarcity is addressed by subsistence
production and sharing; excess is rare and roles are fixed.
•   Critique: Stable but inflexible; little innovation or adaptation
to changing wants, and growth is limited.
