Q2   (Answers should be related to as follows)   [20]
a.   Explain with diagrams how an equilibrium in demand and supply is
established in a market. Analyze the impact of shifts in demand and
supply   on   the   equilibrium   price   and   quantity.
Market equilibrium occurs where the quantity demanded equals the
quantity supplied, establishing a stable price and quantity in the
market. Shifts in demand or supply alter this equilibrium, leading to
new price and quantity outcomes (see the generated image above).
Market Equilibrium
Market equilibrium is achieved at the point where the demand curve
intersects the supply curve, indicating the price at which the quantity
of a good consumers are willing to buy equals the quantity producers
CO-1
are willing to sell. At this point, there is no surplus or shortage in the
BL- Understand and   [10]
Apply   market, and the price tends to remain stable unless external factors
intervene. The equilibrium price and quantity are determined by the
interaction of market forces under given conditions of consumer
preferences, income levels, production costs, and technology (see the
generated image above).
(see the generated image above)
Impact of Demand Shifts
When demand increases due to factors such as higher consumer
income or changing preferences, the demand curve shifts to the right,
leading to a higher equilibrium price and quantity. Conversely, a
decrease in demand shifts the curve to the left, resulting in a lower
equilibrium price and quantity. These shifts illustrate how changes in
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
non-price determinants of demand affect market outcomes even when
supply remains constant (see the generated image above).
• Increase in demand: higher equilibrium price and quantity (see
the generated image above)
• Decrease in demand: lower equilibrium price and quantity
Impact of Supply Shifts
An increase in supply, caused by technological advancements or
lower input costs, shifts the supply curve to the right, resulting in a
lower equilibrium price and a higher equilibrium quantity. A decrease
in supply, such as from resource scarcity or higher production costs,
shifts the curve to the left, leading to a higher price and reduced
quantity. These movements demonstrate how supply-side factors
independently influence market equilibrium .
• Increase in supply: lower price, higher quantity
• Decrease in supply: higher price, lower quantity
Simultaneous Shifts in Demand and Supply
When both demand and supply shift simultaneously, the effect on
equilibrium price and quantity depends on the relative magnitude and
direction of the shifts. If both curves shift rightward (increase),
quantity rises, but the price change is indeterminate without knowing
which shift is stronger. Similarly, opposing shifts can lead to clear
outcomes for price or quantity, while the other variable's direction
remains uncertain. This complexity underscores the importance of
analyzing both curves dynamically in real-world markets .
b.   Illustrate the law of diminishing marginal utility with real-life
examples   and   graphical   representations.
CO- 2; BL-
Understand and   [10]
The Law of Diminishing Marginal Utility states that as a consumer
Apply
consumes more units of a good or service, the additional satisfaction
(marginal utility) gained from each subsequent unit decreases.
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
Eventually, consuming more units may lead to zero or even negative
marginal utility.
Real-Life Examples
1. Eating Pizza Slices: The first slice of pizza when you are very
hungry provides high satisfaction. The second slice still adds
enjoyment but less than the first. The third or fourth slice adds
even less satisfaction, and by the fifth slice you might feel full
or even uncomfortable, resulting in zero or negative utility.
2. Drinking Water When Thirsty: The first sips quench your
thirst greatly, providing high utility. As you drink more, the
satisfaction decreases until drinking more is not pleasurable.
3. Purchasing the Same Product: Buying one new smartphone
gives high satisfaction, but buying a second identical phone
soon after adds little value; the marginal utility is low or
negative.
Graphical Representation
• The X-axis represents the quantity consumed.
• The Y-axis represents the marginal utility from each unit.
• The marginal utility curve slopes downward, reflecting
diminishing additional satisfaction.
The   curve   may   cross   the   X-axis   at   the
Q3   [20]
a.   Explain how consumer equilibrium is achieved using indifference
curve   analysis.
Consumer equilibrium occurs when a consumer maximizes
CO-2; BL-   satisfaction (utility) from the consumption of two goods, given their [10]
Understand & Apply
income and the prices of these goods. This can be analyzed through
indifference curves and the budget line.
Key Concepts:
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
• An indifference curve represents combinations of two goods
that provide equal satisfaction to the consumer.
• The budget line shows all possible combinations of the two
goods that a consumer can afford with a given income and
prices.
• The Marginal Rate of Substitution (MRS) is the rate at which
a consumer is willing to substitute one good for another while
maintaining the same level of satisfaction (the slope of the
indifference curve).
• The slope of the budget line equals the ratio of the prices of the
two   goods   (
$ \frac{P_X}{P_Y} $).
Conditions for Consumer Equilibrium
1. Slope   Equality   (Tangency   Condition):
$ MRS_{XY} = \frac{MU_X}{MU_Y} = \frac{P_X}{P_Y} $
This means the rate at which the consumer is willing to trade
one good for another equals the market rate at which trade must
happen.
2. Convexity   and   Diminishing   MRS:
The indifference curve must be convex to the origin (reflecting
diminishing MRS), ensuring a unique equilibrium point.
Graphical Representation
• The consumer equilibrium is at the point where the budget line
is tangent to the highest attainable indifference curve.
• At this point, the consumer achieves maximum utility without
exceeding their budget.
• Points inside the budget line are affordable but do not maximize
utility.
• Points outside the budget line provide higher utility but are
unaffordable.
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
Intuition
•   If $ MRS_{XY} > \frac{P_X}{P_Y} $, the consumer values
Good X more relatively and will buy more of it, reducing MRS.
•   If $ MRS_{XY} < \frac{P_X}{P_Y} $, the consumer values
Good Y relatively more and will buy more of Good Y,
increasing MRS.
• This adjustment continues until the equality condition holds,
achieving equilibrium.
In summary, the consumer selects the optimal bundle of goods at the
tangency point, maximizing satisfaction given budget constraints.
CO-2;   b. Why Average Cost (AC) curve is ‘U’ shape? Explain the relationship
BL- Understand and   between AC and MC curves in the Short Run with the help of a
Analyze
diagram.
The short-run Average Cost (AC) curve is U-shaped due to the
combined effects of Average Fixed Costs (AFC) and Average
Variable Costs (AVC) as output changes:
• At low output levels: AC is high because fixed costs are spread
over a small quantity, making AFC per unit large. AVC can also
be high due to inefficiencies from underutilization of resources.
• As output increases: AFC declines sharply since fixed costs [10]
spread over more units. AVC also falls initially due to increasing
returns to the variable factor and better utilization of fixed
resources, causing AC to decline.
• Beyond a certain output (the optimum scale): Diminishing
returns set in, making AVC rise sharply as additional units
become more costly to produce. This increase in AVC
outweighs the continuing small decline in AFC, causing AC to
rise again.
Thus, the AC curve falls, reaches a minimum, and then rises —
making it U-shaped. This behavior is explained by the Law of
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
Variable Proportions and the transition from economies of scale to
diseconomies of scale.
Relationship Between Average Cost (AC) and Marginal Cost
(MC) Curves
• The Marginal Cost (MC) curve shows the cost of producing
one additional unit of output.
• When MC is less than AC, producing an additional unit
decreases the AC, so AC falls.
• When MC is greater than AC, producing an additional unit
increases the AC, so AC rises.
• When MC equals AC, AC is at its minimum point, and the MC
curve intersects the AC curve at this point.
Graphical Summary
• The MC curve is also U-shaped due to diminishing marginal
returns.
• The MC curve cuts the AC curve at the lowest point of the AC
curve.
In summary, the U-shaped AC curve reflects initial economies and
later diseconomies of scale in production, while the MC curve guides
the direction of the AC curve based on whether it lies below or above
the   AC   at   any   output   level.
Q4   [20]
a.
Compare and contrast Monopoly and Oligopoly. Give suitable real-
world   examples   to   support   your   answer.
CO- 3; BL-
[10]
Understand & Apply
Monopoly
• Number of Sellers: A single seller dominates the entire market.
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
• Product Type: Unique product with no close substitutes.
• Market Power: Seller is a price maker with significant control
over price and quantity.
• Competition: Absence of competition.
• Barriers to Entry: Very high, due to legal, economic, or
institutional factors.
• Price Level: Typically higher because of the lack of
competition.
• Consumer Choice: Limited, as only one supplier exists.
Example:
• Public utilities like electricity supply by regional providers.
• U.S. Postal Service’s monopoly on first-class mail delivery.
Oligopoly
• Number of Sellers: A few large firms dominate the market
(usually 2 to 10).
• Product Type: Products can be homogeneous (e.g., steel) or
differentiated (e.g., automobiles).
• Market Power: Firms have some control over price but are
mutually interdependent.
• Competition: Exists but limited; firms consider rivals’ actions.
• Barriers to Entry: High, often due to economies of scale and
capital requirements.
• Price Level: Moderate; price rigidity and occasional price wars
or collusion occur.
• Consumer Choice: Greater than monopoly but limited
compared to perfect competition.
Example:
• Automobile industry (e.g., Toyota, Ford, Hyundai).
• Smartphone manufacturers (e.g., Apple, Samsung, OnePlus).
********** 9 **********
--- page 10 ---
Key Differences
Feature   Monopoly   Oligopoly
Sellers   Single   Few (2-10)
Product   Unique   Homogeneous   or
differentiated products
Market   Full   control   Shared,   interdependent
Control   over price   pricing
Competition   None   Some, with strategic behavior
Entry   Very High   High
Barriers
Pricing   Price maker   Price rigidity and strategic
pricing
Consumer   Limited   More   options   but   still
Choice   restricted
Understanding these differences helps explain market behaviors like
pricing, output decisions, and consumer impact in different industries.
b.   Explain the impact of LPG on the growth of the Indian Economy after
1991.
The Liberalization, Privatization, and Globalization (LPG) reforms
initiated in 1991 brought a transformative impact on the Indian
economy by shifting it from a closed, state-controlled system to an
CO-3; BL-
open, market-driven economy integrated with the global market.   [10]
Remember & Apply
Key Impacts of LPG on India's Economic Growth:
1. Acceleration of GDP Growth:
o Before 1991, India’s GDP growth averaged around 3.5%
annually, known as the “Hindu rate of growth.”
********** 10 **********
--- page 11 ---
o Post-reforms, growth accelerated to an average of 6.5%
annually between 1991 and 2010, with peaks around 8.5%
in the mid-2000s.
o This rapid growth was driven by increased efficiency,
competition, and private sector development.
2. Increase in Foreign Direct Investment (FDI):
o FDI inflows increased dramatically, from around USD 133
million in 1991-92 to over USD 81 billion by 2022-23.
o This brought in advanced technology, managerial skills,
and capital, significantly boosting industries such as IT,
pharmaceuticals, and automobiles.
3. Expansion of the Services Sector:
o The services sector became the dominant contributor to
GDP, exceeding 50% by 2020.
o IT and software services, led by companies like TCS and
Infosys, turned India into a major global IT outsourcing
hub.
4. Trade Growth and Global Integration:
o Trade barriers were reduced; import tariffs fell from over
100% pre-1991 to around 30% in recent years.
o Exports expanded substantially, increasing India's share in
global trade.
5. Industrial and Private Sector Growth:
o The dismantling of the License Raj removed bureaucratic
hurdles, fostering entrepreneurship and competition.
o Privatization of public enterprises improved efficiency and
competitiveness in many sectors.
6. Employment and Income Effects:
o Increased investment led to job creation, especially in
urban and service sectors.
********** 11 **********
--- page 12 ---
o Rising per capita income improved living standards for
many, though income inequality and regional disparities
also increased.
7. Challenges:

o Environmental degradation and rising inequality called for
policy attention.
In summary, LPG reforms post-1991 dramatically transformed and
modernized the Indian economy, driving faster growth, global
integration, and increased competitiveness, while presenting new
socio-economic challenges to be managed
Q5   [20]
a.   Critically evaluate Elton Mayo’s contribution to management thought
through the Hawthorne experiments. How are these findings relevant
in   today’s   business   environment?
Elton Mayo’s contribution to management thought primarily stems
from his role in the Hawthorne experiments conducted at the Western
Electric Hawthorne Works in Chicago during the late 1920s to early
1930s. These experiments revolutionized understanding of worker
productivity and motivation by highlighting the significant influence
CO- 4; BL-   of social and psychological factors beyond just physical working
[10]
Understand & Apply
conditions or monetary incentives.
Contribution Through Hawthorne Experiments
• Shift from Mechanistic to Human Relations Approach:
Mayo challenged the classical scientific management view that
workers are motivated solely by wages and physical conditions.
Instead, he emphasized emotional and social needs.
• Importance of Social Groups: The experiments showed that
workers’ productivity improves when they feel socially
********** 12 **********
--- page 13 ---
accepted and part of a supportive group. Informal group norms
and peer relationships strongly influence work behaviors.
• Hawthorne Effect: Productivity increased simply because
workers knew they were being observed and felt attended to.
This highlighted that attention and acknowledgment can
motivate employees.
• Role of Management: The studies underscored the importance
of managerial skills related to interpersonal relationships,
communication, and employee welfare, not just technical
efficiency.
Relevance in Today’s Business Environment
• Employee Engagement and Motivation: Modern HR practices
focus heavily on social recognition, teamwork, and participative
management, echoing Mayo’s insights.
• Workplace Culture: The idea that positive social environments
boost productivity remains central, especially in team-based and
collaborative work settings.
• Leadership Skills: Emotional intelligence, empathy, and
communication are prioritized qualities in leaders, stemming
from human relations thinking.
• Remote and Hybrid Work Challenges: Though technology
changes work modalities, human needs for belonging,
recognition, and support persist and require deliberate
management strategies.
• Organizational Behavior and Psychology: Mayo laid the
foundation for these fields, which now help design workplaces
that consider employee well-being holistically.
In summary, Elton Mayo’s work through the Hawthorne experiments
significantly transformed management thought by highlighting the
human and social dimensions of work. This legacy continues to shape
effective management practices and organizational success today,
where human factors remain crucial amid evolving workplace
contexts
********** 13 **********
--- page 14 ---

This statement means that management’s primary function is

1. Strategic Decisions:

policies.

mergers.   [10]

o Made by top-level management.

planning.

3. Operational Decisions:

Why Motivation is Necessary

Issue of Motivation Explained with Maslow’s Hierarchy of Needs
Theory
Maslow’s theory suggests that human needs are arranged in a
hierarchy from basic to advanced levels:
1. Physiological Needs: Food, shelter, salary (basic survival).
2. Safety Needs: Job security, safe working conditions.
3. Social Needs: Belongingness, acceptance by peers and
managers.
4. Esteem Needs: Recognition, status, confidence.
5. Self-actualization Needs: Personal growth, achieving one’s
potential.
According to Maslow, employees must have lower-level needs
satisfied before they seek fulfillment of higher-level needs. Managers
should   tailor motivation   strategies to address these needs
sequentially.

and self-actualization needs.

b.   As a manager of a fast-food café and restaurant, you have observed

Suggested Inventory Control Techniques
1. First In, First Out (FIFO) Method:
o Use older stock items first to minimize spoilage and waste.
********** 20 **********
--- page 21 ---

used before new arrivals.

ingredients, dairy, and meats.

o Establish minimum inventory levels (par levels) for each
item to avoid both overstocking and stockouts.
o Automatically reorder ingredients or supplies when
inventory hits par, ensuring smooth operations without
excess holding costs.
3. Regular and Consistent Inventory Tracking:
o Conduct frequent cycle counts, focusing on high-value and
perishable items.
o Use the same trained staff to do inventory counts
consistently,   improving   accuracy   and   spotting
discrepancies early.
4. Inventory Categorization and Organization:
o Group inventory into categories (e.g., produce, dairy,
meats).
o Label shelves clearly to reduce counting errors and speed
up restocking and usage.
5. Use of Technology and POS Integration:
o Employ inventory management software compatible with
Point-of-Sale (POS) systems to track sales and inventory
in real time.

reduce manual errors.

o Maintain a food waste log to identify and minimize
spoilage or loss.
********** 21 **********
--- page 22 ---
o Optimize menu offerings by using surplus or near-expiry
ingredients creatively.

minimizing waste.

inventory verification, to reduce theft and errors.
Benefits

purchasing.
• Improved cash flow by minimizing tied-up capital in excess
inventory.
• Enhanced operational efficiency ensuring smooth preparation
and timely service.
Implementing these inventory control techniques will help the
restaurant manage costs more effectively and improve overall
profitability while maintaining high service standards.

Q2   (Answers should be related to as follows)   [20]
a.   Critically evaluate the various economic systems and discuss how the
problem of scarcity of resources is addressed in each system?
Ans: 1. Capitalist (Market) Economy
•   Resource Allocation: Uses the price mechanism—resources
are owned privately, and markets decide what, how and for whom to
produce based on supply and demand.
•   Scarcity Approach: Scarcity is addressed by rationing through
prices; only those able and willing to pay obtain resources.
Opportunity cost guides choices; the most valued uses attract
investment. Efficiency is high, but inequality and under-provision of
public goods may persist.
•   Critique: While innovation is encouraged, unequal resource
distribution can leave some needs unmet. Market failures like
monopolies or externalities diminish allocative efficiency.
2. Socialist (Planned/Command) Economy
•   Resource Allocation: The government owns most resources
and uses centralized plans to allocate them. Production and
distribution are determined by planners with social welfare as the
main goal.
•   Scarcity Approach: Scarcity is handled through rationing,
quota systems, and state prioritization; essential goods and services
are often guaranteed. Equal access is emphasized but with less
CO-1   flexibility for individual preferences.
BL- Understand and   •   Critique: Risk   of   inefficiency   and   misallocation— [10]
Apply   overproduction (gluts) or shortages occur if planners misjudge needs,
as real-time consumer choices are missing. Innovation can be stifled,
and black markets may emerge for unmet needs.
3. Mixed Economy
•   Resource Allocation: Combines private enterprise with
government regulation. The market handles most economic activity,
but the government intervenes for social welfare, public goods, and
correcting market failures.
•   Scarcity Approach: Scarcity is addressed through a blend:
prices guide many resource allocations, but laws, subsidies, and
public programs redistribute resources to address social welfare and
ensure minimum standards.
•   Critique: Tries to balance efficiency with equity. However,
government intervention can introduce distortions and red tape, while
unregulated sectors may retain some inefficiencies and inequality.
4. Traditional Economy
•   Resource Allocation: Relies on customs and traditions—
resources are allocated by established patterns, like farming or barter.
•   Scarcity Approach: Scarcity is addressed by subsistence
production and sharing; excess is rare and roles are fixed.
•   Critique: Stable but inflexible; little innovation or adaptation
to changing wants, and growth is limited.
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
b.   Assess the concept of price elasticity of demand in detail. Discuss the
factors   influencing   elasticity.
Ans. Price elasticity of demand (PED) measures how sensitive the
quantity demanded of a good   is to changes in   its price.
Mathematically, it is defined as:
% change in quantity demanded
PED =
% change in price
A result greater than 1 (in absolute value) indicates elastic demand —
quantity demanded changes more than proportionally to price
changes. If the value is less than 1, demand is inelastic — quantity
demanded changes less than the price. If PED is exactly 1, demand is
unit elastic. Most goods have a negative PED because of the inverse
relationship between price and quantity demanded, as per the law of
demand.[2][4][1]
Factors Influencing Price Elasticity
1. Availability of substitutes: If close substitutes exist, demand is
usually more elastic. Consumers can easily switch to alternatives
CO- 1; BL- Apply   [10]
if price rises.
2. Necessity vs. Luxury: Necessities tend to have inelastic
demand (consumers buy regardless of price), whereas luxuries
are more elastic.
3. Proportion of income: Goods taking up a larger share of the
consumer's income have more elastic demand, since price
changes have a bigger impact on budget.
4. Time horizon: Demand elasticity increases over time, as
consumers find alternatives or adjust behavior.
5. Addictiveness and brand loyalty: Addictive or strongly
branded goods usually have inelastic demand because buyers
continue purchasing even if prices rise.
6. Definition of the market: Broader categories (e.g., food) are
less elastic than narrow ones (e.g., specific ice cream brands).
In summary, price elasticity of demand reflects how consumers
respond to price changes and is shaped by substitutes, necessity,
income share, time, and consumer habits. It is central to business
pricing,   tax   policy,   and   economic   welfare   analysis.
Q3   [20]
a.
Compare and contrast cardinal utility and ordinal utility approaches.
Critically discuss their relevance in analyzing consumer behavior.
CO-2; BL- Understand
[10]
& Analyze   Ans. Cardinal Utility vs Ordinal Utility: Comparison and
Relevance
Cardinal utility is the approach in economics where satisfaction from
consuming goods is assigned numerical values ("utils"). It assumes
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
utility can be precisely measured—such as saying eating pizza gives
10 units of satisfaction, while ice cream gives 6 units—which allows
for direct comparison and calculation of marginal utility, total utility,
and how much more a consumer prefers one bundle over another. This
quantitative method is used in early microeconomic models, like
Marshall’s marginal utility analysis.
Ordinal utility, preferred in modern economics, rejects measurable
values and instead ranks preferences qualitatively. Here, consumers
only indicate order—"tea is preferred to coffee"—without quantifying
by how much. Ordinal utility forms the basis for indifference curve
analysis, which describes consumer equilibrium and responses to
price/income changes using preference rankings rather than
numbers.[2][3][4]
Critical discussion: While cardinal utility made early economic
modeling possible, its reliance on measuring subjective satisfaction
proved unrealistic—consumers rarely assign exact numbers to
happiness from goods. Ordinal utility, being based on rankings,
reflects actual decision-making and is more robust for analyzing
diverse consumer behavior. Most modern theories use ordinal utility
because it does not assume interpersonal or precise measurement of
satisfaction, just observable choices. Nevertheless, both approaches
enrich our understanding of consumption: cardinal utility highlights
concepts like diminishing marginal utility, and ordinal utility provides
tools for analyzing choices, market demand, and equilibrium using
indifference curves and budget constraints.
CO-2;   b.   Discuss the Law of Diminishing Returns. Explain the various stages
BL-   understand and   of   this law   with   the   help   of   a   table and   diagram.
Analyze
Ans. Law of Diminishing Returns: Explanation and Stages
The Law of Diminishing Returns states that when increasing units of
a variable input (e.g., labor) are added to fixed inputs (e.g., land or
capital), the total output initially increases at an increasing rate, then
at a decreasing rate, and eventually declines. This law operates only
in the short run when at least one input remains fixed.
Stages:
[10]
1. Increasing Returns: Total product (TP) rises at an increasing
rate as each added unit of variable input contributes more than
the previous one. Marginal product (MP) also increases. This
happens because fixed inputs are underutilized initially, leading
to better utilization and specialization.
2. Diminishing Returns: TP continues to rise but at a decreasing
rate; MP declines but remains positive. Due to fixed input
constraints, added variable units become less effective. This
stage is economically important as rational producers prefer to
operate here.
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
3. Negative Returns: TP decreases; MP becomes negative as
overcrowding and inefficiency set in from excessive variable
input.
Example Table:
Labor   Total Product   Marginal
Units   (TP)   Product (MP)
1   10   10
2   25   15
3   36   11
4   44   8
5   48   4
6   48   0
7   45   -3
Diagram: The total product curve rises steeply in Stage 1, flattens in
Stage 2, and declines in Stage 3. Marginal product curve peaks early,
then declines, crossing zero at the maximum TP point.
This law guides producers to optimize input usage and avoid
inefficiencies caused by overuse   of   variable   inputs.[1][2][3]
Q4   [20]
a.   Using diagrams, explain how price and output are determined in a
perfectly competitive market. Discuss all the features of the Perfect
Competition   Structure   and   its   example.
Ans: In a perfectly competitive market, price and output are
determined where the industry supply and demand curves intersect,
establishing the equilibrium market price (P1). Individual firms, being
price takers, face a horizontal demand curve at this price. Each firm
CO- 3; BL-   maximizes profit by producing where marginal cost (MC) equals
marginal revenue (MR), which, in perfect competition, equals the [10]
Understand & Apply
market price. This point determines the firm's optimal output (Q1).
The diagram shows the industry supply and demand curves setting the
equilibrium price, with firms’ MC curves intersecting MR at Q1. The
features of perfect competition include many firms selling
homogeneous products, free entry and exit, perfect knowledge, and
no market power for any single firm. Examples include markets for
agricultural products like wheat or vegetables, where numerous
sellers produce identical goods and prices are dictated by overall
supply and demand.
b.   Discuss the main components of the New Economic Policy (1991) in
CO-3; BL- Remember   India. How did those components together transform the Indian
economy?   [10]
and Apply
Ans: The New Economic Policy (NEP) of 1991 in India was a
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
landmark shift that aimed to transform a struggling, inward-looking
economy into a vibrant, market-oriented, and globally integrated one.
The   policy’s   three core components were   Liberalization,
Privatization, and Globalization (LPG), each bringing substantial
reform and long-term economic change.[1][2]
Main Components
• Liberalization: The government reduced its direct control over
industries by abolishing licensing requirements for most sectors,
removing trade barriers, reducing regulations, and opening up to
foreign goods and capital. Financial sector reforms allowed the
entry of private and foreign banks, deregulated interest rates,
and improved monetary policy transparency.[3][1]
• Privatization: The public sector’s dominant role was reduced
by disinvesting state enterprises, allowing private sector entry
into key industries, and making public sector firms more
competitive. Monopoly laws were relaxed to encourage growth
and efficiency.[4][1][3]
• Globalization: India integrated with the global economy by
promoting exports, encouraging foreign direct investment (FDI),
easing import restrictions, and linking domestic policies to
international standards. The rupee was devalued to boost
exports, and investment approvals for foreign companies were
streamlined.[2][5][1]
Transformative Impacts on the Indian Economy
These reforms produced deep and wide-ranging effects. Economic
growth accelerated, with GDP growth rates rising to 6–7% per annum
in the decades that followed, sometimes reaching double digits.
Foreign investment surged—from just $97 million in 1991 to over $81
billion by 2024—bringing in modern technology, managerial know-
how, and global integration.[6][1]
The private sector became a major driver in industries like IT,
banking, telecom, and manufacturing. Foreign exchange reserves
grew dramatically, providing stability and confidence. Poverty levels
declined as rapid growth created jobs and raised living standards,
although the gap between rich and poor also widened. India became a
key global economic player, joining organizations like the WTO and
G20, increasing exports, and negotiating free trade agreements.[7][1]
In sum, the 1991 NEP broke old barriers, energized productivity, and
converted India into a dynamic, globally competitive economy by
combining the strengths of liberalized markets, private enterprise, and
global   engagement.[1][2][6]
Q5   [20]
CO- 4; BL- Understand   a.   Discuss the contributions of F.W. Taylor and Henry Fayol to the
[10]
& Apply   development of management thought. Compare their approaches with
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
suitable   examples.
Ans: F.W. Taylor and Henry Fayol are foundational figures in
management thought, each shaping the discipline with distinct yet
complementary approaches.
Taylor's   Contributions   (Scientific   Management):
F.W. Taylor, known as the 'Father of Scientific Management,' focused
on improving efficiency at the shop-floor level. His main principles
included: using scientific methods to analyze work rather than relying
on rule-of-thumb, selecting and training workers scientifically,
fostering cooperation between management and workers, and
implementing differential piece-rate wage systems. Taylor introduced
"time and motion studies" to optimize task performance and believed
in splitting responsibilities—managers planned while workers
executed. For example, at Bethlehem Steel, his studies improved
shovel design, reducing waste and boosting output.[1][2][3]
Fayol's   Contributions   (Administrative   Theory):
Henry Fayol, regarded as the 'Father of Modern Management,' shifted
the focus to the organization as a whole. He set out 14 management
principles such as division of work, authority with responsibility,
unity of command, unity of direction, and centralization. Fayol
defined   five   managerial   functions:   planning,   organizing,
commanding, coordinating, and controlling, offering a top-down view
of management. His methods are universal, guiding managers in all
industries. For example, his "unity of command" principle ensures
each employee receives orders from only one superior, preventing
confusion.[4][5][6][7]
Comparison Table:
Aspect   F.W. Taylor   Henry Fayol
Focus   Shop-floor/Production   Top   Management/Whole
Level   Organization
Approac   Bottom-up,   Scientific,   Top-down,   Administrative,
h   Task-based   Process-based
Principle   Four main   (Scientific   Fourteen   (Administrative
s   management)   theory)
Methods   Observation,   Experience,   general
experimentation   guidelines
Wage   Differential   piece-rate   Profit sharing emphasized
System   system
Example   Optimizing   worker   Structuring departments, roles
tasks/machines
In essence, Taylor emphasized "how things are done efficiently,"
helping operational managers boost productivity, while Fayol stressed
"how things are managed and led," advising top managers on
administration. Both laid the groundwork for modern management—
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
a blend of scientific analysis and administrative best practices—
which   remains   relevant   across   industries   today.[8][9][10]
b.   Examine the key steps involved in the planning process. Why do many
organizational   plans   fail   despite   careful   preparation?
Ans: The planning process typically involves the following steps:
1. Setting Objectives: Clearly define what the organization aims
to achieve. Objectives should be specific, measurable,
achievable, relevant, and time-bound (SMART).[1][2][3]
2. Developing Planning Premises: Assess internal and external
factors likely to affect plans. This includes market trends,
resource availability, legal considerations, and competition.[4][1]
3. Identifying Alternative Courses of Action: Generate different
ways to reach objectives, considering both conventional and
innovative options.[5][1]
4. Evaluating Alternatives: Analyze the strengths, weaknesses,
advantages, and risks of each option to make an informed
decision.[1][4]
5. Selecting the Best Alternative: Choose the most appropriate
plan based on evaluation, balancing efficiency, feasibility, and
alignment with organizational goals.[6][1]
CO-4;   6. Formulating Derivative Plans: Develop detailed sub-plans for
BL- Apply   departments or teams to support the main plan. This ensures [10]
coordination across the organization.[4]
7. Implementation: Communicate the plan, allocate resources,
assign responsibilities, and initiate action.[7][1]
8. Monitoring and Follow-Up: Continually review progress,
compare results with objectives, and make adjustments as
necessary to stay on track.[5][4]
Why Organizational Plans Fail
Despite careful preparation, many organizational plans do not achieve
their intended results due to several reasons:
• Lack of Clarity: Ambiguous goals or poor communication lead
to confusion and misaligned efforts.[8]
• Poor Execution: Even well-made plans fail if implementation
is weak, resources are inadequate, or   staff are not
committed.[9][8]
• Resource Constraints: Insufficient finances, manpower,
technology, or time result in bottlenecks.[9]
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
• Resistance to Change: Employees or management may be
reluctant to accept new plans due to entrenched habits or fear of
loss.[10]
• Inadequate Monitoring: Failure to regularly review progress
and respond to setbacks causes strategies to drift off track.[8][9]
• External Environmental Changes:   Market   volatility,
regulatory shifts, or technological disruption can render plans
obsolete.[4]
• Unrealistic Objectives: Overly ambitious or poorly researched
goals set organizations up for disappointment.[9]
• Lack of Leadership Commitment: Without strong support
from top management, plans may lack authority and
momentum.[8]
In summary, successful organizational planning demands not only
careful preparation but also clear communication, resource allocation,
adaptability, consistent follow-through, and buy-in from all involved
stakeholders.The planning process in management involves several
systematic steps. First, organizations set clear objectives, defining
what they want to achieve in specific, measurable terms. Next, they
develop planning premises by assessing external and internal factors
likely to impact their goals, such as market trends and resource
availability. Third, they identify alternative courses of action,
generating different strategies to accomplish objectives. These
alternatives are then evaluated based on feasibility, costs, risks, and
expected outcomes. After this evaluation, decision-makers select the
most suitable plan and proceed to formulate derivative or supporting
plans for each department. The plan is implemented by assigning
resources, communicating roles, and executing tasks. Finally,
organizations monitor progress, comparing results against objectives
and making necessary adjustments to ensure success.[6][1][5][4][8][9]
Despite careful planning, many organizational plans fail. Key reasons
include a lack of clarity in goals or communication, which leads to
misalignment and confusion among employees. Poor execution can
undermine even the best plans, especially when resources are
insufficient or staff are not fully committed. Resistance to change is
common, as employees may fear disruption or feel attached to existing
routines. Plans can become outdated due to unexpected changes in the
external environment, such as new technologies or regulatory shifts.
Insufficient monitoring and follow-up also allow problems to go
unnoticed until it is too late to adjust course. Unrealistic expectations
and lack of top management support further contribute to
organizational plans falling short of their intended outcomes.[10][4][8][9]

Q1
Write a Short Note on: (Answers should be related to as follows)   [20]
a.   Scarcity of Resources and Production Possibility Curve
Scarcity of Resources:
Scarcity means that resources are limited, but human wants and
needs are unlimited. Because resources such as land, labor,
capital, and raw materials are finite, we cannot produce an
unlimited number of goods and services. This fundamental
economic problem forces societies to make choices about how to
allocate resources efficiently. Scarcity leads to trade-offs,
meaning if you use resources for one purpose, you can't use them
CO- 1; BL-   for another.
[5]
Understand   The PPC (also known as the Production Possibility Frontier, or
PPF) is a graph that illustrates the maximum possible output
combinations of two goods or services that an economy can
produce with its available resources and technology, assuming
full and efficient use.
Scarcity of Resources and PPC
•   Scarcity of resources limits the total production capacity,
which is represented by the PPC.
• The PPC illustrates the trade-offs and opportunity costs that
arise due to scarcity.
********** 1 **********
--- page 2 ---
•   It helps visualize the maximum output combinations and the
cost of reallocating resources.
b.   Properties   of   isoquant   curves.
Isoquants represent combinations of inputs producing the same
output. Key properties: (1) Downward sloping—trade-off between
inputs; (2) Convex to origin—diminishing marginal rate of technical
substitution (MRTS);   (3) Do not intersect—each isoquant
CO-2; BL-   corresponds to a unique output level; (4) Higher isoquants represent [5]
Understand
greater output; (5) Slope indicates MRTS between inputs.
Understanding isoquants helps firms optimize input usage and
production planning under cost constraints. They are fundamental in
production theory, demonstrating efficient input substitution to
maintain output.
c.   Distinguish   between   perfect   and   imperfect   competition.
Perfect competition features many buyers and sellers with identical
products, free entry and exit, perfect information, and price takers.
Prices are determined by market forces without individual influence.
Imperfect   competition   includes monopoly,   oligopoly,   and
CO-3; BL-   monopolistic competition where firms have market power to set [5]
Understand
prices, product differentiation exists, and barriers to entry prevail.
Imperfect competition leads to price discrimination, restricted output,
and economic inefficiency, unlike the allocative efficiency and
optimal resource distribution in perfect competition.
d.   Nature   of   Management|
CO- 4; BL-   Management is a systematic process of planning, organizing, leading, [5]
Understand
and controlling resources to achieve organizational goals effectively
and efficiently. It is a universal, continuous, dynamic, and goal-
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
oriented activity involving both art and science. Management
balances human, financial, and physical resources while adapting to
changing environments.   It requires skills in decision-making,
communication, coordination, and leadership. Management integrates
individual efforts toward collective success in diverse organizations.
Q2   (Answers should be related to as follows)   [20]
a.   Explain with diagrams how an equilibrium in demand and supply is
established in a market. Analyze the impact of shifts in demand and
supply   on   the   equilibrium   price   and   quantity.
Market equilibrium occurs where the quantity demanded equals the
quantity supplied, establishing a stable price and quantity in the
market. Shifts in demand or supply alter this equilibrium, leading to
new price and quantity outcomes (see the generated image above).
Market Equilibrium
Market equilibrium is achieved at the point where the demand curve
intersects the supply curve, indicating the price at which the quantity
of a good consumers are willing to buy equals the quantity producers
CO-1
are willing to sell. At this point, there is no surplus or shortage in the
BL- Understand and   [10]
Apply   market, and the price tends to remain stable unless external factors
intervene. The equilibrium price and quantity are determined by the
interaction of market forces under given conditions of consumer
preferences, income levels, production costs, and technology (see the
generated image above).
(see the generated image above)
Impact of Demand Shifts
When demand increases due to factors such as higher consumer
income or changing preferences, the demand curve shifts to the right,
leading to a higher equilibrium price and quantity. Conversely, a
decrease in demand shifts the curve to the left, resulting in a lower
equilibrium price and quantity. These shifts illustrate how changes in
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
non-price determinants of demand affect market outcomes even when
supply remains constant (see the generated image above).
• Increase in demand: higher equilibrium price and quantity (see
the generated image above)
• Decrease in demand: lower equilibrium price and quantity
Impact of Supply Shifts
An increase in supply, caused by technological advancements or
lower input costs, shifts the supply curve to the right, resulting in a
lower equilibrium price and a higher equilibrium quantity. A decrease
in supply, such as from resource scarcity or higher production costs,
shifts the curve to the left, leading to a higher price and reduced
quantity. These movements demonstrate how supply-side factors
independently influence market equilibrium .
• Increase in supply: lower price, higher quantity
• Decrease in supply: higher price, lower quantity
Simultaneous Shifts in Demand and Supply
When both demand and supply shift simultaneously, the effect on
equilibrium price and quantity depends on the relative magnitude and
direction of the shifts. If both curves shift rightward (increase),
quantity rises, but the price change is indeterminate without knowing
which shift is stronger. Similarly, opposing shifts can lead to clear
outcomes for price or quantity, while the other variable's direction
remains uncertain. This complexity underscores the importance of
analyzing both curves dynamically in real-world markets .
b.   Illustrate the law of diminishing marginal utility with real-life
examples   and   graphical   representations.
CO- 2; BL-
Understand and   [10]
The Law of Diminishing Marginal Utility states that as a consumer
Apply
consumes more units of a good or service, the additional satisfaction
(marginal utility) gained from each subsequent unit decreases.
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
Eventually, consuming more units may lead to zero or even negative
marginal utility.
Real-Life Examples
1. Eating Pizza Slices: The first slice of pizza when you are very
hungry provides high satisfaction. The second slice still adds
enjoyment but less than the first. The third or fourth slice adds
even less satisfaction, and by the fifth slice you might feel full
or even uncomfortable, resulting in zero or negative utility.
2. Drinking Water When Thirsty: The first sips quench your
thirst greatly, providing high utility. As you drink more, the
satisfaction decreases until drinking more is not pleasurable.
3. Purchasing the Same Product: Buying one new smartphone
gives high satisfaction, but buying a second identical phone
soon after adds little value; the marginal utility is low or
negative.
Graphical Representation
• The X-axis represents the quantity consumed.
• The Y-axis represents the marginal utility from each unit.
• The marginal utility curve slopes downward, reflecting
diminishing additional satisfaction.
The   curve   may   cross   the   X-axis   at   the
Q3   [20]
a.   Explain how consumer equilibrium is achieved using indifference
curve   analysis.
Consumer equilibrium occurs when a consumer maximizes
CO-2; BL-   satisfaction (utility) from the consumption of two goods, given their [10]
Understand & Apply
income and the prices of these goods. This can be analyzed through
indifference curves and the budget line.
Key Concepts:
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
• An indifference curve represents combinations of two goods
that provide equal satisfaction to the consumer.
• The budget line shows all possible combinations of the two
goods that a consumer can afford with a given income and
prices.
• The Marginal Rate of Substitution (MRS) is the rate at which
a consumer is willing to substitute one good for another while
maintaining the same level of satisfaction (the slope of the
indifference curve).
• The slope of the budget line equals the ratio of the prices of the
two   goods   (
$ \frac{P_X}{P_Y} $).
Conditions for Consumer Equilibrium
1. Slope   Equality   (Tangency   Condition):
$ MRS_{XY} = \frac{MU_X}{MU_Y} = \frac{P_X}{P_Y} $
This means the rate at which the consumer is willing to trade
one good for another equals the market rate at which trade must
happen.
2. Convexity   and   Diminishing   MRS:
The indifference curve must be convex to the origin (reflecting
diminishing MRS), ensuring a unique equilibrium point.
Graphical Representation
• The consumer equilibrium is at the point where the budget line
is tangent to the highest attainable indifference curve.
• At this point, the consumer achieves maximum utility without
exceeding their budget.
• Points inside the budget line are affordable but do not maximize
utility.
• Points outside the budget line provide higher utility but are
unaffordable.
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
Intuition
•   If $ MRS_{XY} > \frac{P_X}{P_Y} $, the consumer values
Good X more relatively and will buy more of it, reducing MRS.
•   If $ MRS_{XY} < \frac{P_X}{P_Y} $, the consumer values
Good Y relatively more and will buy more of Good Y,
increasing MRS.
• This adjustment continues until the equality condition holds,
achieving equilibrium.
In summary, the consumer selects the optimal bundle of goods at the
tangency point, maximizing satisfaction given budget constraints.
CO-2;   b. Why Average Cost (AC) curve is ‘U’ shape? Explain the relationship
BL- Understand and   between AC and MC curves in the Short Run with the help of a
Analyze
diagram.
The short-run Average Cost (AC) curve is U-shaped due to the
combined effects of Average Fixed Costs (AFC) and Average
Variable Costs (AVC) as output changes:
• At low output levels: AC is high because fixed costs are spread
over a small quantity, making AFC per unit large. AVC can also
be high due to inefficiencies from underutilization of resources.
• As output increases: AFC declines sharply since fixed costs [10]
spread over more units. AVC also falls initially due to increasing
returns to the variable factor and better utilization of fixed
resources, causing AC to decline.
• Beyond a certain output (the optimum scale): Diminishing
returns set in, making AVC rise sharply as additional units
become more costly to produce. This increase in AVC
outweighs the continuing small decline in AFC, causing AC to
rise again.
Thus, the AC curve falls, reaches a minimum, and then rises —
making it U-shaped. This behavior is explained by the Law of
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
Variable Proportions and the transition from economies of scale to
diseconomies of scale.
Relationship Between Average Cost (AC) and Marginal Cost
(MC) Curves
• The Marginal Cost (MC) curve shows the cost of producing
one additional unit of output.
• When MC is less than AC, producing an additional unit
decreases the AC, so AC falls.
• When MC is greater than AC, producing an additional unit
increases the AC, so AC rises.
• When MC equals AC, AC is at its minimum point, and the MC
curve intersects the AC curve at this point.
Graphical Summary
• The MC curve is also U-shaped due to diminishing marginal
returns.
• The MC curve cuts the AC curve at the lowest point of the AC
curve.
In summary, the U-shaped AC curve reflects initial economies and
later diseconomies of scale in production, while the MC curve guides
the direction of the AC curve based on whether it lies below or above
the   AC   at   any   output   level.
Q4   [20]
a.
Compare and contrast Monopoly and Oligopoly. Give suitable real-
world   examples   to   support   your   answer.
CO- 3; BL-
[10]
Understand & Apply
Monopoly
• Number of Sellers: A single seller dominates the entire market.
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
• Product Type: Unique product with no close substitutes.
• Market Power: Seller is a price maker with significant control
over price and quantity.
• Competition: Absence of competition.
• Barriers to Entry: Very high, due to legal, economic, or
institutional factors.
• Price Level: Typically higher because of the lack of
competition.
• Consumer Choice: Limited, as only one supplier exists.
Example:
• Public utilities like electricity supply by regional providers.
• U.S. Postal Service’s monopoly on first-class mail delivery.
Oligopoly
• Number of Sellers: A few large firms dominate the market
(usually 2 to 10).
• Product Type: Products can be homogeneous (e.g., steel) or
differentiated (e.g., automobiles).
• Market Power: Firms have some control over price but are
mutually interdependent.
• Competition: Exists but limited; firms consider rivals’ actions.
• Barriers to Entry: High, often due to economies of scale and
capital requirements.
• Price Level: Moderate; price rigidity and occasional price wars
or collusion occur.
• Consumer Choice: Greater than monopoly but limited
compared to perfect competition.
Example:
• Automobile industry (e.g., Toyota, Ford, Hyundai).
• Smartphone manufacturers (e.g., Apple, Samsung, OnePlus).
********** 9 **********
--- page 10 ---
Key Differences
Feature   Monopoly   Oligopoly
Sellers   Single   Few (2-10)
Product   Unique   Homogeneous   or
differentiated products
Market   Full   control   Shared,   interdependent
Control   over price   pricing
Competition   None   Some, with strategic behavior
Entry   Very High   High
Barriers
Pricing   Price maker   Price rigidity and strategic
pricing
Consumer   Limited   More   options   but   still
Choice   restricted
Understanding these differences helps explain market behaviors like
pricing, output decisions, and consumer impact in different industries.

The Liberalization, Privatization, and Globalization (LPG) reforms

open, market-driven economy integrated with the global market.   [10]

o This rapid growth was driven by increased efficiency,

2. Increase in Foreign Direct Investment (FDI):
o FDI inflows increased dramatically, from around USD 133
million in 1991-92 to over USD 81 billion by 2022-23.

and capital, significantly boosting industries such as IT,
pharmaceuticals, and automobiles.
3. Expansion of the Services Sector:
o The services sector became the dominant contributor to
GDP, exceeding 50% by 2020.
o IT and software services, led by companies like TCS and
Infosys, turned India into a major global IT outsourcing
hub.
4. Trade Growth and Global Integration:
o Trade barriers were reduced; import tariffs fell from over
100% pre-1991 to around 30% in recent years.
o Exports expanded substantially, increasing India's share in
global trade.
5. Industrial and Private Sector Growth:
o The dismantling of the License Raj removed bureaucratic
hurdles, fostering entrepreneurship and competition.
o Privatization of public enterprises improved efficiency and
competitiveness in many sectors.
6. Employment and Income Effects:
o Increased investment led to job creation, especially in
urban and service sectors.
********** 11 **********
--- page 12 ---
o Rising per capita income improved living standards for
many, though income inequality and regional disparities
also increased.
7. Challenges:
o Some sectors like agriculture saw slower growth,
impacting rural livelihoods.
o Greater exposure to global markets introduced economic
volatility.
o Environmental degradation and rising inequality called for
policy attention.
In summary, LPG reforms post-1991 dramatically transformed and
modernized the Indian economy, driving faster growth, global
integration, and increased competitiveness, while presenting new
socio-economic challenges to be managed
Q5   [20]
a.   Critically evaluate Elton Mayo’s contribution to management thought
through the Hawthorne experiments. How are these findings relevant
in   today’s   business   environment?
Elton Mayo’s contribution to management thought primarily stems
from his role in the Hawthorne experiments conducted at the Western
Electric Hawthorne Works in Chicago during the late 1920s to early
1930s. These experiments revolutionized understanding of worker
productivity and motivation by highlighting the significant influence
CO- 4; BL-   of social and psychological factors beyond just physical working
[10]
Understand & Apply
conditions or monetary incentives.
Contribution Through Hawthorne Experiments
• Shift from Mechanistic to Human Relations Approach:
Mayo challenged the classical scientific management view that
workers are motivated solely by wages and physical conditions.
Instead, he emphasized emotional and social needs.
• Importance of Social Groups: The experiments showed that
workers’ productivity improves when they feel socially
********** 12 **********
--- page 13 ---
accepted and part of a supportive group. Informal group norms
and peer relationships strongly influence work behaviors.
• Hawthorne Effect: Productivity increased simply because
workers knew they were being observed and felt attended to.
This highlighted that attention and acknowledgment can
motivate employees.
• Role of Management: The studies underscored the importance
of managerial skills related to interpersonal relationships,
communication, and employee welfare, not just technical
efficiency.
Relevance in Today’s Business Environment
• Employee Engagement and Motivation: Modern HR practices
focus heavily on social recognition, teamwork, and participative
management, echoing Mayo’s insights.
• Workplace Culture: The idea that positive social environments
boost productivity remains central, especially in team-based and
collaborative work settings.
• Leadership Skills: Emotional intelligence, empathy, and
communication are prioritized qualities in leaders, stemming
from human relations thinking.
• Remote and Hybrid Work Challenges: Though technology
changes work modalities, human needs for belonging,
recognition, and support persist and require deliberate
management strategies.
• Organizational Behavior and Psychology: Mayo laid the
foundation for these fields, which now help design workplaces
that consider employee well-being holistically.
In summary, Elton Mayo’s work through the Hawthorne experiments
significantly transformed management thought by highlighting the
human and social dimensions of work. This legacy continues to shape
effective management practices and organizational success today,
where human factors remain crucial amid evolving workplace
contexts
********** 13 **********
--- page 14 ---

This statement means that management’s primary function is

1. Strategic Decisions:

policies.

mergers.   [10]

o Examples: marketing campaigns, budgeting, workforce
planning.
o Made by middle management.
3. Operational Decisions:
o Short-term and routine.

In summary, managerial decision-making at every level is critical for

Why Motivation is Necessary

• Retention and Loyalty: Motivation reduces turnover and builds

Issue of Motivation Explained with Maslow’s Hierarchy of Needs
Theory
Maslow’s theory suggests that human needs are arranged in a
hierarchy from basic to advanced levels:
1. Physiological Needs: Food, shelter, salary (basic survival).
2. Safety Needs: Job security, safe working conditions.
3. Social Needs: Belongingness, acceptance by peers and
managers.
4. Esteem Needs: Recognition, status, confidence.
5. Self-actualization Needs: Personal growth, achieving one’s
potential.
According to Maslow, employees must have lower-level needs
satisfied before they seek fulfillment of higher-level needs. Managers
should   tailor motivation   strategies to address these needs
sequentially.
• For example, fair pay and job security meet basic needs.
• Promoting teamwork and healthy work culture meet social
needs.
• Providing recognition and career development addresses esteem
and self-actualization needs.

Even in economies where work is a necessity, motivation is crucial
for deriving optimal efforts from employees. Understanding and

b.   As a manager of a fast-food café and restaurant, you have observed

Suggested Inventory Control Techniques
1. First In, First Out (FIFO) Method:
o Use older stock items first to minimize spoilage and waste.
********** 20 **********

used before new arrivals.

item to avoid both overstocking and stockouts.
o Automatically reorder ingredients or supplies when
inventory hits par, ensuring smooth operations without
excess holding costs.
3. Regular and Consistent Inventory Tracking:
o Conduct frequent cycle counts, focusing on high-value and
perishable items.
o Use the same trained staff to do inventory counts
consistently,   improving   accuracy   and   spotting
discrepancies early.
4. Inventory Categorization and Organization:
o Group inventory into categories (e.g., produce, dairy,
meats).
o Label shelves clearly to reduce counting errors and speed
up restocking and usage.
5. Use of Technology and POS Integration:
o Employ inventory management software compatible with
Point-of-Sale (POS) systems to track sales and inventory
in real time.

reduce manual errors.

spoilage or loss.
********** 21 **********
--- page 22 ---
o Optimize menu offerings by using surplus or near-expiry
ingredients creatively.

inventory verification, to reduce theft and errors.
Benefits

purchasing.
• Improved cash flow by minimizing tied-up capital in excess
inventory.
• Enhanced operational efficiency ensuring smooth preparation
and timely service.
Implementing these inventory control techniques will help the
restaurant manage costs more effectively and improve overall
