Q1
Write a Short Note on: (Answers should be related to as follows)   [20]
a.   Scarcity of Resources and Production Possibility Curve
Scarcity of Resources:
Scarcity means that resources are limited, but human wants and
needs are unlimited. Because resources such as land, labor,
capital, and raw materials are finite, we cannot produce an
unlimited number of goods and services. This fundamental
economic problem forces societies to make choices about how to
allocate resources efficiently. Scarcity leads to trade-offs,
meaning if you use resources for one purpose, you can't use them
CO- 1; BL-   for another.
[5]
Understand   The PPC (also known as the Production Possibility Frontier, or
PPF) is a graph that illustrates the maximum possible output
combinations of two goods or services that an economy can
produce with its available resources and technology, assuming
full and efficient use.
Scarcity of Resources and PPC
•   Scarcity of resources limits the total production capacity,
which is represented by the PPC.
• The PPC illustrates the trade-offs and opportunity costs that
arise due to scarcity.
********** 1 **********
--- page 2 ---
•   It helps visualize the maximum output combinations and the
cost of reallocating resources.
b.   Properties   of   isoquant   curves.
Isoquants represent combinations of inputs producing the same
output. Key properties: (1) Downward sloping—trade-off between
inputs; (2) Convex to origin—diminishing marginal rate of technical
substitution (MRTS);   (3) Do not intersect—each isoquant
CO-2; BL-   corresponds to a unique output level; (4) Higher isoquants represent [5]
Understand
greater output; (5) Slope indicates MRTS between inputs.
Understanding isoquants helps firms optimize input usage and
production planning under cost constraints. They are fundamental in
production theory, demonstrating efficient input substitution to
maintain output.
c.   Distinguish   between   perfect   and   imperfect   competition.
Perfect competition features many buyers and sellers with identical
products, free entry and exit, perfect information, and price takers.
Prices are determined by market forces without individual influence.
Imperfect   competition   includes monopoly,   oligopoly,   and
CO-3; BL-   monopolistic competition where firms have market power to set [5]
Understand
prices, product differentiation exists, and barriers to entry prevail.
Imperfect competition leads to price discrimination, restricted output,
and economic inefficiency, unlike the allocative efficiency and
optimal resource distribution in perfect competition.
d.   Nature   of   Management|
CO- 4; BL-   Management is a systematic process of planning, organizing, leading, [5]
Understand
and controlling resources to achieve organizational goals effectively
and efficiently. It is a universal, continuous, dynamic, and goal-
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
oriented activity involving both art and science. Management
balances human, financial, and physical resources while adapting to
changing environments.   It requires skills in decision-making,
communication, coordination, and leadership. Management integrates
individual efforts toward collective success in diverse organizations.
Q2   (Answers should be related to as follows)   [20]
a.   Explain with diagrams how an equilibrium in demand and supply is
established in a market. Analyze the impact of shifts in demand and
supply   on   the   equilibrium   price   and   quantity.
Market equilibrium occurs where the quantity demanded equals the
quantity supplied, establishing a stable price and quantity in the
market. Shifts in demand or supply alter this equilibrium, leading to
new price and quantity outcomes (see the generated image above).
Market Equilibrium
Market equilibrium is achieved at the point where the demand curve
intersects the supply curve, indicating the price at which the quantity
of a good consumers are willing to buy equals the quantity producers
CO-1
are willing to sell. At this point, there is no surplus or shortage in the
BL- Understand and   [10]
Apply   market, and the price tends to remain stable unless external factors
intervene. The equilibrium price and quantity are determined by the
interaction of market forces under given conditions of consumer
preferences, income levels, production costs, and technology (see the
generated image above).
(see the generated image above)
Impact of Demand Shifts
When demand increases due to factors such as higher consumer
income or changing preferences, the demand curve shifts to the right,
leading to a higher equilibrium price and quantity. Conversely, a
decrease in demand shifts the curve to the left, resulting in a lower
equilibrium price and quantity. These shifts illustrate how changes in
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
non-price determinants of demand affect market outcomes even when
supply remains constant (see the generated image above).
• Increase in demand: higher equilibrium price and quantity (see
the generated image above)
• Decrease in demand: lower equilibrium price and quantity
Impact of Supply Shifts
An increase in supply, caused by technological advancements or
lower input costs, shifts the supply curve to the right, resulting in a
lower equilibrium price and a higher equilibrium quantity. A decrease
in supply, such as from resource scarcity or higher production costs,
shifts the curve to the left, leading to a higher price and reduced
quantity. These movements demonstrate how supply-side factors
independently influence market equilibrium .
• Increase in supply: lower price, higher quantity
• Decrease in supply: higher price, lower quantity
Simultaneous Shifts in Demand and Supply
When both demand and supply shift simultaneously, the effect on
equilibrium price and quantity depends on the relative magnitude and
direction of the shifts. If both curves shift rightward (increase),
quantity rises, but the price change is indeterminate without knowing
which shift is stronger. Similarly, opposing shifts can lead to clear
outcomes for price or quantity, while the other variable's direction
remains uncertain. This complexity underscores the importance of
analyzing both curves dynamically in real-world markets .
b.   Illustrate the law of diminishing marginal utility with real-life
examples   and   graphical   representations.
CO- 2; BL-
Understand and   [10]
The Law of Diminishing Marginal Utility states that as a consumer
Apply
consumes more units of a good or service, the additional satisfaction
(marginal utility) gained from each subsequent unit decreases.
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
Eventually, consuming more units may lead to zero or even negative
marginal utility.
Real-Life Examples
1. Eating Pizza Slices: The first slice of pizza when you are very
hungry provides high satisfaction. The second slice still adds
enjoyment but less than the first. The third or fourth slice adds
even less satisfaction, and by the fifth slice you might feel full
or even uncomfortable, resulting in zero or negative utility.
2. Drinking Water When Thirsty: The first sips quench your
thirst greatly, providing high utility. As you drink more, the
satisfaction decreases until drinking more is not pleasurable.
3. Purchasing the Same Product: Buying one new smartphone
gives high satisfaction, but buying a second identical phone
soon after adds little value; the marginal utility is low or
negative.
Graphical Representation
• The X-axis represents the quantity consumed.
• The Y-axis represents the marginal utility from each unit.
• The marginal utility curve slopes downward, reflecting
diminishing additional satisfaction.
The   curve   may   cross   the   X-axis   at   the
Q3   [20]
a.   Explain how consumer equilibrium is achieved using indifference
curve   analysis.
Consumer equilibrium occurs when a consumer maximizes
CO-2; BL-   satisfaction (utility) from the consumption of two goods, given their [10]
Understand & Apply
income and the prices of these goods. This can be analyzed through
indifference curves and the budget line.
Key Concepts:
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
• An indifference curve represents combinations of two goods
that provide equal satisfaction to the consumer.
• The budget line shows all possible combinations of the two
goods that a consumer can afford with a given income and
prices.
• The Marginal Rate of Substitution (MRS) is the rate at which
a consumer is willing to substitute one good for another while
maintaining the same level of satisfaction (the slope of the
indifference curve).
• The slope of the budget line equals the ratio of the prices of the
two   goods   (
$ \frac{P_X}{P_Y} $).
Conditions for Consumer Equilibrium
1. Slope   Equality   (Tangency   Condition):
$ MRS_{XY} = \frac{MU_X}{MU_Y} = \frac{P_X}{P_Y} $
This means the rate at which the consumer is willing to trade
one good for another equals the market rate at which trade must
happen.
2. Convexity   and   Diminishing   MRS:
The indifference curve must be convex to the origin (reflecting
diminishing MRS), ensuring a unique equilibrium point.
Graphical Representation
• The consumer equilibrium is at the point where the budget line
is tangent to the highest attainable indifference curve.
• At this point, the consumer achieves maximum utility without
exceeding their budget.
• Points inside the budget line are affordable but do not maximize
utility.
• Points outside the budget line provide higher utility but are
unaffordable.
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
Intuition
•   If $ MRS_{XY} > \frac{P_X}{P_Y} $, the consumer values
Good X more relatively and will buy more of it, reducing MRS.
•   If $ MRS_{XY} < \frac{P_X}{P_Y} $, the consumer values
Good Y relatively more and will buy more of Good Y,
increasing MRS.
• This adjustment continues until the equality condition holds,
achieving equilibrium.
In summary, the consumer selects the optimal bundle of goods at the
tangency point, maximizing satisfaction given budget constraints.
CO-2;   b. Why Average Cost (AC) curve is ‘U’ shape? Explain the relationship
BL- Understand and   between AC and MC curves in the Short Run with the help of a
Analyze
diagram.
The short-run Average Cost (AC) curve is U-shaped due to the
combined effects of Average Fixed Costs (AFC) and Average
Variable Costs (AVC) as output changes:
• At low output levels: AC is high because fixed costs are spread
over a small quantity, making AFC per unit large. AVC can also
be high due to inefficiencies from underutilization of resources.
• As output increases: AFC declines sharply since fixed costs [10]
spread over more units. AVC also falls initially due to increasing
returns to the variable factor and better utilization of fixed
resources, causing AC to decline.
• Beyond a certain output (the optimum scale): Diminishing
returns set in, making AVC rise sharply as additional units
become more costly to produce. This increase in AVC
outweighs the continuing small decline in AFC, causing AC to
rise again.
Thus, the AC curve falls, reaches a minimum, and then rises —
making it U-shaped. This behavior is explained by the Law of
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
Variable Proportions and the transition from economies of scale to
diseconomies of scale.
Relationship Between Average Cost (AC) and Marginal Cost
(MC) Curves
• The Marginal Cost (MC) curve shows the cost of producing
one additional unit of output.
• When MC is less than AC, producing an additional unit
decreases the AC, so AC falls.
• When MC is greater than AC, producing an additional unit
increases the AC, so AC rises.
• When MC equals AC, AC is at its minimum point, and the MC
curve intersects the AC curve at this point.
Graphical Summary
• The MC curve is also U-shaped due to diminishing marginal
returns.
• The MC curve cuts the AC curve at the lowest point of the AC
curve.
In summary, the U-shaped AC curve reflects initial economies and
later diseconomies of scale in production, while the MC curve guides
the direction of the AC curve based on whether it lies below or above
the   AC   at   any   output   level.
Q4   [20]
a.
Compare and contrast Monopoly and Oligopoly. Give suitable real-
world   examples   to   support   your   answer.
CO- 3; BL-
[10]
Understand & Apply
Monopoly
• Number of Sellers: A single seller dominates the entire market.
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
• Product Type: Unique product with no close substitutes.
• Market Power: Seller is a price maker with significant control
over price and quantity.
• Competition: Absence of competition.
• Barriers to Entry: Very high, due to legal, economic, or
institutional factors.
• Price Level: Typically higher because of the lack of
competition.
• Consumer Choice: Limited, as only one supplier exists.
Example:
• Public utilities like electricity supply by regional providers.
• U.S. Postal Service’s monopoly on first-class mail delivery.
Oligopoly
• Number of Sellers: A few large firms dominate the market
(usually 2 to 10).
• Product Type: Products can be homogeneous (e.g., steel) or
differentiated (e.g., automobiles).
• Market Power: Firms have some control over price but are
mutually interdependent.
• Competition: Exists but limited; firms consider rivals’ actions.
• Barriers to Entry: High, often due to economies of scale and
capital requirements.
• Price Level: Moderate; price rigidity and occasional price wars
or collusion occur.
• Consumer Choice: Greater than monopoly but limited
compared to perfect competition.
Example:
• Automobile industry (e.g., Toyota, Ford, Hyundai).
• Smartphone manufacturers (e.g., Apple, Samsung, OnePlus).
********** 9 **********
--- page 10 ---
Key Differences
Feature   Monopoly   Oligopoly
Sellers   Single   Few (2-10)
Product   Unique   Homogeneous   or
differentiated products
Market   Full   control   Shared,   interdependent
Control   over price   pricing
Competition   None   Some, with strategic behavior
Entry   Very High   High
Barriers
Pricing   Price maker   Price rigidity and strategic
pricing
Consumer   Limited   More   options   but   still
Choice   restricted
Understanding these differences helps explain market behaviors like
pricing, output decisions, and consumer impact in different industries.
b.   Explain the impact of LPG on the growth of the Indian Economy after
1991.
The Liberalization, Privatization, and Globalization (LPG) reforms
initiated in 1991 brought a transformative impact on the Indian
economy by shifting it from a closed, state-controlled system to an
CO-3; BL-
open, market-driven economy integrated with the global market.   [10]
Remember & Apply
Key Impacts of LPG on India's Economic Growth:
1. Acceleration of GDP Growth:
o Before 1991, India’s GDP growth averaged around 3.5%
annually, known as the “Hindu rate of growth.”
********** 10 **********
--- page 11 ---
o Post-reforms, growth accelerated to an average of 6.5%
annually between 1991 and 2010, with peaks around 8.5%
in the mid-2000s.
o This rapid growth was driven by increased efficiency,
competition, and private sector development.
2. Increase in Foreign Direct Investment (FDI):
o FDI inflows increased dramatically, from around USD 133
million in 1991-92 to over USD 81 billion by 2022-23.
o This brought in advanced technology, managerial skills,
and capital, significantly boosting industries such as IT,
pharmaceuticals, and automobiles.
3. Expansion of the Services Sector:
o The services sector became the dominant contributor to
GDP, exceeding 50% by 2020.
o IT and software services, led by companies like TCS and
Infosys, turned India into a major global IT outsourcing
hub.
4. Trade Growth and Global Integration:
o Trade barriers were reduced; import tariffs fell from over
100% pre-1991 to around 30% in recent years.
o Exports expanded substantially, increasing India's share in
global trade.
5. Industrial and Private Sector Growth:
o The dismantling of the License Raj removed bureaucratic
hurdles, fostering entrepreneurship and competition.
o Privatization of public enterprises improved efficiency and
competitiveness in many sectors.
6. Employment and Income Effects:
o Increased investment led to job creation, especially in
urban and service sectors.
********** 11 **********
--- page 12 ---
o Rising per capita income improved living standards for
many, though income inequality and regional disparities
also increased.
7. Challenges:
o Some sectors like agriculture saw slower growth,
impacting rural livelihoods.
o Greater exposure to global markets introduced economic
volatility.
o Environmental degradation and rising inequality called for
policy attention.
In summary, LPG reforms post-1991 dramatically transformed and
modernized the Indian economy, driving faster growth, global
integration, and increased competitiveness, while presenting new
socio-economic challenges to be managed
Q5   [20]
a.   Critically evaluate Elton Mayo’s contribution to management thought
through the Hawthorne experiments. How are these findings relevant
in   today’s   business   environment?
Elton Mayo’s contribution to management thought primarily stems
from his role in the Hawthorne experiments conducted at the Western
Electric Hawthorne Works in Chicago during the late 1920s to early
1930s. These experiments revolutionized understanding of worker
productivity and motivation by highlighting the significant influence
CO- 4; BL-   of social and psychological factors beyond just physical working
[10]
Understand & Apply
conditions or monetary incentives.
Contribution Through Hawthorne Experiments
• Shift from Mechanistic to Human Relations Approach:
Mayo challenged the classical scientific management view that
workers are motivated solely by wages and physical conditions.
Instead, he emphasized emotional and social needs.
• Importance of Social Groups: The experiments showed that
workers’ productivity improves when they feel socially
********** 12 **********
--- page 13 ---
accepted and part of a supportive group. Informal group norms
and peer relationships strongly influence work behaviors.
• Hawthorne Effect: Productivity increased simply because
workers knew they were being observed and felt attended to.
This highlighted that attention and acknowledgment can
motivate employees.
• Role of Management: The studies underscored the importance
of managerial skills related to interpersonal relationships,
communication, and employee welfare, not just technical
efficiency.
Relevance in Today’s Business Environment
• Employee Engagement and Motivation: Modern HR practices
focus heavily on social recognition, teamwork, and participative
management, echoing Mayo’s insights.
• Workplace Culture: The idea that positive social environments
boost productivity remains central, especially in team-based and
collaborative work settings.
• Leadership Skills: Emotional intelligence, empathy, and
communication are prioritized qualities in leaders, stemming
from human relations thinking.
• Remote and Hybrid Work Challenges: Though technology
changes work modalities, human needs for belonging,
recognition, and support persist and require deliberate
management strategies.
• Organizational Behavior and Psychology: Mayo laid the
foundation for these fields, which now help design workplaces
that consider employee well-being holistically.
In summary, Elton Mayo’s work through the Hawthorne experiments
significantly transformed management thought by highlighting the
human and social dimensions of work. This legacy continues to shape
effective management practices and organizational success today,
where human factors remain crucial amid evolving workplace
contexts
********** 13 **********
--- page 14 ---
b.   Decision-making is the essence of management.” Analyze this
statement by highlighting the types of managerial decisions and their
impact   on   strategic   management
This statement means that management’s primary function is
choosing the best course of action from alternatives to achieve
organizational   goals.   All   managerial   functions—planning,
organizing, leading, and controlling—depend on effective decision-
making.
Types of Managerial Decisions
1. Strategic Decisions:
o Long-term and broad in scope.
o Define the organization’s direction, objectives, and
policies.
o Examples: entering a new market, launching new products,
CO-4;
mergers.   [10]
BL- Apply
o Made by top-level management.
2. Tactical (Managerial) Decisions:
o Medium-term and focused on implementing strategies.
o Allocate resources and coordinate departments.
o Examples: marketing campaigns, budgeting, workforce
planning.
o Made by middle management.
3. Operational Decisions:
o Short-term and routine.
o Concern day-to-day operations.
o Examples: scheduling shifts, inventory reordering, quality
checks.
o Made by lower-level management.
********** 14 **********
--- page 15 ---
Impact of These Decisions on Strategic Management
• Strategic decisions shape the vision and positioning of the
company, setting objectives that guide all other managerial
levels.
• Tactical decisions translate strategies into actionable plans,
ensuring resources are effectively utilized.
• Operational decisions ensure smooth execution and help
respond to immediate challenges.
• Together, these decisions create a coherent system where
strategic management is both conceived and realized.
• Effective decision-making at all levels improves adaptability,
competitive advantage, and organizational performance.
In summary, managerial decision-making at every level is critical for
formulating, implementing, and sustaining strategy, making it the true
essence of management.
Q6   [20]
a.   In emerging economies like India, most people have to work hard for
a living, and a job is a significant part of their lives. So, as the
managers will it still be necessary to motivate employees? Explain the
issue   of   motivation   with   any   theory   of   motivation?
In emerging economies like India, where many people work hard to
CO-4;
BL- Understand and   secure their livelihoods, motivation remains a critical managerial [10]
Apply   function. Despite the necessity of work for survival, simply having a
job does not guarantee high productivity or job satisfaction. Managers
must motivate employees to enhance commitment, performance, and
overall organizational effectiveness.
Why Motivation is Necessary
********** 15 **********
--- page 16 ---
• Beyond Basic Needs: While earning a living meets basic needs,
employees seek recognition, growth, self-fulfillment, and job
satisfaction.
• Improved Performance: Motivated employees are more
productive, creative, and involved in their work.
• Retention and Loyalty: Motivation reduces turnover and builds
loyalty, which is vital in competitive labor markets.
• Adaptation to Change: Motivation supports employees in
embracing organizational changes and challenges.
Issue of Motivation Explained with Maslow’s Hierarchy of Needs
Theory
Maslow’s theory suggests that human needs are arranged in a
hierarchy from basic to advanced levels:
1. Physiological Needs: Food, shelter, salary (basic survival).
2. Safety Needs: Job security, safe working conditions.
3. Social Needs: Belongingness, acceptance by peers and
managers.
4. Esteem Needs: Recognition, status, confidence.
5. Self-actualization Needs: Personal growth, achieving one’s
potential.
According to Maslow, employees must have lower-level needs
satisfied before they seek fulfillment of higher-level needs. Managers
should   tailor motivation   strategies to address these needs
sequentially.
• For example, fair pay and job security meet basic needs.
• Promoting teamwork and healthy work culture meet social
needs.
• Providing recognition and career development addresses esteem
and self-actualization needs.
********** 16 **********
--- page 17 ---
Conclusion
Even in economies where work is a necessity, motivation is crucial
for deriving optimal efforts from employees. Understanding and
addressing diverse employee needs through motivation theories like
Maslow’s helps managers create a more engaged and productive
workforce.
b.   “Controlling is not just the function of the controlling department, but
it is all-pervasive and needs to be done by every department.”
Elaborate on how controlling is linked to the organizing and leading
functions   of   management?   Explain.
Controlling is often viewed as a specialized function performed by a
dedicated controlling department, but in reality, it is an all-pervasive
activity integral to every management level and department. This is
because control ensures that organizational activities are performed
according to the plans, standards, and objectives set by management.
Linking Controlling with Organizing and Leading
1. Controlling and Organizing:
CO-4; BL- Apply   • Organizing involves arranging resources and tasks for effective [10]
goal achievement.
• Controlling ensures that these resources and tasks are utilized
efficiently and according to the organizational structure.
•   It helps verify whether the efforts and workload are distributed
properly and if the organizational setup is working as intended.
• For example, if a department is not meeting its targets,
controlling techniques can identify bottlenecks or overlaps in
responsibilities, prompting reorganizations or adjustments.
2. Controlling and Leading:
• Leading involves motivating, directing, and influencing
employees to perform their tasks effectively.

• Controlling provides feedback on employee performance and
the progress of activities.
•   It helps managers motivate employees by recognizing
achievements or pinpointing areas needing improvement.
• For instance, performance appraisals and feedback sessions are
control mechanisms that guide leadership actions to enhance
employee motivation and performance.

a. A reputed multinational manufacturing unit, with an annual turnover

you apply to achieve better production with the best quality?
To set up a high-end, world-class manufacturing unit producing cost-
efficient, high-quality spare parts, as an operations manager,
implementing effective operational and budgetary controls is critical.
These controls will help optimize production processes, minimize
costs, and ensure consistent quality.
CO-4&5; BL-   Operational Controls
Understand and   1. Process Standardization and Documentation:   [10]
Apply
o Develop standardized operating procedures (SOPs) for all
production activities.
o Use quality management systems (e.g., ISO 9001) for
process consistency.
2. Quality Control (QC) and Quality Assurance (QA):
o Implement in-process inspections and final product quality
checks.
o Use Statistical Process Control (SPC) to monitor process
performance and detect deviations early.
o Establish rejection and rework policies for defective parts.
********** 18 **********
--- page 19 ---
3. Production Scheduling and Capacity Planning:
o Use demand forecasting to create efficient production
schedules.
o Balance workload across machines and workers to avoid
bottlenecks.
o Monitor cycle times to improve throughput.
4. Inventory Management:
o Apply Just-in-Time (JIT) inventory to minimize carrying
costs and waste.
o Use Inventory Control methods like ABC analysis to
prioritize critical components.
5. Maintenance Control:
o Implement Total Productive Maintenance (TPM) to reduce
machine downtime.
o Schedule regular preventive maintenance.
6. Workforce Training and Motivation:
o Train workers on quality standards and safety.
o Involve employees in continuous improvement initiatives.
Budgetary Controls
1. Cost Budgeting:
o Prepare detailed cost budgets for raw materials, labor,
overheads, and utilities.
o Set production cost targets aligned with strategic goals of
cost efficiency.
2. Variance Analysis:
o Regularly compare actual costs with budgets to identify
variances.
********** 19 **********

o Analyze causes for variances and implement corrective
actions.
3. Capital Budgeting:
o Plan and control expenditures on machinery, technology
upgrades, and infrastructure.
o Use ROI and payback period assessments before capital
investments.
4. Performance Metrics and Reporting:
o Develop key performance indicators (KPIs) such as cost
per unit, defect rates, and production efficiency.
o Regular reporting to management for timely decision-
making.
Conclusion
By integrating stringent operational controls with rigorous budgetary
oversight, the manufacturing unit can achieve the goals of high-
quality, cost-efficient production. Continuous monitoring, process
improvement, and employee involvement are key to sustaining global
competitiveness and operational excellence.
b.   As a manager of a fast-food café and restaurant, you have observed
that the restaurant is accruing much cost in storing inventory, leading
to less profit. Suggest and discuss some inventory control techniques
for   reducing   cost   and   achieving   operational   efficiency.
To reduce inventory costs and achieve operational efficiency in a fast-
CO- 4; BL- Apply   food café and restaurant, several proven inventory control techniques [10]
can be applied:
Suggested Inventory Control Techniques
1. First In, First Out (FIFO) Method:
o Use older stock items first to minimize spoilage and waste.
********** 20 **********
--- page 21 ---
o Organize storage so that older inventory is accessible and
used before new arrivals.
o Particularly   important   for   perishable goods   like
ingredients, dairy, and meats.
2. Setting Par Levels:
o Establish minimum inventory levels (par levels) for each
item to avoid both overstocking and stockouts.
o Automatically reorder ingredients or supplies when
inventory hits par, ensuring smooth operations without
excess holding costs.
3. Regular and Consistent Inventory Tracking:
o Conduct frequent cycle counts, focusing on high-value and
perishable items.
o Use the same trained staff to do inventory counts
consistently,   improving   accuracy   and   spotting
discrepancies early.
4. Inventory Categorization and Organization:
o Group inventory into categories (e.g., produce, dairy,
meats).
o Label shelves clearly to reduce counting errors and speed
up restocking and usage.
5. Use of Technology and POS Integration:
o Employ inventory management software compatible with
Point-of-Sale (POS) systems to track sales and inventory
in real time.
o Automate alerts for reorder points, monitor trends, and
reduce manual errors.
6. Waste Tracking and Reduction Programs:
o Maintain a food waste log to identify and minimize
spoilage or loss.
********** 21 **********
--- page 22 ---
o Optimize menu offerings by using surplus or near-expiry
ingredients creatively.
7. Employee Training and Accountability:
o Train staff on inventory procedures and the importance of
minimizing waste.
o Implement checks and balances, such as dual controls for
inventory verification, to reduce theft and errors.
Benefits
• Cost savings through reduced spoilage, waste, and over-
purchasing.
• Improved cash flow by minimizing tied-up capital in excess
inventory.
• Enhanced operational efficiency ensuring smooth preparation
and timely service.
Implementing these inventory control techniques will help the
restaurant manage costs more effectively and improve overall
profitability while maintaining high service standards.

a.   Elaborate on the Production Possibility Curve and its role in determining
the economic growth of a nation.
Production Possibility Curve (PPC) and Its Role in Economic
Growth
The Production Possibility Curve (PPC) is a graphical
representation of the maximum combination of two goods or
services that an economy can produce with its available resources
and technology. It illustrates the concept of opportunity cost,
efficiency, and economic trade-offs.
Key Features of PPC:
1. Concave Shape – Due to the law of increasing opportunity cost.
2. Efficient Points – Any point on the curve represents maximum
resource utilization.
3. Inefficient Points – Points inside the curve indicate
underutilization of resources.
4. Unattainable Points – Points outside the curve are impossible to
achieve with current resources.
Role of PPC in Economic Growth:
CO-1
BL- Understand and   [10]
1. Measures Resource Allocation – Shows how efficiently resources
Apply   are used.
2. Indicates Opportunity Cost – Highlights trade-offs between
different goods.
3. Reflects Economic Efficiency – Movement toward the curve
signifies better resource utilization.
4. Demonstrates Economic Growth – A shift outward in the PPC
represents an increase in a nation’s productive capacity due to:
o Improved Technology – Enhancing production efficiency.
o   Capital Investment – More factories, infrastructure, and
machinery.
o   Skilled Workforce – Education and training improve labor
productivity.
o Discovery of New Resources – More raw materials lead to
higher output.
*Also draw a diagram which shows economic growth through PPC.
Conclusion
The PPC serves as a vital tool in economic analysis, illustrating
scarcity, trade-offs, and efficiency. A shift outward in the PPC
indicates economic growth, enabling a nation to produce more goods
and services, thereby improving the standard of living.
b.   Elucidate the uses of Elasticity of Demand with suitable
CO- 1; BL-   examples.
Understand and   [10]
Uses of Elasticity of Demand with Examples
Evaluate
Elasticity of demand measures how the quantity demanded of a
product responds to changes in price, income, or other factors. It
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
plays a crucial role in business, policymaking, and economic
planning.
Uses of Elasticity of Demand:
1. Pricing Decisions – Businesses set prices based on demand
elasticity.
o Example: If demand for luxury cars is elastic, a price
increase may reduce sales significantly.
2. Revenue Optimization – Helps firms decide whether to
increase or decrease prices.
o Example: If demand for essential goods like salt is
inelastic, raising prices may increase total revenue.
3. Taxation Policies – Governments impose higher taxes on
goods with inelastic demand to generate revenue.
o Example: Taxes on cigarettes and alcohol do not
drastically reduce consumption.
4. Substitute Availability Analysis – Products with elastic
demand face strong competition from substitutes.
o Example: If the price of Pepsi rises, consumers may switch
to Coca-Cola.
5. Income Distribution Effects – Determines the impact of
income changes on demand.
o Example: Luxury products like designer handbags have
high-income elasticity, meaning demand rises as income
increases.
6. Foreign Trade Policies – Helps governments decide
import/export strategies based on price sensitivity.
o Example: If a country exports inelastic goods (like
petroleum), it can benefit from price hikes.
7. Production and Supply Decisions – Businesses plan
production based on elasticity to avoid overproduction.
o Example: Agricultural products often have inelastic
demand, so excess production may lead to price drops.
Conclusion
Elasticity of demand is a powerful tool for businesses, governments,
and economists, guiding pricing, taxation, and trade policies.
Understanding demand sensitivity helps optimize revenue, improve
economic planning, and enhance consumer welfare.
Q3
[20]
a.   Enumerate the main characteristics of the Indifference Curve.
Analyze the process through which a consumer attains optimal
satisfaction using the Indifference Curve and Budget Line
CO-1&2; BL-   approach.
Understand &   [10]
Characteristics of the Indifference Curve and Consumer
Analyze
Equilibrium
An Indifference Curve (IC) represents different combinations of
two goods that provide the same level of satisfaction to a consumer.
It helps in understanding consumer preferences and choices.
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
Main Characteristics of the Indifference Curve:
1. Downward Sloping – As consumption of one good increases, the
other must decrease to maintain the same satisfaction level.
2. Convex to the Origin – Due to the diminishing marginal rate of
substitution (MRS), consumers are willing to give up less of one
good to gain more of another.
3. Higher IC Represents Higher Satisfaction – A curve further from
the origin signifies a greater level of utility.
4. Indifference Curves Never Intersect – Each curve represents a
unique level of satisfaction.
5. Not a Straight Line – Unlike perfect substitutes, ICs are usually
curved.
Consumer Equilibrium: Indifference Curve and Budget Line
Approach
A consumer attains optimal satisfaction where the budget
constraint and the highest possible indifference curve intersect.
*Also draw a diagram of Consumer Equilibrium to support the
answer.
Steps to Achieve Consumer Equilibrium:
1. Budget Line (BL) – Represents all affordable combinations of two
goods based on income and prices.
2. Point of Tangency – Consumer equilibrium occurs where the
budget line is tangent to the highest indifference curve.
3. Condition for Equilibrium:
o MRSxy = Px / Py (Marginal Rate of Substitution equals the
price ratio of goods).
4. Beyond Equilibrium:
o   If a consumer moves beyond the budget line, it becomes
unaffordable.
o Any point below the budget line is inefficient, as it does
not maximize satisfaction.
Conclusion
Using the Indifference Curve and Budget Line approach,
consumers make rational choices to maximize their satisfaction
while staying within their income constraints. This analysis helps
businesses and policymakers understand consumer behavior and
market demand.

--- page 5 ---

Comparative Study of Cardinal and Ordinal Utility Theories
Utility is the satisfaction a consumer derives from consuming goods
and services. Classical economists used the Cardinal Utility
Theory, which assumes utility can be measured in absolute
numbers, while modern economists prefer the Ordinal Utility
Theory, which ranks preferences instead of assigning numerical
values.
Comparison of Cardinal and Ordinal Utility Theories
Feature   Cardinal Utility Theory   Ordina
Ranks preferenc

Relationship Between Total Utility (TU) and Marginal Utility
(MU)
•   Total Utility (TU) is the total satisfaction obtained from
consuming a given quantity of a good.
•   Marginal Utility (MU) is the additional satisfaction gained from
consuming one more unit of the good.
• Law of Diminishing Marginal Utility: As consumption increases,
MU decreases, and when MU becomes zero, TU is maximized.
Diagram: TU and MU Relationship
TU Curve: Starts increasing, reaches a peak, then declines.
MU Curve: Starts high, decreases, becomes zero when TU is max,
and turns negative if consumption continues.
Graph Representation:
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
• The TU curve rises initially, then flattens at maximum
satisfaction, and declines if overconsumption occurs.
• The MU curve declines with each additional unit consumed,
eventually turning negative.
Thus, the Cardinal Utility Theory explains utility numerically,
while the Ordinal Utility Theory focuses on consumer preferences.
The TU and MU relationship helps in understanding consumer
decision-making regarding optimal consumption levels.
Q4   [20]
a.   What is an isocost line and an isoquant? How do they help a
producer in determining a least-cost output
Isocost Line and Isoquant: Tools for Least-Cost Output Decision
Producers aim to maximize output while minimizing costs. Isocost
lines and isoquants are essential tools in production analysis that
help firms achieve this objective.
1. Isocost Line
An isocost line represents all possible combinations of two inputs
(e.g., labor and capital) that a firm can purchase with a given
budget.
Equation of Isocost Line:
TC=(PL×L)+(PK×K)TC = (P_L \times L) + (P_K \times K)
Where:
• TC = Total cost
• P_L = Price of labor
•   L = Quantity of labor
CO- 1; BL- Understand   • P_K = Price of capital   [10]
& Apply   • K = Quantity of capital
Characteristics:
•   It is a straight line with a negative slope, indicating a trade-off
between labor and capital.
• A shift in the isocost line occurs when the budget changes.
2. Isoquant Curve
An isoquant represents all combinations of two inputs that produce
the same level of output. It is similar to an indifference curve in
consumer theory but used in production analysis.
Characteristics:
• Downward Sloping – More of one input requires less of the other
to maintain output.
• Convex to the Origin – Reflects diminishing marginal returns.
•   Higher Isoquants Represent Higher Output – More inputs
increase production.
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
3. Least-Cost Combination of Inputs
Producers aim to achieve cost minimization where the isoquant is
tangent to the isocost line. This point ensures:
• The Marginal Rate of Technical Substitution (MRTS) equals the
ratio of input prices: MRTS=MPLMPK=PLPKMRTS =
\frac{MP_L}{MP_K} = \frac{P_L}{P_K}
• Any other point would either increase costs or reduce output
efficiency.
*Also draw a diagram of the Least Cost Combination/Producer’s
Equilibrium.
Conclusion
By using isoquant and isocost analysis, firms determine the
optimal mix of labor and capital to minimize costs and maximize
output, leading to higher profitability and efficient resource
utilization.
b.   Explain different types of costs in detail. List all the
segregations’ of cost and explain them in detail
Different Types of Costs in Detail
Cost is the monetary value of resources used in the production of
goods and services. Businesses classify costs based on various
criteria to improve financial planning and decision-making.
1. Classification Based on Behavior:
1. Fixed Costs – Costs that remain constant regardless of production
level.
o Example: Rent, salaries, insurance.
2. Variable Costs – Costs that change with production volume.
o Example: Raw materials, wages, electricity.
3. Semi-Variable Costs – Costs with both fixed and variable
CO-1; BL-
components.
Understand &   o Example: Telephone bills (fixed rental + variable usage   [10]
Analyze   charge).
2. Classification Based on Production:
1. Total Cost (TC) – The sum of all costs incurred in production.
TC=FixedCost+VariableCostTC = Fixed Cost + Variable Cost
2. Average Cost (AC) – Cost per unit of output. AC=TCTotalOutputAC
= \frac{TC}{Total Output}
3. Marginal Cost (MC) – The additional cost incurred when
producing one more unit. MC=ΔTCΔOutputMC = \frac{\Delta
TC}{\Delta Output}
3. Classification Based on Traceability:
1. Direct Costs – Costs that can be traced to a specific product.
o Example: Raw materials, direct labor.
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
2. Indirect Costs – Costs that support production but cannot be
directly assigned.
o Example: Factory rent, administrative expenses.
4. Classification Based on Decision-Making:
1. Opportunity Cost – The cost of the next best alternative
foregone.
o Example: Choosing to produce shoes instead of bags.
2. Sunk Cost – Costs already incurred and cannot be recovered.
o Example: Research and development expenses.
5. Classification Based on Periodicity:
1. Short-Run Cost – Costs incurred in the short term with some fixed
inputs.
2. Long-Run Cost – Costs where all inputs become variable over
time.
Conclusion
Understanding different types of costs helps businesses optimize
pricing, budgeting, and profitability strategies, ensuring efficient
resource allocation and financial management.
Q5
[20]
a.   Explain the super-normal profit and loss-making situations of a
firm in a perfectly competitive market with a suitable diagram
and example.
Super-Normal Profit and Loss-Making Situations in a Perfectly
Competitive Market
In a perfectly competitive market, firms are price takers and can
earn different levels of profit based on cost and revenue conditions.
The two key situations are:
1. Super-Normal Profit (Abnormal Profit)
A firm earns super-normal profit when total revenue exceeds total
cost. This happens when the price (P) > Average Total Cost
(ATC).
CO- 1; BL- Analyze   Graph Explanation:   [10]
• The firm's equilibrium is at P = MR = MC (Profit-Maximizing
Condition).
•   Since P > ATC, the firm earns an economic profit, represented by
the shaded area in the graph.
Example:
A tech startup with an innovative product might temporarily earn
super-normal profits before competitors enter the market.
*Also draw a diagram.
2. Loss-Making Situation
A firm incurs losses when price (P) < ATC, meaning total revenue
is insufficient to cover total costs.
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
Graph Explanation:
• The firm's equilibrium is still at P = MR = MC, but ATC > P, leading
to losses.
•   If P ≥ AVC (Average Variable Cost), the firm continues production
to cover variable costs.
•   If P < AVC, the firm shuts down to avoid further losses.
Example:
A small farmer selling wheat at a price lower than production cost
experiences losses, especially if market prices decline due to
oversupply.
*Also draw a diagram.
Conclusion
In the long run, firms in perfect competition earn only normal
profit, as super-normal profits attract new entrants, increasing
supply and lowering prices. Conversely, loss-making firms exit the
market, reducing supply and raising prices until only normal profit
remains.
b. How Maslow’s theory of Motivation is different from
McClelland’s theory of Achievement? Explain with a suitable
example.
Difference Between Maslow’s and McClelland’s Theories of
Motivation
Maslow’s Hierarchy of Needs and McClelland’s Theory of
Achievement both explain motivation but differ in approach.
1. Maslow’s Theory of Motivation (1943)
o Based on a hierarchical structure of five needs:
(i) Physiological, (ii) Safety, (iii) Social, (iv) Esteem, (v)
Self-Actualization.
o   Individuals satisfy lower-level needs before progressing to
CO-3;   higher ones.
BL- Remember &   o Example: An employee first seeks job security (safety   [10]
need) before aiming for recognition (esteem need).
Apply
2. McClelland’s Theory of Achievement (1961)
o Focuses on three key motivational drivers:
(i) Need for Achievement (nAch), (ii) Need for Power
(nPow), (iii) Need for Affiliation (nAff).
o Motivation varies between individuals based on dominant
needs.
o Example: An entrepreneur with high nAch takes risks and
strives for success, while a politician with high nPow
seeks influence.
Key Difference:
Maslow’s theory applies universally, while McClelland’s focuses on
individual personality traits that drive motivation in a professional
setting.

a. How do you support the view that Management must be defined
in terms of effectiveness, coordination of resources, and
objectives?
Management in Terms of Effectiveness, Coordination of
Resources, and Objectives
Management is the process of planning, organizing, leading, and
controlling resources to achieve organizational goals efficiently and
effectively. It must be defined in terms of effectiveness,
coordination of resources, and objectives because these elements
determine a firm’s success.
1. Effectiveness – Refers to achieving the desired goals within
a given time frame.
o A manager must ensure that employees work toward
achieving the company’s mission.
o Example: A software company must deliver a product on
CO-3;   schedule to meet customer expectations.
BL- Apply &   2. Coordination of Resources – Involves efficiently utilizing   [10]
Analyze   human, financial, and material resources.
o   Effective management ensures minimum waste and
maximum productivity.
o Example: A manufacturing firm must allocate raw
materials, labor, and technology efficiently to optimize
production.
3. Objectives Alignment – Every organization operates to
achieve specific short-term and long-term objectives.
o Managers align company activities with strategic goals.
o Example: A retail chain expanding internationally must
align operations with market demand and financial goals.
Conclusion
Management is not just about administering tasks but ensuring
effectiveness, optimizing resources, and aligning activities with
objectives. A well-managed organization achieves growth,
sustainability, and competitive advantage.
b.   Define Planning. What are the essentials of effective planning
and highlight the key factors leading to failure of the Plan.
Definition of Planning
Planning is the process of setting goals, determining actions, and
outlining strategies to achieve organizational objectives. It acts as a
CO-2&3; BL-   roadmap for decision-making and future activities.
[10]
Remember and Apply   Essentials of Effective Planning:
1. Clear Objectives – Goals should be well-defined and measurable.
2. Flexibility – Plans should adapt to changing circumstances.
3. Feasibility – Plans must be realistic and based on available
resources.
4. Accuracy – Based on factual data and logical assumptions.
********** 10 **********
--- page 11 ---
5. Time-Bound – Includes specific timelines for goal achievement.
6. Coordination – Aligns all departments and resources for unified
action.
Key Factors Leading to Plan Failure:
1. Lack of Clear Vision – Undefined objectives create confusion.
2. Rigid Structure – Inability to adapt to changes leads to
inefficiency.
3. Inaccurate Forecasting – Poor data analysis results in flawed
decisions.
4. Resource Constraints – Insufficient funds, manpower, or
technology hinder execution.
5. Resistance to Change – Employees and stakeholders may oppose
new plans.
6. Poor Communication – Lack of clarity in goals and expectations
causes misalignment.
Conclusion
Effective planning ensures goal achievement, resource
optimization, and organizational success, while failure in planning
results in inefficiency and missed opportunities.
Q7
[20]
a.   Briefly explain the contributions of F W Taylor and Elton Mayo in the
discipline of Management.
Contributions of F.W. Taylor and Elton Mayo in Management
1. F.W. Taylor – Scientific Management Theory
Frederick Winslow Taylor, known as the father of scientific
management, introduced principles to enhance productivity through
systematic work processes.
Key Contributions:
1. Scientific Work Methods – Replaced traditional methods with
CO-3; BL-   time and motion studies.
Understand and   2. Standardization – Established rules and tools for efficiency.   [10]
Apply   3. Task Specialization – Encouraged division of labor for increased
output.
4. Incentive-Based Pay – Linked wages to worker performance.
5. Four Principles of Management – Science, Harmony,
Cooperation, and Development.
Example: Taylor’s principles helped in assembly-line production,
as seen in Henry Ford’s automobile manufacturing.
2. Elton Mayo – Human Relations Theory
Elton Mayo emphasized the importance of social and
psychological factors in workplace productivity through the
Hawthorne Studies.
********** 11 **********
--- page 12 ---
Key Contributions:
1. Workplace Environment – Found that employee morale affects
efficiency.
2. Group Dynamics – Social relationships impact productivity.
3. Employee Motivation – Encouraged recognition and
communication between workers and management.
4. Participative Management – Recommended involving employees
in decision-making.
Example: Modern HR practices focus on employee engagement,
teamwork, and leadership development, influenced by Mayo’s
research.
Conclusion
Taylor’s scientific management improved productivity through
efficiency, while Mayo’s human relations approach highlighted
the importance of motivation and teamwork. Both theories shaped
modern management practices.
b.   Explain the concept of "Inventory Control" and discuss its
importance in the context of modern business operations. Also,
highlight any one technique used for effective inventory control.
Concept of Inventory Control
Inventory control refers to the process of managing stock levels to
ensure the right quantity of materials is available at the right time
while minimizing costs. It helps businesses avoid overstocking,
understocking, and wastage.
Importance of Inventory Control in Modern Business
Operations:
1. Cost Reduction – Reduces storage, handling, and wastage costs.
2. Efficient Production – Ensures smooth operations by maintaining
CO- 3; BL-   optimal stock levels.
Understand and   3. Customer Satisfaction – Prevents stockouts and delays, ensuring   [10]

The EOQ model determines the optimal order quantity that
minimizes total inventory costs, including ordering and holding
costs.
EOQ Formula:
EOQ=2DSHEOQ = \sqrt{\frac{2DS}{H}}
Where:
• D = Annual demand
•   S = Ordering cost per order
********** 12 **********
--- page 13 ---
• H = Holding cost per unit per year
Conclusion
Effective inventory control enhances cost efficiency, smooth
operations, and customer satisfaction. Techniques like EOQ help
businesses maintain an optimal balance between demand and supply.

a.   Elaborate on the Production Possibility Curve and its role in determining
the economic growth of a nation.
Production Possibility Curve (PPC) and Its Role in Economic
Growth
The Production Possibility Curve (PPC) is a graphical
representation of the maximum combination of two goods or
services that an economy can produce with its available resources
and technology. It illustrates the concept of opportunity cost,
efficiency, and economic trade-offs.
Key Features of PPC:
1. Concave Shape – Due to the law of increasing opportunity cost.
2. Efficient Points – Any point on the curve represents maximum
resource utilization.
3. Inefficient Points – Points inside the curve indicate
underutilization of resources.
4. Unattainable Points – Points outside the curve are impossible to
achieve with current resources.
Role of PPC in Economic Growth:
CO-1
BL- Understand and   [10]
1. Measures Resource Allocation – Shows how efficiently resources
Apply   are used.
2. Indicates Opportunity Cost – Highlights trade-offs between
different goods.
3. Reflects Economic Efficiency – Movement toward the curve
signifies better resource utilization.
4. Demonstrates Economic Growth – A shift outward in the PPC
represents an increase in a nation’s productive capacity due to:
o Improved Technology – Enhancing production efficiency.
o   Capital Investment – More factories, infrastructure, and
machinery.
o   Skilled Workforce – Education and training improve labor
productivity.
o Discovery of New Resources – More raw materials lead to
higher output.
*Also draw a diagram which shows economic growth through PPC.
Conclusion
The PPC serves as a vital tool in economic analysis, illustrating
scarcity, trade-offs, and efficiency. A shift outward in the PPC
indicates economic growth, enabling a nation to produce more goods
and services, thereby improving the standard of living.
b.   Elucidate the uses of Elasticity of Demand with suitable
CO- 1; BL-   examples.
Understand and   [10]
Uses of Elasticity of Demand with Examples
Evaluate
Elasticity of demand measures how the quantity demanded of a
product responds to changes in price, income, or other factors. It
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
plays a crucial role in business, policymaking, and economic
planning.
Uses of Elasticity of Demand:
1. Pricing Decisions – Businesses set prices based on demand
elasticity.
o Example: If demand for luxury cars is elastic, a price
increase may reduce sales significantly.
2. Revenue Optimization – Helps firms decide whether to
increase or decrease prices.
o Example: If demand for essential goods like salt is
inelastic, raising prices may increase total revenue.
3. Taxation Policies – Governments impose higher taxes on
goods with inelastic demand to generate revenue.
o Example: Taxes on cigarettes and alcohol do not
drastically reduce consumption.
4. Substitute Availability Analysis – Products with elastic
demand face strong competition from substitutes.
o Example: If the price of Pepsi rises, consumers may switch
to Coca-Cola.
5. Income Distribution Effects – Determines the impact of
income changes on demand.
o Example: Luxury products like designer handbags have
high-income elasticity, meaning demand rises as income
increases.
6. Foreign Trade Policies – Helps governments decide
import/export strategies based on price sensitivity.
o Example: If a country exports inelastic goods (like
petroleum), it can benefit from price hikes.
7. Production and Supply Decisions – Businesses plan
production based on elasticity to avoid overproduction.
o Example: Agricultural products often have inelastic
demand, so excess production may lead to price drops.
Conclusion
Elasticity of demand is a powerful tool for businesses, governments,
and economists, guiding pricing, taxation, and trade policies.
Understanding demand sensitivity helps optimize revenue, improve
economic planning, and enhance consumer welfare.
Q3
[20]
a.   Enumerate the main characteristics of the Indifference Curve.
Analyze the process through which a consumer attains optimal
satisfaction using the Indifference Curve and Budget Line
CO-1&2; BL-   approach.
Understand &   [10]
Characteristics of the Indifference Curve and Consumer
Analyze
Equilibrium
An Indifference Curve (IC) represents different combinations of
two goods that provide the same level of satisfaction to a consumer.
It helps in understanding consumer preferences and choices.
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
Main Characteristics of the Indifference Curve:
1. Downward Sloping – As consumption of one good increases, the
other must decrease to maintain the same satisfaction level.
2. Convex to the Origin – Due to the diminishing marginal rate of
substitution (MRS), consumers are willing to give up less of one
good to gain more of another.
3. Higher IC Represents Higher Satisfaction – A curve further from
the origin signifies a greater level of utility.
4. Indifference Curves Never Intersect – Each curve represents a
unique level of satisfaction.
5. Not a Straight Line – Unlike perfect substitutes, ICs are usually
curved.
Consumer Equilibrium: Indifference Curve and Budget Line
Approach
A consumer attains optimal satisfaction where the budget
constraint and the highest possible indifference curve intersect.
*Also draw a diagram of Consumer Equilibrium to support the
answer.
Steps to Achieve Consumer Equilibrium:
1. Budget Line (BL) – Represents all affordable combinations of two
goods based on income and prices.
2. Point of Tangency – Consumer equilibrium occurs where the
budget line is tangent to the highest indifference curve.
3. Condition for Equilibrium:
o MRSxy = Px / Py (Marginal Rate of Substitution equals the
price ratio of goods).
4. Beyond Equilibrium:
o   If a consumer moves beyond the budget line, it becomes
unaffordable.
o Any point below the budget line is inefficient, as it does
not maximize satisfaction.
Conclusion
Using the Indifference Curve and Budget Line approach,
consumers make rational choices to maximize their satisfaction
while staying within their income constraints. This analysis helps
businesses and policymakers understand consumer behavior and
market demand.
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
CO-1&2;   b.   "The interaction between Total Utility (TU) and Marginal Utility
BL- Apply   (MU) is very significant in decision-making on the part of
consumers. Classical economists employed Cardinal Utility
Analysis to measure satisfaction in terms of absolute numbers,
whereas contemporary economists opt for the Ordinal Utility
method, ranking preferences” In line with this viewpoint
perform a comparative study of Cardinal and Ordinal Utility
theories, assessing their assumptions. Also, Illustrate the
relationship between TU and MU using an appropriate diagram.
Comparative Study of Cardinal and Ordinal Utility Theories
Utility is the satisfaction a consumer derives from consuming goods
and services. Classical economists used the Cardinal Utility
Theory, which assumes utility can be measured in absolute
numbers, while modern economists prefer the Ordinal Utility
Theory, which ranks preferences instead of assigning numerical
values.
Comparison of Cardinal and Ordinal Utility Theories
Feature   Cardinal Utility Theory   Ordina
Ranks preferenc
Definition   Measures utility in absolute terms (utils).

Relationship Between Total Utility (TU) and Marginal Utility
(MU)
•   Total Utility (TU) is the total satisfaction obtained from
consuming a given quantity of a good.
•   Marginal Utility (MU) is the additional satisfaction gained from
consuming one more unit of the good.
• Law of Diminishing Marginal Utility: As consumption increases,
MU decreases, and when MU becomes zero, TU is maximized.
Diagram: TU and MU Relationship
TU Curve: Starts increasing, reaches a peak, then declines.
MU Curve: Starts high, decreases, becomes zero when TU is max,
and turns negative if consumption continues.
Graph Representation:
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
• The TU curve rises initially, then flattens at maximum
satisfaction, and declines if overconsumption occurs.
• The MU curve declines with each additional unit consumed,
eventually turning negative.
Thus, the Cardinal Utility Theory explains utility numerically,
while the Ordinal Utility Theory focuses on consumer preferences.
The TU and MU relationship helps in understanding consumer
decision-making regarding optimal consumption levels.
Q4   [20]
a.   What is an isocost line and an isoquant? How do they help a
producer in determining a least-cost output
Isocost Line and Isoquant: Tools for Least-Cost Output Decision
Producers aim to maximize output while minimizing costs. Isocost
lines and isoquants are essential tools in production analysis that
help firms achieve this objective.
1. Isocost Line
An isocost line represents all possible combinations of two inputs
(e.g., labor and capital) that a firm can purchase with a given
budget.
Equation of Isocost Line:
TC=(PL×L)+(PK×K)TC = (P_L \times L) + (P_K \times K)
Where:
• TC = Total cost
• P_L = Price of labor
•   L = Quantity of labor
CO- 1; BL- Understand   • P_K = Price of capital   [10]
& Apply   • K = Quantity of capital
Characteristics:
•   It is a straight line with a negative slope, indicating a trade-off
between labor and capital.
• A shift in the isocost line occurs when the budget changes.
2. Isoquant Curve
An isoquant represents all combinations of two inputs that produce
the same level of output. It is similar to an indifference curve in
consumer theory but used in production analysis.
Characteristics:
• Downward Sloping – More of one input requires less of the other
to maintain output.
• Convex to the Origin – Reflects diminishing marginal returns.
•   Higher Isoquants Represent Higher Output – More inputs
increase production.
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
3. Least-Cost Combination of Inputs
Producers aim to achieve cost minimization where the isoquant is
tangent to the isocost line. This point ensures:
• The Marginal Rate of Technical Substitution (MRTS) equals the
ratio of input prices: MRTS=MPLMPK=PLPKMRTS =
\frac{MP_L}{MP_K} = \frac{P_L}{P_K}
• Any other point would either increase costs or reduce output
efficiency.
*Also draw a diagram of the Least Cost Combination/Producer’s
Equilibrium.
Conclusion
By using isoquant and isocost analysis, firms determine the
optimal mix of labor and capital to minimize costs and maximize
output, leading to higher profitability and efficient resource
utilization.
b.   Explain different types of costs in detail. List all the
segregations’ of cost and explain them in detail
Different Types of Costs in Detail
Cost is the monetary value of resources used in the production of
goods and services. Businesses classify costs based on various
criteria to improve financial planning and decision-making.
1. Classification Based on Behavior:
1. Fixed Costs – Costs that remain constant regardless of production
level.
o Example: Rent, salaries, insurance.
2. Variable Costs – Costs that change with production volume.
o Example: Raw materials, wages, electricity.
3. Semi-Variable Costs – Costs with both fixed and variable
CO-1; BL-
components.
Understand &   o Example: Telephone bills (fixed rental + variable usage   [10]
Analyze   charge).
2. Classification Based on Production:
1. Total Cost (TC) – The sum of all costs incurred in production.
TC=FixedCost+VariableCostTC = Fixed Cost + Variable Cost
2. Average Cost (AC) – Cost per unit of output. AC=TCTotalOutputAC
= \frac{TC}{Total Output}
3. Marginal Cost (MC) – The additional cost incurred when
producing one more unit. MC=ΔTCΔOutputMC = \frac{\Delta
TC}{\Delta Output}
3. Classification Based on Traceability:
1. Direct Costs – Costs that can be traced to a specific product.
o Example: Raw materials, direct labor.
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
2. Indirect Costs – Costs that support production but cannot be
directly assigned.
o Example: Factory rent, administrative expenses.
4. Classification Based on Decision-Making:
1. Opportunity Cost – The cost of the next best alternative
foregone.
o Example: Choosing to produce shoes instead of bags.
2. Sunk Cost – Costs already incurred and cannot be recovered.
o Example: Research and development expenses.
5. Classification Based on Periodicity:
1. Short-Run Cost – Costs incurred in the short term with some fixed
inputs.
2. Long-Run Cost – Costs where all inputs become variable over
time.
Conclusion
Understanding different types of costs helps businesses optimize
pricing, budgeting, and profitability strategies, ensuring efficient
resource allocation and financial management.
Q5
[20]
a.   Explain the super-normal profit and loss-making situations of a
firm in a perfectly competitive market with a suitable diagram
and example.
Super-Normal Profit and Loss-Making Situations in a Perfectly
Competitive Market
In a perfectly competitive market, firms are price takers and can
earn different levels of profit based on cost and revenue conditions.
The two key situations are:
1. Super-Normal Profit (Abnormal Profit)
A firm earns super-normal profit when total revenue exceeds total
cost. This happens when the price (P) > Average Total Cost
(ATC).
CO- 1; BL- Analyze   Graph Explanation:   [10]
• The firm's equilibrium is at P = MR = MC (Profit-Maximizing
Condition).
•   Since P > ATC, the firm earns an economic profit, represented by
the shaded area in the graph.
Example:
A tech startup with an innovative product might temporarily earn
super-normal profits before competitors enter the market.
*Also draw a diagram.
2. Loss-Making Situation
A firm incurs losses when price (P) < ATC, meaning total revenue
is insufficient to cover total costs.
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
Graph Explanation:
• The firm's equilibrium is still at P = MR = MC, but ATC > P, leading
to losses.
•   If P ≥ AVC (Average Variable Cost), the firm continues production
to cover variable costs.
•   If P < AVC, the firm shuts down to avoid further losses.
Example:
A small farmer selling wheat at a price lower than production cost
experiences losses, especially if market prices decline due to
oversupply.
*Also draw a diagram.
Conclusion
In the long run, firms in perfect competition earn only normal
profit, as super-normal profits attract new entrants, increasing
supply and lowering prices. Conversely, loss-making firms exit the
market, reducing supply and raising prices until only normal profit
remains.
b. How Maslow’s theory of Motivation is different from
McClelland’s theory of Achievement? Explain with a suitable
example.
Difference Between Maslow’s and McClelland’s Theories of
Motivation
Maslow’s Hierarchy of Needs and McClelland’s Theory of
Achievement both explain motivation but differ in approach.
1. Maslow’s Theory of Motivation (1943)
o Based on a hierarchical structure of five needs:
(i) Physiological, (ii) Safety, (iii) Social, (iv) Esteem, (v)
Self-Actualization.
o   Individuals satisfy lower-level needs before progressing to
CO-3;   higher ones.
BL- Remember &   o Example: An employee first seeks job security (safety   [10]
need) before aiming for recognition (esteem need).
Apply
2. McClelland’s Theory of Achievement (1961)
o Focuses on three key motivational drivers:
(i) Need for Achievement (nAch), (ii) Need for Power
(nPow), (iii) Need for Affiliation (nAff).
o Motivation varies between individuals based on dominant
needs.
o Example: An entrepreneur with high nAch takes risks and
strives for success, while a politician with high nPow
seeks influence.
Key Difference:
Maslow’s theory applies universally, while McClelland’s focuses on
individual personality traits that drive motivation in a professional
setting.
Q6   [20]
********** 9 **********
--- page 10 ---
a. How do you support the view that Management must be defined
in terms of effectiveness, coordination of resources, and
objectives?
Management in Terms of Effectiveness, Coordination of
Resources, and Objectives
Management is the process of planning, organizing, leading, and
controlling resources to achieve organizational goals efficiently and
effectively. It must be defined in terms of effectiveness,
coordination of resources, and objectives because these elements
determine a firm’s success.
1. Effectiveness – Refers to achieving the desired goals within
a given time frame.
o A manager must ensure that employees work toward
achieving the company’s mission.
o Example: A software company must deliver a product on
CO-3;   schedule to meet customer expectations.
BL- Apply &   2. Coordination of Resources – Involves efficiently utilizing   [10]
Analyze   human, financial, and material resources.
o   Effective management ensures minimum waste and
maximum productivity.
o Example: A manufacturing firm must allocate raw
materials, labor, and technology efficiently to optimize
production.
3. Objectives Alignment – Every organization operates to
achieve specific short-term and long-term objectives.
o Managers align company activities with strategic goals.
o Example: A retail chain expanding internationally must
align operations with market demand and financial goals.
Conclusion
Management is not just about administering tasks but ensuring
effectiveness, optimizing resources, and aligning activities with
objectives. A well-managed organization achieves growth,
sustainability, and competitive advantage.
b.   Define Planning. What are the essentials of effective planning
and highlight the key factors leading to failure of the Plan.
Definition of Planning
Planning is the process of setting goals, determining actions, and
outlining strategies to achieve organizational objectives. It acts as a
CO-2&3; BL-   roadmap for decision-making and future activities.
[10]
Remember and Apply   Essentials of Effective Planning:
1. Clear Objectives – Goals should be well-defined and measurable.
2. Flexibility – Plans should adapt to changing circumstances.
3. Feasibility – Plans must be realistic and based on available
resources.
4. Accuracy – Based on factual data and logical assumptions.
********** 10 **********
--- page 11 ---
5. Time-Bound – Includes specific timelines for goal achievement.
6. Coordination – Aligns all departments and resources for unified
action.
Key Factors Leading to Plan Failure:
1. Lack of Clear Vision – Undefined objectives create confusion.
2. Rigid Structure – Inability to adapt to changes leads to
inefficiency.
3. Inaccurate Forecasting – Poor data analysis results in flawed
decisions.
4. Resource Constraints – Insufficient funds, manpower, or
technology hinder execution.
5. Resistance to Change – Employees and stakeholders may oppose
new plans.
6. Poor Communication – Lack of clarity in goals and expectations
causes misalignment.
Conclusion
Effective planning ensures goal achievement, resource
optimization, and organizational success, while failure in planning
results in inefficiency and missed opportunities.
Q7
[20]
a.   Briefly explain the contributions of F W Taylor and Elton Mayo in the
discipline of Management.
Contributions of F.W. Taylor and Elton Mayo in Management
1. F.W. Taylor – Scientific Management Theory
Frederick Winslow Taylor, known as the father of scientific
management, introduced principles to enhance productivity through
systematic work processes.
Key Contributions:
1. Scientific Work Methods – Replaced traditional methods with
CO-3; BL-   time and motion studies.
Understand and   2. Standardization – Established rules and tools for efficiency.   [10]
Apply   3. Task Specialization – Encouraged division of labor for increased
output.
4. Incentive-Based Pay – Linked wages to worker performance.
5. Four Principles of Management – Science, Harmony,
Cooperation, and Development.
Example: Taylor’s principles helped in assembly-line production,
as seen in Henry Ford’s automobile manufacturing.
2. Elton Mayo – Human Relations Theory
Elton Mayo emphasized the importance of social and
psychological factors in workplace productivity through the
Hawthorne Studies.
********** 11 **********
--- page 12 ---
Key Contributions:
1. Workplace Environment – Found that employee morale affects
efficiency.
2. Group Dynamics – Social relationships impact productivity.
3. Employee Motivation – Encouraged recognition and
communication between workers and management.
4. Participative Management – Recommended involving employees
in decision-making.
Example: Modern HR practices focus on employee engagement,
teamwork, and leadership development, influenced by Mayo’s
research.
Conclusion
Taylor’s scientific management improved productivity through
efficiency, while Mayo’s human relations approach highlighted
the importance of motivation and teamwork. Both theories shaped
modern management practices.
b.   Explain the concept of "Inventory Control" and discuss its
importance in the context of modern business operations. Also,
highlight any one technique used for effective inventory control.
Concept of Inventory Control
Inventory control refers to the process of managing stock levels to
ensure the right quantity of materials is available at the right time
while minimizing costs. It helps businesses avoid overstocking,
understocking, and wastage.
Importance of Inventory Control in Modern Business
Operations:
1. Cost Reduction – Reduces storage, handling, and wastage costs.
2. Efficient Production – Ensures smooth operations by maintaining
CO- 3; BL-   optimal stock levels.
Understand and   3. Customer Satisfaction – Prevents stockouts and delays, ensuring   [10]
Apply   timely deliveries.
4. Improved Cash Flow – Prevents excessive capital being tied up in
inventory.
5. Minimizing Losses – Reduces risks of spoilage, theft, and
obsolescence.
Technique for Effective Inventory Control – Economic Order
Quantity (EOQ):
The EOQ model determines the optimal order quantity that
minimizes total inventory costs, including ordering and holding
costs.
EOQ Formula:
EOQ=2DSHEOQ = \sqrt{\frac{2DS}{H}}
Where:
• D = Annual demand
•   S = Ordering cost per order
********** 12 **********
--- page 13 ---
• H = Holding cost per unit per year
Conclusion
Effective inventory control enhances cost efficiency, smooth
operations, and customer satisfaction. Techniques like EOQ help
businesses maintain an optimal balance between demand and supply.

represents an increase in a nation’s productive capacity due to:
o Improved Technology – Enhancing production efficiency.
o   Capital Investment – More factories, infrastructure, and
machinery.
o   Skilled Workforce – Education and training improve labor
productivity.
o Discovery of New Resources – More raw materials lead to
higher output.
*Also draw a diagram which shows economic growth through PPC.
Conclusion
The PPC serves as a vital tool in economic analysis, illustrating
scarcity, trade-offs, and efficiency. A shift outward in the PPC
indicates economic growth, enabling a nation to produce more goods
and services, thereby improving the standard of living.
b.   Elucidate the uses of Elasticity of Demand with suitable
CO- 1; BL-   examples.
Understand and   [10]
Uses of Elasticity of Demand with Examples
Evaluate
Elasticity of demand measures how the quantity demanded of a
product responds to changes in price, income, or other factors. It
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
plays a crucial role in business, policymaking, and economic
planning.
Uses of Elasticity of Demand:
1. Pricing Decisions – Businesses set prices based on demand
elasticity.
o Example: If demand for luxury cars is elastic, a price
increase may reduce sales significantly.
2. Revenue Optimization – Helps firms decide whether to
increase or decrease prices.
o Example: If demand for essential goods like salt is
inelastic, raising prices may increase total revenue.
3. Taxation Policies – Governments impose higher taxes on
goods with inelastic demand to generate revenue.
o Example: Taxes on cigarettes and alcohol do not
drastically reduce consumption.
4. Substitute Availability Analysis – Products with elastic
demand face strong competition from substitutes.
o Example: If the price of Pepsi rises, consumers may switch
to Coca-Cola.
5. Income Distribution Effects – Determines the impact of
income changes on demand.
o Example: Luxury products like designer handbags have
high-income elasticity, meaning demand rises as income
increases.
6. Foreign Trade Policies – Helps governments decide
import/export strategies based on price sensitivity.
o Example: If a country exports inelastic goods (like
petroleum), it can benefit from price hikes.
7. Production and Supply Decisions – Businesses plan
production based on elasticity to avoid overproduction.
o Example: Agricultural products often have inelastic
demand, so excess production may lead to price drops.
Conclusion
Elasticity of demand is a powerful tool for businesses, governments,
and economists, guiding pricing, taxation, and trade policies.
Understanding demand sensitivity helps optimize revenue, improve
economic planning, and enhance consumer welfare.
Q3
[20]
a.   Enumerate the main characteristics of the Indifference Curve.
Analyze the process through which a consumer attains optimal
satisfaction using the Indifference Curve and Budget Line
CO-1&2; BL-   approach.
Understand &   [10]
Characteristics of the Indifference Curve and Consumer
Analyze
Equilibrium
An Indifference Curve (IC) represents different combinations of
two goods that provide the same level of satisfaction to a consumer.
It helps in understanding consumer preferences and choices.
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
Main Characteristics of the Indifference Curve:
1. Downward Sloping – As consumption of one good increases, the
other must decrease to maintain the same satisfaction level.
2. Convex to the Origin – Due to the diminishing marginal rate of
substitution (MRS), consumers are willing to give up less of one
good to gain more of another.
3. Higher IC Represents Higher Satisfaction – A curve further from
the origin signifies a greater level of utility.
4. Indifference Curves Never Intersect – Each curve represents a
unique level of satisfaction.
5. Not a Straight Line – Unlike perfect substitutes, ICs are usually
curved.
Consumer Equilibrium: Indifference Curve and Budget Line
Approach
A consumer attains optimal satisfaction where the budget
constraint and the highest possible indifference curve intersect.
*Also draw a diagram of Consumer Equilibrium to support the
answer.
Steps to Achieve Consumer Equilibrium:
1. Budget Line (BL) – Represents all affordable combinations of two
goods based on income and prices.
2. Point of Tangency – Consumer equilibrium occurs where the
budget line is tangent to the highest indifference curve.
3. Condition for Equilibrium:
o MRSxy = Px / Py (Marginal Rate of Substitution equals the
price ratio of goods).
4. Beyond Equilibrium:
o   If a consumer moves beyond the budget line, it becomes
unaffordable.
o Any point below the budget line is inefficient, as it does
not maximize satisfaction.
Conclusion
Using the Indifference Curve and Budget Line approach,
consumers make rational choices to maximize their satisfaction
while staying within their income constraints. This analysis helps
businesses and policymakers understand consumer behavior and
market demand.
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---

perform a comparative study of Cardinal and Ordinal Utility
