Comparative Study of Cardinal and Ordinal Utility Theories
Utility is the satisfaction a consumer derives from consuming goods
and services. Classical economists used the Cardinal Utility
Theory, which assumes utility can be measured in absolute
numbers, while modern economists prefer the Ordinal Utility
Theory, which ranks preferences instead of assigning numerical
values.
Comparison of Cardinal and Ordinal Utility Theories
Feature   Cardinal Utility Theory   Ordina
Ranks preferenc
Definition   Measures utility in absolute terms (utils).
specific number
Utility is only co
Measurement Utility is quantifiable.

Main Concept

satisfaction."   say by how muc
Relationship Between Total Utility (TU) and Marginal Utility
(MU)
•   Total Utility (TU) is the total satisfaction obtained from
consuming a given quantity of a good.
•   Marginal Utility (MU) is the additional satisfaction gained from
consuming one more unit of the good.
• Law of Diminishing Marginal Utility: As consumption increases,
MU decreases, and when MU becomes zero, TU is maximized.
Diagram: TU and MU Relationship
TU Curve: Starts increasing, reaches a peak, then declines.
MU Curve: Starts high, decreases, becomes zero when TU is max,
and turns negative if consumption continues.
Graph Representation:
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
• The TU curve rises initially, then flattens at maximum
satisfaction, and declines if overconsumption occurs.
• The MU curve declines with each additional unit consumed,
eventually turning negative.
Thus, the Cardinal Utility Theory explains utility numerically,
while the Ordinal Utility Theory focuses on consumer preferences.
The TU and MU relationship helps in understanding consumer
decision-making regarding optimal consumption levels.
Q4   [20]
a.   What is an isocost line and an isoquant? How do they help a
producer in determining a least-cost output
Isocost Line and Isoquant: Tools for Least-Cost Output Decision
Producers aim to maximize output while minimizing costs. Isocost
lines and isoquants are essential tools in production analysis that
help firms achieve this objective.
1. Isocost Line
An isocost line represents all possible combinations of two inputs
(e.g., labor and capital) that a firm can purchase with a given
budget.
Equation of Isocost Line:
TC=(PL×L)+(PK×K)TC = (P_L \times L) + (P_K \times K)
Where:
• TC = Total cost
• P_L = Price of labor
•   L = Quantity of labor
CO- 1; BL- Understand   • P_K = Price of capital   [10]
& Apply   • K = Quantity of capital
Characteristics:
•   It is a straight line with a negative slope, indicating a trade-off
between labor and capital.
• A shift in the isocost line occurs when the budget changes.
2. Isoquant Curve
An isoquant represents all combinations of two inputs that produce
the same level of output. It is similar to an indifference curve in
consumer theory but used in production analysis.
Characteristics:
• Downward Sloping – More of one input requires less of the other
to maintain output.
• Convex to the Origin – Reflects diminishing marginal returns.
•   Higher Isoquants Represent Higher Output – More inputs
increase production.
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
3. Least-Cost Combination of Inputs
Producers aim to achieve cost minimization where the isoquant is
tangent to the isocost line. This point ensures:
• The Marginal Rate of Technical Substitution (MRTS) equals the
ratio of input prices: MRTS=MPLMPK=PLPKMRTS =
\frac{MP_L}{MP_K} = \frac{P_L}{P_K}
• Any other point would either increase costs or reduce output
efficiency.
*Also draw a diagram of the Least Cost Combination/Producer’s
Equilibrium.
Conclusion
By using isoquant and isocost analysis, firms determine the
optimal mix of labor and capital to minimize costs and maximize
output, leading to higher profitability and efficient resource
utilization.
b.   Explain different types of costs in detail. List all the
segregations’ of cost and explain them in detail
Different Types of Costs in Detail
Cost is the monetary value of resources used in the production of
goods and services. Businesses classify costs based on various
criteria to improve financial planning and decision-making.
1. Classification Based on Behavior:
1. Fixed Costs – Costs that remain constant regardless of production
level.
o Example: Rent, salaries, insurance.
2. Variable Costs – Costs that change with production volume.
o Example: Raw materials, wages, electricity.
3. Semi-Variable Costs – Costs with both fixed and variable
CO-1; BL-
components.
Understand &   o Example: Telephone bills (fixed rental + variable usage   [10]
Analyze   charge).
2. Classification Based on Production:
1. Total Cost (TC) – The sum of all costs incurred in production.
TC=FixedCost+VariableCostTC = Fixed Cost + Variable Cost
2. Average Cost (AC) – Cost per unit of output. AC=TCTotalOutputAC
= \frac{TC}{Total Output}
3. Marginal Cost (MC) – The additional cost incurred when
producing one more unit. MC=ΔTCΔOutputMC = \frac{\Delta
TC}{\Delta Output}
3. Classification Based on Traceability:
1. Direct Costs – Costs that can be traced to a specific product.
o Example: Raw materials, direct labor.
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
2. Indirect Costs – Costs that support production but cannot be
directly assigned.
o Example: Factory rent, administrative expenses.
4. Classification Based on Decision-Making:
1. Opportunity Cost – The cost of the next best alternative
foregone.
o Example: Choosing to produce shoes instead of bags.
2. Sunk Cost – Costs already incurred and cannot be recovered.
o Example: Research and development expenses.
5. Classification Based on Periodicity:
1. Short-Run Cost – Costs incurred in the short term with some fixed
inputs.
2. Long-Run Cost – Costs where all inputs become variable over
time.
Conclusion
Understanding different types of costs helps businesses optimize
pricing, budgeting, and profitability strategies, ensuring efficient
resource allocation and financial management.
Q5
[20]
a.   Explain the super-normal profit and loss-making situations of a
firm in a perfectly competitive market with a suitable diagram
and example.
Super-Normal Profit and Loss-Making Situations in a Perfectly
Competitive Market
In a perfectly competitive market, firms are price takers and can
earn different levels of profit based on cost and revenue conditions.
The two key situations are:
1. Super-Normal Profit (Abnormal Profit)
A firm earns super-normal profit when total revenue exceeds total
cost. This happens when the price (P) > Average Total Cost
(ATC).
CO- 1; BL- Analyze   Graph Explanation:   [10]
• The firm's equilibrium is at P = MR = MC (Profit-Maximizing
Condition).
•   Since P > ATC, the firm earns an economic profit, represented by
the shaded area in the graph.
Example:
A tech startup with an innovative product might temporarily earn
super-normal profits before competitors enter the market.
*Also draw a diagram.
2. Loss-Making Situation
A firm incurs losses when price (P) < ATC, meaning total revenue
is insufficient to cover total costs.
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
Graph Explanation:
• The firm's equilibrium is still at P = MR = MC, but ATC > P, leading
to losses.
•   If P ≥ AVC (Average Variable Cost), the firm continues production
to cover variable costs.
•   If P < AVC, the firm shuts down to avoid further losses.
Example:
A small farmer selling wheat at a price lower than production cost
experiences losses, especially if market prices decline due to
oversupply.
*Also draw a diagram.
Conclusion
In the long run, firms in perfect competition earn only normal
profit, as super-normal profits attract new entrants, increasing
supply and lowering prices. Conversely, loss-making firms exit the
market, reducing supply and raising prices until only normal profit
remains.
b. How Maslow’s theory of Motivation is different from
McClelland’s theory of Achievement? Explain with a suitable
example.
Difference Between Maslow’s and McClelland’s Theories of
Motivation
Maslow’s Hierarchy of Needs and McClelland’s Theory of
Achievement both explain motivation but differ in approach.
1. Maslow’s Theory of Motivation (1943)
o Based on a hierarchical structure of five needs:
(i) Physiological, (ii) Safety, (iii) Social, (iv) Esteem, (v)
Self-Actualization.
o   Individuals satisfy lower-level needs before progressing to
CO-3;   higher ones.
BL- Remember &   o Example: An employee first seeks job security (safety   [10]
need) before aiming for recognition (esteem need).
Apply
2. McClelland’s Theory of Achievement (1961)
o Focuses on three key motivational drivers:
(i) Need for Achievement (nAch), (ii) Need for Power
(nPow), (iii) Need for Affiliation (nAff).
o Motivation varies between individuals based on dominant
needs.
o Example: An entrepreneur with high nAch takes risks and
strives for success, while a politician with high nPow
seeks influence.
Key Difference:
Maslow’s theory applies universally, while McClelland’s focuses on
individual personality traits that drive motivation in a professional
setting.

--- page 10 ---
a. How do you support the view that Management must be defined
in terms of effectiveness, coordination of resources, and
objectives?
Management in Terms of Effectiveness, Coordination of
Resources, and Objectives
Management is the process of planning, organizing, leading, and
controlling resources to achieve organizational goals efficiently and
effectively. It must be defined in terms of effectiveness,
coordination of resources, and objectives because these elements
determine a firm’s success.
1. Effectiveness – Refers to achieving the desired goals within
a given time frame.
o A manager must ensure that employees work toward
achieving the company’s mission.
o Example: A software company must deliver a product on
CO-3;   schedule to meet customer expectations.
BL- Apply &   2. Coordination of Resources – Involves efficiently utilizing   [10]
Analyze   human, financial, and material resources.
o   Effective management ensures minimum waste and
maximum productivity.
o Example: A manufacturing firm must allocate raw
materials, labor, and technology efficiently to optimize
production.
3. Objectives Alignment – Every organization operates to
achieve specific short-term and long-term objectives.
o Managers align company activities with strategic goals.
o Example: A retail chain expanding internationally must
align operations with market demand and financial goals.
Conclusion
Management is not just about administering tasks but ensuring
effectiveness, optimizing resources, and aligning activities with
objectives. A well-managed organization achieves growth,
sustainability, and competitive advantage.
b.   Define Planning. What are the essentials of effective planning
and highlight the key factors leading to failure of the Plan.
Definition of Planning
Planning is the process of setting goals, determining actions, and
outlining strategies to achieve organizational objectives. It acts as a
CO-2&3; BL-   roadmap for decision-making and future activities.
[10]
Remember and Apply   Essentials of Effective Planning:
1. Clear Objectives – Goals should be well-defined and measurable.
2. Flexibility – Plans should adapt to changing circumstances.
3. Feasibility – Plans must be realistic and based on available
resources.
4. Accuracy – Based on factual data and logical assumptions.
********** 10 **********
--- page 11 ---
5. Time-Bound – Includes specific timelines for goal achievement.
6. Coordination – Aligns all departments and resources for unified
action.
Key Factors Leading to Plan Failure:
1. Lack of Clear Vision – Undefined objectives create confusion.
2. Rigid Structure – Inability to adapt to changes leads to
inefficiency.
3. Inaccurate Forecasting – Poor data analysis results in flawed
decisions.
4. Resource Constraints – Insufficient funds, manpower, or
technology hinder execution.
5. Resistance to Change – Employees and stakeholders may oppose
new plans.
6. Poor Communication – Lack of clarity in goals and expectations
causes misalignment.
Conclusion
Effective planning ensures goal achievement, resource
optimization, and organizational success, while failure in planning
results in inefficiency and missed opportunities.
Q7
[20]
a.   Briefly explain the contributions of F W Taylor and Elton Mayo in the
discipline of Management.
Contributions of F.W. Taylor and Elton Mayo in Management
1. F.W. Taylor – Scientific Management Theory
Frederick Winslow Taylor, known as the father of scientific
management, introduced principles to enhance productivity through
systematic work processes.
Key Contributions:
1. Scientific Work Methods – Replaced traditional methods with
CO-3; BL-   time and motion studies.
Understand and   2. Standardization – Established rules and tools for efficiency.   [10]
Apply   3. Task Specialization – Encouraged division of labor for increased
output.
4. Incentive-Based Pay – Linked wages to worker performance.
5. Four Principles of Management – Science, Harmony,
Cooperation, and Development.
Example: Taylor’s principles helped in assembly-line production,
as seen in Henry Ford’s automobile manufacturing.
2. Elton Mayo – Human Relations Theory
Elton Mayo emphasized the importance of social and
psychological factors in workplace productivity through the
Hawthorne Studies.
********** 11 **********
--- page 12 ---
Key Contributions:
1. Workplace Environment – Found that employee morale affects
efficiency.
2. Group Dynamics – Social relationships impact productivity.
3. Employee Motivation – Encouraged recognition and
communication between workers and management.
4. Participative Management – Recommended involving employees
in decision-making.
Example: Modern HR practices focus on employee engagement,
teamwork, and leadership development, influenced by Mayo’s
research.
Conclusion
Taylor’s scientific management improved productivity through
efficiency, while Mayo’s human relations approach highlighted
the importance of motivation and teamwork. Both theories shaped
modern management practices.
b.   Explain the concept of "Inventory Control" and discuss its
importance in the context of modern business operations. Also,
highlight any one technique used for effective inventory control.
Concept of Inventory Control
Inventory control refers to the process of managing stock levels to
ensure the right quantity of materials is available at the right time
while minimizing costs. It helps businesses avoid overstocking,
understocking, and wastage.
Importance of Inventory Control in Modern Business
Operations:
1. Cost Reduction – Reduces storage, handling, and wastage costs.
2. Efficient Production – Ensures smooth operations by maintaining
CO- 3; BL-   optimal stock levels.
Understand and   3. Customer Satisfaction – Prevents stockouts and delays, ensuring   [10]
Apply   timely deliveries.
4. Improved Cash Flow – Prevents excessive capital being tied up in
inventory.
5. Minimizing Losses – Reduces risks of spoilage, theft, and
obsolescence.
Technique for Effective Inventory Control – Economic Order
Quantity (EOQ):
The EOQ model determines the optimal order quantity that
minimizes total inventory costs, including ordering and holding
costs.
EOQ Formula:
EOQ=2DSHEOQ = \sqrt{\frac{2DS}{H}}
Where:
• D = Annual demand
•   S = Ordering cost per order
********** 12 **********
--- page 13 ---
• H = Holding cost per unit per year
Conclusion
Effective inventory control enhances cost efficiency, smooth
operations, and customer satisfaction. Techniques like EOQ help
businesses maintain an optimal balance between demand and supply.

a.   Elaborate on the Production Possibility Curve and its role in determining
the economic growth of a nation.
Production Possibility Curve (PPC) and Its Role in Economic
Growth
The Production Possibility Curve (PPC) is a graphical
representation of the maximum combination of two goods or
services that an economy can produce with its available resources
and technology. It illustrates the concept of opportunity cost,
efficiency, and economic trade-offs.
Key Features of PPC:
1. Concave Shape – Due to the law of increasing opportunity cost.
2. Efficient Points – Any point on the curve represents maximum
resource utilization.
3. Inefficient Points – Points inside the curve indicate
underutilization of resources.
4. Unattainable Points – Points outside the curve are impossible to
achieve with current resources.
Role of PPC in Economic Growth:
CO-1
BL- Understand and   [10]
1. Measures Resource Allocation – Shows how efficiently resources
Apply   are used.
2. Indicates Opportunity Cost – Highlights trade-offs between
different goods.
3. Reflects Economic Efficiency – Movement toward the curve
signifies better resource utilization.
4. Demonstrates Economic Growth – A shift outward in the PPC
represents an increase in a nation’s productive capacity due to:
o Improved Technology – Enhancing production efficiency.
o   Capital Investment – More factories, infrastructure, and
machinery.
o   Skilled Workforce – Education and training improve labor
productivity.
o Discovery of New Resources – More raw materials lead to
higher output.
*Also draw a diagram which shows economic growth through PPC.
Conclusion
The PPC serves as a vital tool in economic analysis, illustrating
scarcity, trade-offs, and efficiency. A shift outward in the PPC
indicates economic growth, enabling a nation to produce more goods
and services, thereby improving the standard of living.

Comparative Study of Cardinal and Ordinal Utility Theories
Utility is the satisfaction a consumer derives from consuming goods
and services. Classical economists used the Cardinal Utility
Theory, which assumes utility can be measured in absolute
numbers, while modern economists prefer the Ordinal Utility
Theory, which ranks preferences instead of assigning numerical
values.
Comparison of Cardinal and Ordinal Utility Theories
Feature   Cardinal Utility Theory   Ordina
Ranks preferenc
Definition   Measures utility in absolute terms (utils).

Relationship Between Total Utility (TU) and Marginal Utility
(MU)
•   Total Utility (TU) is the total satisfaction obtained from
consuming a given quantity of a good.
•   Marginal Utility (MU) is the additional satisfaction gained from
consuming one more unit of the good.
• Law of Diminishing Marginal Utility: As consumption increases,
MU decreases, and when MU becomes zero, TU is maximized.
Diagram: TU and MU Relationship
TU Curve: Starts increasing, reaches a peak, then declines.
MU Curve: Starts high, decreases, becomes zero when TU is max,
and turns negative if consumption continues.
Graph Representation:
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
• The TU curve rises initially, then flattens at maximum
satisfaction, and declines if overconsumption occurs.
• The MU curve declines with each additional unit consumed,
eventually turning negative.
Thus, the Cardinal Utility Theory explains utility numerically,
while the Ordinal Utility Theory focuses on consumer preferences.
The TU and MU relationship helps in understanding consumer
decision-making regarding optimal consumption levels.
Q4   [20]
a.   What is an isocost line and an isoquant? How do they help a
producer in determining a least-cost output
Isocost Line and Isoquant: Tools for Least-Cost Output Decision
Producers aim to maximize output while minimizing costs. Isocost
lines and isoquants are essential tools in production analysis that
help firms achieve this objective.
1. Isocost Line
An isocost line represents all possible combinations of two inputs
(e.g., labor and capital) that a firm can purchase with a given
budget.
Equation of Isocost Line:
TC=(PL×L)+(PK×K)TC = (P_L \times L) + (P_K \times K)
Where:
• TC = Total cost
• P_L = Price of labor
•   L = Quantity of labor
CO- 1; BL- Understand   • P_K = Price of capital   [10]
& Apply   • K = Quantity of capital
Characteristics:
•   It is a straight line with a negative slope, indicating a trade-off
between labor and capital.
• A shift in the isocost line occurs when the budget changes.
2. Isoquant Curve
An isoquant represents all combinations of two inputs that produce
the same level of output. It is similar to an indifference curve in
consumer theory but used in production analysis.
Characteristics:
• Downward Sloping – More of one input requires less of the other
to maintain output.
• Convex to the Origin – Reflects diminishing marginal returns.
•   Higher Isoquants Represent Higher Output – More inputs
increase production.
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
3. Least-Cost Combination of Inputs
Producers aim to achieve cost minimization where the isoquant is
tangent to the isocost line. This point ensures:
• The Marginal Rate of Technical Substitution (MRTS) equals the
ratio of input prices: MRTS=MPLMPK=PLPKMRTS =
\frac{MP_L}{MP_K} = \frac{P_L}{P_K}
• Any other point would either increase costs or reduce output
efficiency.
*Also draw a diagram of the Least Cost Combination/Producer’s
Equilibrium.
Conclusion
By using isoquant and isocost analysis, firms determine the
optimal mix of labor and capital to minimize costs and maximize
output, leading to higher profitability and efficient resource
utilization.
b.   Explain different types of costs in detail. List all the
segregations’ of cost and explain them in detail
Different Types of Costs in Detail
Cost is the monetary value of resources used in the production of
goods and services. Businesses classify costs based on various
criteria to improve financial planning and decision-making.
1. Classification Based on Behavior:
1. Fixed Costs – Costs that remain constant regardless of production
level.
o Example: Rent, salaries, insurance.
2. Variable Costs – Costs that change with production volume.
o Example: Raw materials, wages, electricity.
3. Semi-Variable Costs – Costs with both fixed and variable
CO-1; BL-
components.
Understand &   o Example: Telephone bills (fixed rental + variable usage   [10]
Analyze   charge).
2. Classification Based on Production:
1. Total Cost (TC) – The sum of all costs incurred in production.
TC=FixedCost+VariableCostTC = Fixed Cost + Variable Cost
2. Average Cost (AC) – Cost per unit of output. AC=TCTotalOutputAC
= \frac{TC}{Total Output}
3. Marginal Cost (MC) – The additional cost incurred when
producing one more unit. MC=ΔTCΔOutputMC = \frac{\Delta
TC}{\Delta Output}
3. Classification Based on Traceability:
1. Direct Costs – Costs that can be traced to a specific product.
o Example: Raw materials, direct labor.
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
2. Indirect Costs – Costs that support production but cannot be
directly assigned.
o Example: Factory rent, administrative expenses.
4. Classification Based on Decision-Making:
1. Opportunity Cost – The cost of the next best alternative
foregone.
o Example: Choosing to produce shoes instead of bags.
2. Sunk Cost – Costs already incurred and cannot be recovered.
o Example: Research and development expenses.
5. Classification Based on Periodicity:
1. Short-Run Cost – Costs incurred in the short term with some fixed
inputs.
2. Long-Run Cost – Costs where all inputs become variable over
time.
Conclusion
Understanding different types of costs helps businesses optimize
pricing, budgeting, and profitability strategies, ensuring efficient
resource allocation and financial management.
Q5
[20]
a.   Explain the super-normal profit and loss-making situations of a
firm in a perfectly competitive market with a suitable diagram
and example.
Super-Normal Profit and Loss-Making Situations in a Perfectly
Competitive Market
In a perfectly competitive market, firms are price takers and can
earn different levels of profit based on cost and revenue conditions.
The two key situations are:
1. Super-Normal Profit (Abnormal Profit)
A firm earns super-normal profit when total revenue exceeds total
cost. This happens when the price (P) > Average Total Cost
(ATC).
CO- 1; BL- Analyze   Graph Explanation:   [10]
• The firm's equilibrium is at P = MR = MC (Profit-Maximizing
Condition).
•   Since P > ATC, the firm earns an economic profit, represented by
the shaded area in the graph.
Example:
A tech startup with an innovative product might temporarily earn
super-normal profits before competitors enter the market.
*Also draw a diagram.
2. Loss-Making Situation
A firm incurs losses when price (P) < ATC, meaning total revenue
is insufficient to cover total costs.
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
Graph Explanation:
• The firm's equilibrium is still at P = MR = MC, but ATC > P, leading
to losses.
•   If P ≥ AVC (Average Variable Cost), the firm continues production
to cover variable costs.
•   If P < AVC, the firm shuts down to avoid further losses.
Example:
A small farmer selling wheat at a price lower than production cost
experiences losses, especially if market prices decline due to
oversupply.
*Also draw a diagram.
Conclusion
In the long run, firms in perfect competition earn only normal
profit, as super-normal profits attract new entrants, increasing
supply and lowering prices. Conversely, loss-making firms exit the
market, reducing supply and raising prices until only normal profit
remains.
b. How Maslow’s theory of Motivation is different from
McClelland’s theory of Achievement? Explain with a suitable
example.
Difference Between Maslow’s and McClelland’s Theories of
Motivation
Maslow’s Hierarchy of Needs and McClelland’s Theory of
Achievement both explain motivation but differ in approach.
1. Maslow’s Theory of Motivation (1943)
o Based on a hierarchical structure of five needs:
(i) Physiological, (ii) Safety, (iii) Social, (iv) Esteem, (v)
Self-Actualization.
o   Individuals satisfy lower-level needs before progressing to
CO-3;   higher ones.
BL- Remember &   o Example: An employee first seeks job security (safety   [10]
need) before aiming for recognition (esteem need).
Apply
2. McClelland’s Theory of Achievement (1961)
o Focuses on three key motivational drivers:
(i) Need for Achievement (nAch), (ii) Need for Power
(nPow), (iii) Need for Affiliation (nAff).
o Motivation varies between individuals based on dominant
needs.
o Example: An entrepreneur with high nAch takes risks and
strives for success, while a politician with high nPow
seeks influence.
Key Difference:
Maslow’s theory applies universally, while McClelland’s focuses on
individual personality traits that drive motivation in a professional
setting.

a. How do you support the view that Management must be defined
in terms of effectiveness, coordination of resources, and
objectives?
Management in Terms of Effectiveness, Coordination of
Resources, and Objectives
Management is the process of planning, organizing, leading, and
controlling resources to achieve organizational goals efficiently and
effectively. It must be defined in terms of effectiveness,
coordination of resources, and objectives because these elements
determine a firm’s success.
1. Effectiveness – Refers to achieving the desired goals within
a given time frame.
o A manager must ensure that employees work toward
achieving the company’s mission.
o Example: A software company must deliver a product on
CO-3;   schedule to meet customer expectations.
BL- Apply &   2. Coordination of Resources – Involves efficiently utilizing   [10]
Analyze   human, financial, and material resources.
o   Effective management ensures minimum waste and
maximum productivity.
o Example: A manufacturing firm must allocate raw
materials, labor, and technology efficiently to optimize
production.
3. Objectives Alignment – Every organization operates to
achieve specific short-term and long-term objectives.
o Managers align company activities with strategic goals.
o Example: A retail chain expanding internationally must
align operations with market demand and financial goals.
Conclusion
Management is not just about administering tasks but ensuring
effectiveness, optimizing resources, and aligning activities with
objectives. A well-managed organization achieves growth,
sustainability, and competitive advantage.
b.   Define Planning. What are the essentials of effective planning
and highlight the key factors leading to failure of the Plan.
Definition of Planning
Planning is the process of setting goals, determining actions, and
outlining strategies to achieve organizational objectives. It acts as a
CO-2&3; BL-   roadmap for decision-making and future activities.
[10]
Remember and Apply   Essentials of Effective Planning:
1. Clear Objectives – Goals should be well-defined and measurable.
2. Flexibility – Plans should adapt to changing circumstances.
3. Feasibility – Plans must be realistic and based on available
resources.
4. Accuracy – Based on factual data and logical assumptions.
********** 10 **********
--- page 11 ---
5. Time-Bound – Includes specific timelines for goal achievement.
6. Coordination – Aligns all departments and resources for unified
action.
Key Factors Leading to Plan Failure:
1. Lack of Clear Vision – Undefined objectives create confusion.
2. Rigid Structure – Inability to adapt to changes leads to
inefficiency.
3. Inaccurate Forecasting – Poor data analysis results in flawed
decisions.
4. Resource Constraints – Insufficient funds, manpower, or
technology hinder execution.
5. Resistance to Change – Employees and stakeholders may oppose
new plans.
6. Poor Communication – Lack of clarity in goals and expectations
causes misalignment.
Conclusion
Effective planning ensures goal achievement, resource
optimization, and organizational success, while failure in planning
results in inefficiency and missed opportunities.

a.   Briefly explain the contributions of F W Taylor and Elton Mayo in the
discipline of Management.
Contributions of F.W. Taylor and Elton Mayo in Management
1. F.W. Taylor – Scientific Management Theory
Frederick Winslow Taylor, known as the father of scientific
management, introduced principles to enhance productivity through
systematic work processes.
Key Contributions:
1. Scientific Work Methods – Replaced traditional methods with
CO-3; BL-   time and motion studies.
Understand and   2. Standardization – Established rules and tools for efficiency.   [10]
Apply   3. Task Specialization – Encouraged division of labor for increased
output.
4. Incentive-Based Pay – Linked wages to worker performance.
5. Four Principles of Management – Science, Harmony,
Cooperation, and Development.
Example: Taylor’s principles helped in assembly-line production,
as seen in Henry Ford’s automobile manufacturing.
2. Elton Mayo – Human Relations Theory
Elton Mayo emphasized the importance of social and
psychological factors in workplace productivity through the
Hawthorne Studies.
********** 11 **********
--- page 12 ---
Key Contributions:
1. Workplace Environment – Found that employee morale affects
efficiency.
2. Group Dynamics – Social relationships impact productivity.
3. Employee Motivation – Encouraged recognition and
communication between workers and management.
4. Participative Management – Recommended involving employees
in decision-making.
Example: Modern HR practices focus on employee engagement,
teamwork, and leadership development, influenced by Mayo’s
research.
Conclusion
Taylor’s scientific management improved productivity through
efficiency, while Mayo’s human relations approach highlighted
the importance of motivation and teamwork. Both theories shaped
modern management practices.
b.   Explain the concept of "Inventory Control" and discuss its
importance in the context of modern business operations. Also,
highlight any one technique used for effective inventory control.
Concept of Inventory Control
Inventory control refers to the process of managing stock levels to
ensure the right quantity of materials is available at the right time
while minimizing costs. It helps businesses avoid overstocking,
understocking, and wastage.
Importance of Inventory Control in Modern Business
Operations:
1. Cost Reduction – Reduces storage, handling, and wastage costs.
2. Efficient Production – Ensures smooth operations by maintaining
CO- 3; BL-   optimal stock levels.
Understand and   3. Customer Satisfaction – Prevents stockouts and delays, ensuring   [10]
Apply   timely deliveries.
4. Improved Cash Flow – Prevents excessive capital being tied up in
inventory.
5. Minimizing Losses – Reduces risks of spoilage, theft, and
obsolescence.
Technique for Effective Inventory Control – Economic Order
Quantity (EOQ):
The EOQ model determines the optimal order quantity that
minimizes total inventory costs, including ordering and holding
costs.
EOQ Formula:
EOQ=2DSHEOQ = \sqrt{\frac{2DS}{H}}
Where:
• D = Annual demand
•   S = Ordering cost per order
********** 12 **********
--- page 13 ---
• H = Holding cost per unit per year
Conclusion
Effective inventory control enhances cost efficiency, smooth
operations, and customer satisfaction. Techniques like EOQ help
businesses maintain an optimal balance between demand and supply.

Q2   [20]
a.   Critically examine the Law of Demand with the help of a diagram with
its exceptions.
Critical Examination of the Law of Demand
Definition of the Law of Demand
The Law of Demand states that, "other things being equal, the
quantity demanded of a good decreases when its price increases, and
vice versa." This means that there is an inverse relationship between
price and quantity demanded.
Diagram of the Law of Demand
Below is a graphical representation of the Law of Demand:
Demand Curve: The demand curve is downward sloping from left
to right, indicating the inverse relationship between price and
quantity demanded.
Price (P)
|
|   • (P1, Q1)
|
|   • (P2, Q2)
|
|___________ Quantity (Q)
• As price (P) decreases from P1 to P2, the quantity demanded (Q)
increases from Q1 to Q2.
•   This downward slope shows the negative relationship between
CO-1
price and demand.
BL- Understand and   [10]
Apply
Critical Examination of the Law of Demand
1. Assumptions of the Law of Demand
The Law of Demand holds true only under certain conditions,
including:
Ceteris Paribus (Other Factors Remain Constant) – No change in
income, tastes, or market trends.
No Future Price Expectations – Consumers do not expect price
fluctuations.
Availability of Substitutes – Buyers can switch to alternatives if price
rises.
However, in real-world markets, these conditions may not always
hold, leading to exceptions to the Law of Demand.
Exceptions to the Law of Demand
While the Law of Demand is widely accepted, certain cases defy this
principle, meaning that demand may increase even when prices rise.
These exceptions include:
1. Giffen Goods (Inferior Goods with No Substitutes)
• Named after Sir Robert Giffen, these are staple goods (e.g.,
bread, rice, potatoes) for which demand increases when the
price rises.
• Why? Poor consumers, when faced with rising prices, cut down
on expensive items (meat) and buy more of the staple (rice).
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
Giffen Paradox: Demand curve slopes upward instead of
downward in these cases.
2. Veblen Goods (Luxury or Prestige Goods)
• Named after Thorstein Veblen, these are status symbols (luxury
watches, designer bags, sports cars).
• Higher prices make these goods more desirable due to their
exclusivity (snob appeal).
• Example: A Rolex watch may sell more if its price rises, as it is
seen as a sign of wealth.
3. Speculative Goods (Stock Market & Real Estate)
• When investors believe that prices will continue to rise, they
increase demand despite higher prices.
• Example: During a stock market boom, investors buy more
shares even at inflated prices.
4. Necessities (Inelastic Demand)
•   Essential goods like medicines, electricity, and water do not
follow the Law of Demand.
• Even if prices rise, people cannot reduce consumption
significantly.
5. Future Price Expectations
•   If consumers expect prices to increase further, they may buy
more now, even if prices are already high.
• Example: Real estate investors purchasing property when
market prices are rising.
Conclusion
The Law of Demand is generally valid, but exceptions arise due to
economic, psychological, and social factors.
Giffen goods, Veblen goods, speculative assets, necessities, and future
expectations are key exceptions.
Understanding these exceptions is crucial for businesses, economists,
and policymakers when making pricing and marketing decisions.
b.   As a Price strategist of the product, How would you use the different types
of Price elasticities to determine optimum pricing for your product?
Using Price Elasticity for Optimal Pricing Strategy
As a Price Strategist, understanding price elasticity of demand
CO- 1; BL-
(PED) helps in setting the right pricing strategy to maximize
Understand and   revenue and profitability. Price elasticity measures how sensitive   [10]
Evaluate   consumers are to price changes and can be categorized into different
degrees:
1. Perfectly Inelastic Demand (PED = 0) – Pricing for Necessities
• Demand remains constant regardless of price changes (e.g., life-
saving drugs, salt).
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
•   Strategy: Price can be set higher, as consumers will buy it
regardless of the price.
2. Inelastic Demand (0 < PED < 1) – Pricing for Essential & Brand-Loyal
Products
• A price increase leads to a smaller decrease in demand (e.g., fuel,
electricity).
•   Strategy: Increase price gradually to maximize revenue without
losing many customers.
3. Unitary Elastic Demand (PED = 1) – Balancing Price & Revenue
• A percentage change in price results in an equal percentage
change in demand.
•   Strategy: Maintain price stability, as total revenue remains
unchanged.
4. Elastic Demand (PED > 1) – Pricing for Luxury & Competitive Goods
• A small price increase causes a large drop in demand (e.g., luxury
cars, smartphones).
•   Strategy: Competitive pricing, discounts, and promotions to
attract price-sensitive consumers.
5. Perfectly Elastic Demand (PED = ∞) – Pricing for Commodities
• Any price increase leads to zero demand (e.g., agricultural
products in competitive markets).
•   Strategy: Focus on cost efficiency and competitive pricing to
maintain market share.
Conclusion
By analyzing demand elasticity, I would adjust pricing based on
market conditions, customer behavior, and competitive dynamics.
The goal is to maximize revenue while maintaining customer
demand and profitability.

CO-1&2;   b.   "The Liberalization, Privatization, and Globalization (LPG) reforms
BL- Apply   introduced in 1991 significantly transformed the Indian economy but they
also led to certain socioeconomic challenges." In light of this statement
critically demonstrate the key features of LPG reforms and analyze their
necessity in the context of the Indian Economy. Additionally, assess the
impact of these reforms, highlighting both positive and negative
consequences.
[10]
Liberalization, Privatization, and Globalization (LPG) Reforms –
1991
The LPG reforms of 1991 marked a major shift in India’s economic
policy, transitioning from a closed, government-controlled
economy to a market-driven system. These reforms were
introduced due to the severe economic crisis caused by high fiscal
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
deficit, low foreign exchange reserves, and slow economic
growth.
Key Features of LPG Reforms
Liberalization – Reduced government restrictions on trade,
investment, and industries.
Privatization – Encouraged private sector participation by
disinvesting in state-owned enterprises.
Globalization – Opened the Indian market to foreign companies and
investments.
Necessity of LPG Reforms
• Economic Crisis of 1991 – India faced a balance of payments
crisis with only two weeks of foreign exchange reserves left.
• Low GDP Growth – The economy was growing at a sluggish rate
(~3.5% per year).
•   Inefficient Public Sector – State-owned enterprises were
suffering huge losses.
•   Lack of Foreign Investment – Strict regulations discouraged FDI
and private participation.
Impact of LPG Reforms
Positive Consequences
Higher Economic Growth – GDP growth accelerated, exceeding
7% post-2000.
Rise in Foreign Investments – India attracted significant FDI &
FII inflows.
Expansion of the Private Sector – Boosted efficiency and
innovation.
Global Integration – India became a key player in global trade and
IT outsourcing.
Negative Consequences
Widening Income Inequality – Benefits were not evenly
distributed, leading to a rich-poor divide.
Jobless Growth – Automation and capital-intensive industries
reduced employment opportunities.
Loss of Domestic Industries – Small businesses struggled to
compete with MNCs.
Overdependence on Foreign Capital – Increased vulnerability to
global economic shocks.
Conclusion
The LPG reforms were necessary and transformative, leading to
India’s emergence as a global economic power. However,
challenges such as inequality, unemployment, and industrial
displacement require policy adjustments to ensure inclusive and
sustainable growth.
Q4   [20]

Definition of Average Cost (AC) and Marginal Cost (MC)
Average Cost (AC): The cost per unit of output, calculated as:
AC=TCQAC = \frac{TC}{Q}
where TC is the total cost and Q is the quantity of output.
Marginal Cost (MC): The additional cost incurred when
producing one more unit of output, calculated as:
MC=TCn−TCn−1MC = TC_n - TC_{n-1}
where TC_n is the total cost at the current output level and TC_{n-
1} is the total cost at the previous output level.

Observations

rises as diseconomies set in.
MC first declines, reaches a minimum, then increases, reflecting
the Law of Variable Proportions.
The MC curve cuts the AC curve at its minimum point, a key
characteristic in cost analysis.
Would you like a graphical representation of these cost curves?
b.   What is production in the Theory of Production? Determine how
short-run production varies from long-run production.
Production in the Theory of Production
In economics, production refers to the process of transforming
inputs (land, labor, capital, and raw materials) into outputs
CO-1; BL-   (goods and services) to satisfy consumer needs. The Theory of
Production analyzes how firms use resources efficiently to
Understand &   [10]
maximize output while minimizing costs.
Analyze
Short-Run vs. Long-Run Production
The difference between short-run and long-run production lies in
how firms can adjust their input levels.
1. Short-Run Production (Fixed & Variable Inputs)
•   In the short run, at least one factor of production remains fixed
(usually capital or land), while others (like labor) are variable.
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
•   Firms cannot expand production capacity but can adjust labor
and raw materials.
• Governed by the Law of Diminishing Marginal Returns, where
adding more variable inputs to fixed inputs eventually leads to
declining additional output.
Example: A bakery hires more workers but cannot immediately
expand kitchen space.
2. Long-Run Production (All Inputs are Variable)
•   In the long run, all inputs are variable, meaning firms can expand
production capacity by investing in larger facilities, better
technology, or more skilled labor.
• Governed by Returns to Scale, where increasing inputs leads to
proportionate, increasing, or decreasing output growth.
•   Firms can achieve economies of scale, reducing per-unit costs.
Example: The bakery builds a bigger factory and buys advanced
ovens to increase production.
Conclusion
✔ Short-run production is constrained by fixed inputs, while
long-run production allows firms to adjust all inputs.
✔ Understanding both concepts helps firms make efficient
resource allocation and expansion decisions.
Q5
[20]
a.   Why “entry and exit” features are not similar in perfectly competitive
market structure and imperfect market structure. Explain with a suitable
example.
Difference in "Entry and Exit" Between Perfect and Imperfect
Markets
The ability of firms to enter and exit a market varies based on
market structure. In a perfectly competitive market, entry and exit
are free and unrestricted, while in imperfect markets, various
barriers make it difficult for firms to enter or leave.
CO- 1; BL- Analyze   [10]
1. Entry and Exit in a Perfectly Competitive Market
Free Entry and Exit: Firms can easily enter or exit the market
with no restrictions.
No Barriers to Entry: No licensing, patents, or capital
requirements restrict new firms.
Zero Economic Profit in the Long Run: If firms earn supernormal
profits, new firms enter, increasing supply and driving profits to
normal levels. Similarly, if firms incur losses, they exit, reducing
supply and restoring equilibrium.
Example: Agriculture Market – Farmers can start growing wheat
without restrictions and leave if they incur losses.
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
2. Entry and Exit in an Imperfect Market (Monopoly, Oligopoly,
Monopolistic Competition)
Restricted Entry and Exit: Firms face barriers that prevent free
market access.
Barriers to Entry:
•   Legal Barriers – Patents, licenses (e.g., pharmaceutical industry).
• High Capital Requirements – Heavy investment needed (e.g.,
automobile manufacturing).
• Brand Loyalty & Marketing Dominance – Established firms have
strong market control (e.g., Apple in smartphones).
•   Firms Can Earn Long-Run Profits: Due to entry restrictions,
existing firms retain market power and sustain supernormal
profits.
Example: Telecommunications Industry – A new telecom
company faces huge infrastructure costs and licensing
requirements, making entry difficult.
Conclusion
✔ Perfect competition allows free entry and exit, leading to
market efficiency and normal profits in the long run.
✔ Imperfect markets have entry barriers that protect existing
firms, leading to higher prices, lower competition, and sustained
profits.
✔ This distinction impacts pricing, consumer choice, and
innovation across industries.
b.   Examine Line and Staff organizational structure with suitable
examples.
Line and Staff Organizational Structure
The Line and Staff organizational structure combines direct
authority (line) with specialized support roles (staff) to improve
decision-making and efficiency in an organization.
1. Line Structure (Direct Authority)
CO-3;   •   Involves a clear chain of command, where authority flows from
BL- Remember &   top management to subordinates.   [10]
Apply   • Managers directly control their subordinates and make key
decisions.
•   Suitable for small businesses or military organizations where
quick decision-making is essential.
Example: In a manufacturing company, the production manager
directly oversees workers on the factory floor.
2. Staff Structure (Advisory Role)
•   Staff members support and advise line managers but do not have
direct control over employees.
********** 9 **********
--- page 10 ---
• These roles provide expertise in areas like HR, finance, IT, and
legal.
•   Staff specialists help improve efficiency, but conflicts may arise if
line managers ignore their advice.
Example: In a hospital, doctors (line authority) treat patients, while
HR and finance teams (staff roles) manage hiring and budgeting.
Advantages of Line and Staff Structure
✔ Efficiency – Line managers focus on execution, while staff
provides specialized knowledge.
✔ Expert Decision-Making – Staff specialists bring expertise in
technical fields.
✔ Flexibility – Organizations can expand operations while
maintaining structure.
Challenges
Conflict Between Line and Staff – Disagreements may arise over
decision-making authority.
Complexity – Coordination between different roles can be
challenging.
Conclusion
The Line and Staff structure balances authority and expertise,
making it ideal for large organizations that require specialized
functions and direct leadership.

Definition of Management
Management is the process of planning, organizing, leading, and
controlling resources efficiently and effectively to achieve
organizational goals. It involves coordinating human, financial, and
material resources to maximize productivity and ensure success.

Management is both a science and an art, as it combines systematic

b.   Explain how Planning helps in effective controlling.
How Planning Helps in Effective Controlling
Planning and controlling are interdependent functions of
management. Planning sets the objectives and the roadmap, while
controlling ensures that activities align with the plan. Effective
planning enhances the controlling process in the following ways:
1. Sets Clear Standards – Planning establishes performance
benchmarks, making it easier to measure actual performance
against expectations.
2. Provides Direction – A well-defined plan guides employees and
managers, reducing deviations.
3. Facilitates Performance Measurement – Controlling compares
actual performance with planned goals to identify gaps.
4. Minimizes Uncertainty – Planning anticipates future challenges,
CO-2&3; BL-   allowing for proactive control measures.
5. Ensures Coordination – Planning integrates various departments,   [10]
Remember and Apply
ensuring smooth workflow and controlled operations.
6. Enhances Decision-Making – Managers can take corrective
actions based on planned objectives.
7. Prevents Wastage of Resources – Planning helps in optimal
resource allocation, making control more effective.
8. Encourages Accountability – Employees are aware of their
responsibilities, making it easier to monitor and correct
deviations.
9. Improves Efficiency – Planning identifies potential risks and
control mechanisms, enhancing overall productivity.
10. Helps in Corrective Actions – If deviations occur, planning
provides alternative strategies for rectification.
Thus, planning lays the foundation for controlling by setting clear
objectives, enabling monitoring, and ensuring corrective actions to
achieve organizational success.

--- page 12 ---

Role of Quality Control in Ensuring Customer Satisfaction and
Organizational Success
Quality Control (QC) is a critical management function that
ensures products and services meet predefined quality standards. It
plays a vital role in customer satisfaction and overall organizational
success in the following ways:
1. Ensures Consistency – QC helps maintain uniform quality,
ensuring customers receive reliable products and services.
2. Enhances Customer Satisfaction – High-quality products meet
customer expectations, leading to greater trust and loyalty.
3. Reduces Defects and Errors – Systematic quality checks minimize
defects, reducing rework and wastage.
4. Boosts Brand Reputation – A company known for quality builds a
strong brand image and market credibility.
CO-3; BL-
5. Increases Efficiency – QC streamlines production, reducing delays

and operational inefficiencies.

regulations and standards.
7. Reduces Costs – Detecting defects early prevents expensive
recalls and customer complaints.
8. Encourages Continuous Improvement – Quality control fosters
innovation and refinement in processes.
9. Enhances Employee Productivity – Employees work with clear
quality guidelines, improving efficiency.
10. Gains Competitive Advantage – Superior quality differentiates a
company from competitors.
11. Minimizes Customer Complaints and Returns – High-quality
products reduce dissatisfaction and return rates.
12. Increases Profitability – Satisfied customers lead to repeat
business and higher revenue.
By ensuring product reliability and meeting customer expectations,

b.   How scientific management thought has brought refinement to
management. Illustrate the contribution of Fayol in the development of
management thought.
CO- 3; BL-
Understand and   Refinement of Management through Scientific Management   [10]
Apply   Thought
Scientific management, pioneered by Frederick W. Taylor,
introduced a systematic approach to improving efficiency,
productivity, and work processes. It brought refinement to
management in the following ways:
********** 12 **********
--- page 13 ---
1. Standardization of Work – Defined efficient methods to perform
tasks.
2. Time and Motion Studies – Analyzed tasks to minimize wasted
efforts.
3. Work Specialization – Assigned tasks based on worker expertise.
4. Scientific Selection of Workers – Chose employees based on skills
and training.
5. Incentive-Based Motivation – Linked wages to productivity to
encourage better performance.
6. Systematic Training – Provided structured training to enhance
skills.
7. Hierarchy and Supervision – Introduced better organizational
structures.
8. Improved Work Environment – Focused on worker-friendly
conditions to boost efficiency.
Contribution of Henri Fayol to Management Thought
Henri Fayol, known as the Father of Administrative Management,
developed principles to improve managerial effectiveness. His key
contributions include:
1. 14 Principles of Management – Provided guidelines for efficient
management, including division of work, unity of command, and
centralization.
2. Five Functions of Management – Introduced Planning,
Organizing, Commanding, Coordinating, and Controlling, forming
the foundation of modern management.
3. Administrative Focus – Emphasized the role of top management
in achieving organizational success.
4. Flexibility in Management – Stressed adaptability based on
organizational needs.
5. Holistic Approach – Considered both technical and human
aspects of management.
Taylor’s scientific management improved operational efficiency,
while Fayol’s administrative management shaped organizational
structure and leadership, together refining modern management
practices.

a.   Elaborate on the Production Possibility Curve and its role in determining
the economic growth of a nation.
Production Possibility Curve (PPC) and Its Role in Economic
Growth
The Production Possibility Curve (PPC) is a graphical
representation of the maximum combination of two goods or
services that an economy can produce with its available resources
and technology. It illustrates the concept of opportunity cost,
efficiency, and economic trade-offs.
Key Features of PPC:
1. Concave Shape – Due to the law of increasing opportunity cost.
2. Efficient Points – Any point on the curve represents maximum
resource utilization.
3. Inefficient Points – Points inside the curve indicate
underutilization of resources.
4. Unattainable Points – Points outside the curve are impossible to
achieve with current resources.
Role of PPC in Economic Growth:
CO-1
BL- Understand and   [10]
1. Measures Resource Allocation – Shows how efficiently resources
Apply   are used.
2. Indicates Opportunity Cost – Highlights trade-offs between
different goods.
3. Reflects Economic Efficiency – Movement toward the curve
signifies better resource utilization.
4. Demonstrates Economic Growth – A shift outward in the PPC
represents an increase in a nation’s productive capacity due to:
o Improved Technology – Enhancing production efficiency.
o   Capital Investment – More factories, infrastructure, and
machinery.
o   Skilled Workforce – Education and training improve labor
productivity.
o Discovery of New Resources – More raw materials lead to
higher output.
*Also draw a diagram which shows economic growth through PPC.
Conclusion
The PPC serves as a vital tool in economic analysis, illustrating
scarcity, trade-offs, and efficiency. A shift outward in the PPC
indicates economic growth, enabling a nation to produce more goods
and services, thereby improving the standard of living.
b.   Elucidate the uses of Elasticity of Demand with suitable
CO- 1; BL-   examples.
Understand and   [10]
Uses of Elasticity of Demand with Examples
Evaluate
Elasticity of demand measures how the quantity demanded of a
product responds to changes in price, income, or other factors. It
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
plays a crucial role in business, policymaking, and economic
planning.
Uses of Elasticity of Demand:
1. Pricing Decisions – Businesses set prices based on demand
elasticity.
o Example: If demand for luxury cars is elastic, a price
increase may reduce sales significantly.
2. Revenue Optimization – Helps firms decide whether to
increase or decrease prices.
o Example: If demand for essential goods like salt is
inelastic, raising prices may increase total revenue.
3. Taxation Policies – Governments impose higher taxes on
goods with inelastic demand to generate revenue.
o Example: Taxes on cigarettes and alcohol do not
drastically reduce consumption.
4. Substitute Availability Analysis – Products with elastic
demand face strong competition from substitutes.
o Example: If the price of Pepsi rises, consumers may switch
to Coca-Cola.
5. Income Distribution Effects – Determines the impact of
income changes on demand.
o Example: Luxury products like designer handbags have
high-income elasticity, meaning demand rises as income
increases.
6. Foreign Trade Policies – Helps governments decide
import/export strategies based on price sensitivity.
o Example: If a country exports inelastic goods (like
petroleum), it can benefit from price hikes.
7. Production and Supply Decisions – Businesses plan
production based on elasticity to avoid overproduction.
o Example: Agricultural products often have inelastic
demand, so excess production may lead to price drops.
Conclusion
Elasticity of demand is a powerful tool for businesses, governments,
and economists, guiding pricing, taxation, and trade policies.
Understanding demand sensitivity helps optimize revenue, improve
economic planning, and enhance consumer welfare.
Q3
[20]
a.   Enumerate the main characteristics of the Indifference Curve.
Analyze the process through which a consumer attains optimal
satisfaction using the Indifference Curve and Budget Line
CO-1&2; BL-   approach.
Understand &   [10]
Characteristics of the Indifference Curve and Consumer
Analyze
Equilibrium
An Indifference Curve (IC) represents different combinations of
two goods that provide the same level of satisfaction to a consumer.
It helps in understanding consumer preferences and choices.
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
Main Characteristics of the Indifference Curve:
1. Downward Sloping – As consumption of one good increases, the
other must decrease to maintain the same satisfaction level.
2. Convex to the Origin – Due to the diminishing marginal rate of
substitution (MRS), consumers are willing to give up less of one
good to gain more of another.
3. Higher IC Represents Higher Satisfaction – A curve further from
the origin signifies a greater level of utility.
4. Indifference Curves Never Intersect – Each curve represents a
unique level of satisfaction.
5. Not a Straight Line – Unlike perfect substitutes, ICs are usually
curved.
Consumer Equilibrium: Indifference Curve and Budget Line
Approach
A consumer attains optimal satisfaction where the budget
constraint and the highest possible indifference curve intersect.
*Also draw a diagram of Consumer Equilibrium to support the
answer.
Steps to Achieve Consumer Equilibrium:
1. Budget Line (BL) – Represents all affordable combinations of two
goods based on income and prices.
2. Point of Tangency – Consumer equilibrium occurs where the
budget line is tangent to the highest indifference curve.
3. Condition for Equilibrium:
o MRSxy = Px / Py (Marginal Rate of Substitution equals the
price ratio of goods).
4. Beyond Equilibrium:
o   If a consumer moves beyond the budget line, it becomes
unaffordable.
o Any point below the budget line is inefficient, as it does
not maximize satisfaction.
Conclusion
Using the Indifference Curve and Budget Line approach,
consumers make rational choices to maximize their satisfaction
while staying within their income constraints. This analysis helps
businesses and policymakers understand consumer behavior and
market demand.

--- page 5 ---

Utility is the satisfaction a consumer derives from consuming goods
and services. Classical economists used the Cardinal Utility
Theory, which assumes utility can be measured in absolute
numbers, while modern economists prefer the Ordinal Utility
Theory, which ranks preferences instead of assigning numerical
values.

Relationship Between Total Utility (TU) and Marginal Utility
(MU)
•   Total Utility (TU) is the total satisfaction obtained from
consuming a given quantity of a good.
•   Marginal Utility (MU) is the additional satisfaction gained from
consuming one more unit of the good.
• Law of Diminishing Marginal Utility: As consumption increases,
MU decreases, and when MU becomes zero, TU is maximized.
Diagram: TU and MU Relationship
TU Curve: Starts increasing, reaches a peak, then declines.
MU Curve: Starts high, decreases, becomes zero when TU is max,
and turns negative if consumption continues.

• The TU curve rises initially, then flattens at maximum
satisfaction, and declines if overconsumption occurs.
• The MU curve declines with each additional unit consumed,
eventually turning negative.
Thus, the Cardinal Utility Theory explains utility numerically,
while the Ordinal Utility Theory focuses on consumer preferences.
The TU and MU relationship helps in understanding consumer
decision-making regarding optimal consumption levels.
Q4   [20]
a.   What is an isocost line and an isoquant? How do they help a
producer in determining a least-cost output
Isocost Line and Isoquant: Tools for Least-Cost Output Decision
Producers aim to maximize output while minimizing costs. Isocost
lines and isoquants are essential tools in production analysis that
help firms achieve this objective.
1. Isocost Line
An isocost line represents all possible combinations of two inputs
(e.g., labor and capital) that a firm can purchase with a given
budget.
Equation of Isocost Line:
TC=(PL×L)+(PK×K)TC = (P_L \times L) + (P_K \times K)
Where:
• TC = Total cost
• P_L = Price of labor
•   L = Quantity of labor
CO- 1; BL- Understand   • P_K = Price of capital   [10]
& Apply   • K = Quantity of capital
Characteristics:
•   It is a straight line with a negative slope, indicating a trade-off
between labor and capital.
• A shift in the isocost line occurs when the budget changes.
2. Isoquant Curve
An isoquant represents all combinations of two inputs that produce
the same level of output. It is similar to an indifference curve in
consumer theory but used in production analysis.
Characteristics:
• Downward Sloping – More of one input requires less of the other
to maintain output.
• Convex to the Origin – Reflects diminishing marginal returns.
•   Higher Isoquants Represent Higher Output – More inputs
increase production.
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
3. Least-Cost Combination of Inputs
Producers aim to achieve cost minimization where the isoquant is
tangent to the isocost line. This point ensures:
• The Marginal Rate of Technical Substitution (MRTS) equals the
ratio of input prices: MRTS=MPLMPK=PLPKMRTS =
\frac{MP_L}{MP_K} = \frac{P_L}{P_K}
• Any other point would either increase costs or reduce output
efficiency.
*Also draw a diagram of the Least Cost Combination/Producer’s
Equilibrium.
Conclusion
By using isoquant and isocost analysis, firms determine the
optimal mix of labor and capital to minimize costs and maximize
output, leading to higher profitability and efficient resource
utilization.
b.   Explain different types of costs in detail. List all the
segregations’ of cost and explain them in detail
Different Types of Costs in Detail
Cost is the monetary value of resources used in the production of
goods and services. Businesses classify costs based on various
criteria to improve financial planning and decision-making.
1. Classification Based on Behavior:
1. Fixed Costs – Costs that remain constant regardless of production
level.
o Example: Rent, salaries, insurance.
2. Variable Costs – Costs that change with production volume.
o Example: Raw materials, wages, electricity.
3. Semi-Variable Costs – Costs with both fixed and variable
CO-1; BL-
components.
Understand &   o Example: Telephone bills (fixed rental + variable usage   [10]
Analyze   charge).
2. Classification Based on Production:
1. Total Cost (TC) – The sum of all costs incurred in production.
TC=FixedCost+VariableCostTC = Fixed Cost + Variable Cost
2. Average Cost (AC) – Cost per unit of output. AC=TCTotalOutputAC
= \frac{TC}{Total Output}
3. Marginal Cost (MC) – The additional cost incurred when
producing one more unit. MC=ΔTCΔOutputMC = \frac{\Delta
TC}{\Delta Output}
3. Classification Based on Traceability:
1. Direct Costs – Costs that can be traced to a specific product.
o Example: Raw materials, direct labor.
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
2. Indirect Costs – Costs that support production but cannot be
directly assigned.
o Example: Factory rent, administrative expenses.
4. Classification Based on Decision-Making:
1. Opportunity Cost – The cost of the next best alternative
foregone.
o Example: Choosing to produce shoes instead of bags.
2. Sunk Cost – Costs already incurred and cannot be recovered.
o Example: Research and development expenses.
5. Classification Based on Periodicity:
1. Short-Run Cost – Costs incurred in the short term with some fixed
inputs.
2. Long-Run Cost – Costs where all inputs become variable over
time.
Conclusion
Understanding different types of costs helps businesses optimize
pricing, budgeting, and profitability strategies, ensuring efficient
resource allocation and financial management.
Q5
[20]
a.   Explain the super-normal profit and loss-making situations of a
firm in a perfectly competitive market with a suitable diagram
and example.
Super-Normal Profit and Loss-Making Situations in a Perfectly
Competitive Market
In a perfectly competitive market, firms are price takers and can
earn different levels of profit based on cost and revenue conditions.
The two key situations are:
1. Super-Normal Profit (Abnormal Profit)
A firm earns super-normal profit when total revenue exceeds total
cost. This happens when the price (P) > Average Total Cost
(ATC).
CO- 1; BL- Analyze   Graph Explanation:   [10]
• The firm's equilibrium is at P = MR = MC (Profit-Maximizing
Condition).
•   Since P > ATC, the firm earns an economic profit, represented by
the shaded area in the graph.
Example:
A tech startup with an innovative product might temporarily earn
super-normal profits before competitors enter the market.
*Also draw a diagram.
2. Loss-Making Situation
A firm incurs losses when price (P) < ATC, meaning total revenue
is insufficient to cover total costs.
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
Graph Explanation:
• The firm's equilibrium is still at P = MR = MC, but ATC > P, leading
to losses.
•   If P ≥ AVC (Average Variable Cost), the firm continues production
to cover variable costs.
•   If P < AVC, the firm shuts down to avoid further losses.
Example:
A small farmer selling wheat at a price lower than production cost
experiences losses, especially if market prices decline due to
oversupply.
*Also draw a diagram.
Conclusion
In the long run, firms in perfect competition earn only normal
profit, as super-normal profits attract new entrants, increasing
supply and lowering prices. Conversely, loss-making firms exit the
market, reducing supply and raising prices until only normal profit
remains.
b. How Maslow’s theory of Motivation is different from
McClelland’s theory of Achievement? Explain with a suitable
example.
Difference Between Maslow’s and McClelland’s Theories of
Motivation
Maslow’s Hierarchy of Needs and McClelland’s Theory of

a. How do you support the view that Management must be defined
in terms of effectiveness, coordination of resources, and
objectives?
Management in Terms of Effectiveness, Coordination of
Resources, and Objectives
Management is the process of planning, organizing, leading, and
controlling resources to achieve organizational goals efficiently and
effectively. It must be defined in terms of effectiveness,
coordination of resources, and objectives because these elements
determine a firm’s success.
1. Effectiveness – Refers to achieving the desired goals within
a given time frame.
o A manager must ensure that employees work toward
achieving the company’s mission.
o Example: A software company must deliver a product on
CO-3;   schedule to meet customer expectations.
BL- Apply &   2. Coordination of Resources – Involves efficiently utilizing   [10]
Analyze   human, financial, and material resources.
o   Effective management ensures minimum waste and
maximum productivity.
o Example: A manufacturing firm must allocate raw
materials, labor, and technology efficiently to optimize
production.
3. Objectives Alignment – Every organization operates to
achieve specific short-term and long-term objectives.
o Managers align company activities with strategic goals.
o Example: A retail chain expanding internationally must
align operations with market demand and financial goals.
Conclusion
Management is not just about administering tasks but ensuring
effectiveness, optimizing resources, and aligning activities with
objectives. A well-managed organization achieves growth,
sustainability, and competitive advantage.
b.   Define Planning. What are the essentials of effective planning
and highlight the key factors leading to failure of the Plan.
Definition of Planning
Planning is the process of setting goals, determining actions, and
outlining strategies to achieve organizational objectives. It acts as a
CO-2&3; BL-   roadmap for decision-making and future activities.
[10]
Remember and Apply   Essentials of Effective Planning:
1. Clear Objectives – Goals should be well-defined and measurable.
2. Flexibility – Plans should adapt to changing circumstances.
3. Feasibility – Plans must be realistic and based on available
resources.
4. Accuracy – Based on factual data and logical assumptions.
********** 10 **********
--- page 11 ---
5. Time-Bound – Includes specific timelines for goal achievement.
6. Coordination – Aligns all departments and resources for unified
action.
Key Factors Leading to Plan Failure:
1. Lack of Clear Vision – Undefined objectives create confusion.
2. Rigid Structure – Inability to adapt to changes leads to
inefficiency.
3. Inaccurate Forecasting – Poor data analysis results in flawed
decisions.
4. Resource Constraints – Insufficient funds, manpower, or
technology hinder execution.
5. Resistance to Change – Employees and stakeholders may oppose
new plans.
6. Poor Communication – Lack of clarity in goals and expectations
causes misalignment.
Conclusion
Effective planning ensures goal achievement, resource
optimization, and organizational success, while failure in planning
results in inefficiency and missed opportunities.

Contributions of F.W. Taylor and Elton Mayo in Management
1. F.W. Taylor – Scientific Management Theory
Frederick Winslow Taylor, known as the father of scientific
management, introduced principles to enhance productivity through
systematic work processes.
Key Contributions:
1. Scientific Work Methods – Replaced traditional methods with
CO-3; BL-   time and motion studies.
Understand and   2. Standardization – Established rules and tools for efficiency.   [10]

4. Incentive-Based Pay – Linked wages to worker performance.
5. Four Principles of Management – Science, Harmony,

Example: Taylor’s principles helped in assembly-line production,

2. Elton Mayo – Human Relations Theory

Example: Modern HR practices focus on employee engagement,

Conclusion

efficiency, while Mayo’s human relations approach highlighted

modern management practices.
b.   Explain the concept of "Inventory Control" and discuss its
importance in the context of modern business operations. Also,
highlight any one technique used for effective inventory control.
Concept of Inventory Control
Inventory control refers to the process of managing stock levels to
ensure the right quantity of materials is available at the right time
while minimizing costs. It helps businesses avoid overstocking,
understocking, and wastage.
Importance of Inventory Control in Modern Business
Operations:
1. Cost Reduction – Reduces storage, handling, and wastage costs.
2. Efficient Production – Ensures smooth operations by maintaining
CO- 3; BL-   optimal stock levels.
Understand and   3. Customer Satisfaction – Prevents stockouts and delays, ensuring   [10]
Apply   timely deliveries.
4. Improved Cash Flow – Prevents excessive capital being tied up in
inventory.
5. Minimizing Losses – Reduces risks of spoilage, theft, and
obsolescence.
Technique for Effective Inventory Control – Economic Order
Quantity (EOQ):
The EOQ model determines the optimal order quantity that
minimizes total inventory costs, including ordering and holding
costs.
EOQ Formula:
EOQ=2DSHEOQ = \sqrt{\frac{2DS}{H}}
Where:
• D = Annual demand
•   S = Ordering cost per order
********** 12 **********
--- page 13 ---
• H = Holding cost per unit per year
Conclusion
Effective inventory control enhances cost efficiency, smooth
operations, and customer satisfaction. Techniques like EOQ help
businesses maintain an optimal balance between demand and supply.

a.   Elaborate on the Production Possibility Curve and its role in determining
the economic growth of a nation.
Production Possibility Curve (PPC) and Its Role in Economic
Growth
The Production Possibility Curve (PPC) is a graphical
representation of the maximum combination of two goods or
services that an economy can produce with its available resources
and technology. It illustrates the concept of opportunity cost,
efficiency, and economic trade-offs.
Key Features of PPC:
1. Concave Shape – Due to the law of increasing opportunity cost.
2. Efficient Points – Any point on the curve represents maximum
resource utilization.
3. Inefficient Points – Points inside the curve indicate
underutilization of resources.
4. Unattainable Points – Points outside the curve are impossible to
achieve with current resources.
Role of PPC in Economic Growth:
CO-1
BL- Understand and   [10]
1. Measures Resource Allocation – Shows how efficiently resources
Apply   are used.
2. Indicates Opportunity Cost – Highlights trade-offs between
different goods.
3. Reflects Economic Efficiency – Movement toward the curve
signifies better resource utilization.
4. Demonstrates Economic Growth – A shift outward in the PPC
represents an increase in a nation’s productive capacity due to:
o Improved Technology – Enhancing production efficiency.
o   Capital Investment – More factories, infrastructure, and
machinery.
o   Skilled Workforce – Education and training improve labor
productivity.
o Discovery of New Resources – More raw materials lead to
higher output.
*Also draw a diagram which shows economic growth through PPC.
Conclusion
The PPC serves as a vital tool in economic analysis, illustrating
scarcity, trade-offs, and efficiency. A shift outward in the PPC
indicates economic growth, enabling a nation to produce more goods
and services, thereby improving the standard of living.
b.   Elucidate the uses of Elasticity of Demand with suitable
CO- 1; BL-   examples.
Understand and   [10]
Uses of Elasticity of Demand with Examples
Evaluate
Elasticity of demand measures how the quantity demanded of a
product responds to changes in price, income, or other factors. It
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
plays a crucial role in business, policymaking, and economic
planning.
Uses of Elasticity of Demand:
1. Pricing Decisions – Businesses set prices based on demand
elasticity.
o Example: If demand for luxury cars is elastic, a price
increase may reduce sales significantly.
2. Revenue Optimization – Helps firms decide whether to
increase or decrease prices.
o Example: If demand for essential goods like salt is
inelastic, raising prices may increase total revenue.
3. Taxation Policies – Governments impose higher taxes on
goods with inelastic demand to generate revenue.
o Example: Taxes on cigarettes and alcohol do not
drastically reduce consumption.
4. Substitute Availability Analysis – Products with elastic
demand face strong competition from substitutes.
o Example: If the price of Pepsi rises, consumers may switch
to Coca-Cola.
5. Income Distribution Effects – Determines the impact of
income changes on demand.
o Example: Luxury products like designer handbags have
high-income elasticity, meaning demand rises as income
increases.
6. Foreign Trade Policies – Helps governments decide
import/export strategies based on price sensitivity.
o Example: If a country exports inelastic goods (like
petroleum), it can benefit from price hikes.
7. Production and Supply Decisions – Businesses plan
production based on elasticity to avoid overproduction.
o Example: Agricultural products often have inelastic
demand, so excess production may lead to price drops.
Conclusion
Elasticity of demand is a powerful tool for businesses, governments,
and economists, guiding pricing, taxation, and trade policies.
Understanding demand sensitivity helps optimize revenue, improve
economic planning, and enhance consumer welfare.
Q3
[20]
a.   Enumerate the main characteristics of the Indifference Curve.
Analyze the process through which a consumer attains optimal
satisfaction using the Indifference Curve and Budget Line
CO-1&2; BL-   approach.
Understand &   [10]
Characteristics of the Indifference Curve and Consumer
Analyze
Equilibrium
An Indifference Curve (IC) represents different combinations of
two goods that provide the same level of satisfaction to a consumer.
It helps in understanding consumer preferences and choices.
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
Main Characteristics of the Indifference Curve:
1. Downward Sloping – As consumption of one good increases, the
other must decrease to maintain the same satisfaction level.
2. Convex to the Origin – Due to the diminishing marginal rate of
substitution (MRS), consumers are willing to give up less of one
good to gain more of another.
3. Higher IC Represents Higher Satisfaction – A curve further from
the origin signifies a greater level of utility.
4. Indifference Curves Never Intersect – Each curve represents a
unique level of satisfaction.
5. Not a Straight Line – Unlike perfect substitutes, ICs are usually
curved.
Consumer Equilibrium: Indifference Curve and Budget Line
Approach
A consumer attains optimal satisfaction where the budget
constraint and the highest possible indifference curve intersect.
*Also draw a diagram of Consumer Equilibrium to support the
answer.
Steps to Achieve Consumer Equilibrium:
1. Budget Line (BL) – Represents all affordable combinations of two
goods based on income and prices.
2. Point of Tangency – Consumer equilibrium occurs where the
budget line is tangent to the highest indifference curve.
3. Condition for Equilibrium:
o MRSxy = Px / Py (Marginal Rate of Substitution equals the
price ratio of goods).
4. Beyond Equilibrium:
o   If a consumer moves beyond the budget line, it becomes
unaffordable.
o Any point below the budget line is inefficient, as it does
not maximize satisfaction.
Conclusion
Using the Indifference Curve and Budget Line approach,
consumers make rational choices to maximize their satisfaction
while staying within their income constraints. This analysis helps
businesses and policymakers understand consumer behavior and
market demand.

--- page 5 ---

perform a comparative study of Cardinal and Ordinal Utility
theories, assessing their assumptions. Also, Illustrate the
relationship between TU and MU using an appropriate diagram.
Comparative Study of Cardinal and Ordinal Utility Theories
Utility is the satisfaction a consumer derives from consuming goods
and services. Classical economists used the Cardinal Utility
Theory, which assumes utility can be measured in absolute
numbers, while modern economists prefer the Ordinal Utility
Theory, which ranks preferences instead of assigning numerical
values.
Comparison of Cardinal and Ordinal Utility Theories
Feature   Cardinal Utility Theory   Ordina
Ranks preferenc
